## Biological molecules: monomers, carbohydrates and lipids

All living things are built from the same few kinds of carbon-based molecule. This file covers how small units join into large molecules, the structure and jobs of carbohydrates and lipids, and the food tests used to find them.

### Overview

AS content for every board. Proteins are in file 02; nucleic acids, ATP, water and ions in file 03.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.1 to 3.1.3. Edexcel A 1.12 to 1.14 and 4.9 (starch and cellulose sit in the plants topic). Edexcel B 1.1 and 1.2. OCR A 2.1.2(b) to (j), (q), (r). OCR B 2.1.2(b) to (f). Eduqas CC1(c) to (g).
- OCR A also asks for the elements in each molecule group (2.1.2(c)), cholesterol (2.1.2(j)), and quantitative tests: colorimetry and biosensors (2.1.2(r)).
- OCR B and OCR A name cholesterol; Edexcel B names waterproofing and insulation as lipid roles.
- Eduqas CC1(g) and Edexcel A 1.15 link saturated fat to heart disease: see file 38.
- Edexcel A puts glycogen with starch in Topic 1 (diet) and cellulose in Topic 4 (plant fibres, file 41).

### 3.1.1 Monomers and polymers

*Refs: AQA 3.1.1 · Edexcel B 1.1 iii · OCR A 2.1.2(b), (c) · OCR B 2.1.2(d) · Eduqas CC1(c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The chemistry of life is the same in all organisms, which is indirect evidence for a common ancestor.
- A monomer is a small unit that can join with others to make a larger molecule. Monosaccharides, amino acids and nucleotides are monomers.
- A polymer is a large molecule made of many monomers joined together.
- A condensation reaction joins two molecules with a new chemical bond and releases a molecule of water.
- A hydrolysis reaction breaks a chemical bond between two molecules using a molecule of water.
- (OCR A) Carbohydrates contain C, H and O; lipids contain C, H and O; proteins contain C, H, O, N and S; nucleic acids contain C, H, O, N and P.

#### Explained

Think of condensation as "making, with water out" and hydrolysis as "splitting, with water in" (hydro = water, lysis = splitting). Digestion is hydrolysis. Building starch in a leaf or glycogen in the liver is condensation. Each needs its own enzyme.

Not every large molecule is a polymer. A triglyceride is large but it is made of one glycerol and three fatty acids, which are not repeating identical units, so it is not a polymer.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling lipids polymers.
- Saying hydrolysis "releases" water. It uses water.
- Saying condensation "removes water from the molecule" without saying a bond forms.

### 3.1.2 Carbohydrates

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2 · Edexcel A 1.12, 1.13, 4.9 · Edexcel B 1.1 i to iv · OCR A 2.1.2(d) to (g), (q), (r) · OCR B 2.1.2(b), (c), (e) · Eduqas CC1(c) to (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Monosaccharides are the monomers of carbohydrates. Glucose, galactose and fructose are common ones (all hexoses, C₆H₁₂O₆). Ribose and deoxyribose are pentoses.
- Two monosaccharides join by condensation to form a glycosidic bond, making a disaccharide:
  - maltose = glucose + glucose
  - sucrose = glucose + fructose
  - lactose = glucose + galactose
- Glucose has two isomers. In α-glucose the OH on carbon 1 is below the ring; in β-glucose it is above the ring.
- Polysaccharides are made by condensation of many glucose units:
  - starch and glycogen are made from α-glucose
  - cellulose is made from β-glucose.
- Tests: Benedict's test for reducing sugars; a non-reducing sugar test (hydrolyse with acid first); iodine in potassium iodide for starch.

#### Explained

**Glucose ring.** Six carbons; carbons 1 to 5 and an oxygen form the ring, carbon 6 sticks out. The only difference between α and β is which way the OH on carbon 1 points. This small difference changes everything about the polymers they make.

**Starch** is the energy store of plants. It is a mix of two polymers of α-glucose:

| | Amylose | Amylopectin |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bonds | 1,4-glycosidic only | 1,4 with 1,6 branches |
| Shape | Unbranched chain that coils into a helix | Branched |
| Why it helps | Compact: lots of glucose in a small space | Many ends where enzymes can hydrolyse glucose off quickly |

Starch is insoluble, so it does not lower the water potential of the cell and water does not move in by osmosis. It is large, so it cannot leave the cell.

**Glycogen** is the energy store of animals and fungi. It is like amylopectin but more branched, with shorter chains. More branches means more ends, so glucose can be released fast, which suits animals that move and have a high metabolic rate. It is also insoluble and compact.

**Cellulose** is the main part of plant cell walls. In a chain of β-glucose every other glucose is turned upside down. This gives straight, unbranched chains. Many parallel chains are held together by hydrogen bonds to form microfibrils, and microfibrils group into fibres. Each hydrogen bond is weak, but there are so many that cellulose is very strong. The strength lets the wall resist the pressure of water entering by osmosis, keeping the cell turgid.

**Benedict's test.** All monosaccharides and some disaccharides (maltose, lactose) are reducing sugars. Heat the sample with Benedict's reagent in a water bath. A reducing sugar gives a colour change from blue through green, yellow and orange to brick red, depending on how much sugar there is.

**Non-reducing sugar test.** Sucrose gives no change with Benedict's. If the first test is negative: boil a fresh sample with dilute hydrochloric acid (hydrolyses the glycosidic bond), cool, neutralise with sodium hydrogencarbonate (Benedict's does not work in acid), then do the Benedict's test again. A red-brown precipitate now shows a non-reducing sugar was present.

**Starch test.** Add iodine in potassium iodide solution. Orange-brown turns blue-black.

**Making the tests quantitative.** The colour of the Benedict's result is only a rough guide. Better methods:

- Filter and dry the precipitate, then weigh it.
- Use a colorimeter. Make a dilution series of glucose solutions of known concentration, test each, measure absorbance (or transmission) and plot a calibration curve. Read the unknown's concentration off the curve. (Required practical 11 in AQA uses this for urine-style glucose samples: file 20.)
- (OCR A) A biosensor uses an immobilised enzyme, such as glucose oxidase, and a transducer that turns the reaction into an electrical signal read as a concentration.

#### Worked example

A student makes glucose solutions of 0, 2, 4, 6, 8 and 10 mmol dm⁻³ from a 10 mmol dm⁻³ stock, each with a final volume of 10 cm³. What volumes of stock and water make the 4 mmol dm⁻³ solution?

1. Fraction needed = 4 ÷ 10 = 0.4.
2. Stock volume = 0.4 × 10 = 4 cm³.
3. Water = 10 − 4 = 6 cm³.

Answer: 4 cm³ of stock and 6 cm³ of distilled water.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying starch is made of β-glucose or cellulose of α-glucose.
- Saying cellulose is branched. It is unbranched and straight.
- Saying "cellulose forms hydrogen bonds with other glucose" instead of "between parallel chains".
- Forgetting to neutralise after acid hydrolysis in the non-reducing sugar test.
- Saying "Benedict's turns red" for a reducing sugar without mentioning heating.
- Saying glycogen is found in plants.

### 3.1.3 Lipids

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3 · Edexcel A 1.14 · Edexcel B 1.2 i to iv · OCR A 2.1.2(h) to (j), (q) · OCR B 2.1.2(f) · Eduqas CC1(f), (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Triglycerides and phospholipids are two groups of lipid.
- A triglyceride is made by condensation of one glycerol and three fatty acids. Each condensation forms an ester bond and releases a water molecule (three in total).
- A fatty acid is RCOOH. The R group (hydrocarbon chain) may be saturated (no C=C double bonds) or unsaturated (one or more C=C double bonds).
- In a phospholipid, one fatty acid is replaced by a phosphate-containing group.
- You should explain the different properties of triglycerides and phospholipids from their structures, and recognise saturated and unsaturated fatty acids in diagrams.
- The emulsion test for lipids.

#### Explained

**Triglycerides** are the main energy store in animals and many seeds.

| Property | Why | Use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| High ratio of C–H bonds to C atoms | Many bonds to break in respiration | Release about twice as much energy per gram as carbohydrate |
| Low mass for the energy stored | As above | Good store for animals that move |
| Insoluble in water | Fatty acid tails are non-polar | Do not affect water potential |
| High ratio of hydrogen to oxygen | Oxidation releases water | Metabolic water source (desert animals) |
| Poor conductor of heat (OCR, Edexcel B) | | Insulation under the skin; waterproofing in waxy cuticles |

**Saturated and unsaturated.** A double bond puts a kink in the chain, so unsaturated chains cannot pack tightly. That is why unsaturated fats (oils) are liquid at room temperature and saturated fats are more often solid.

**Phospholipids** have a hydrophilic "head" (the phosphate group, which is charged and attracts water) and two hydrophobic "tails" (the fatty acids, which repel water). In water they form a bilayer with heads facing the water on both sides and tails pointing inwards. This is the basis of every cell membrane (file 06). The hydrophobic core stops water-soluble substances passing freely.

**Cholesterol** (OCR A, OCR B) is a lipid with a four-ring structure. It sits between phospholipids in animal membranes and regulates fluidity. It is also used to make steroid hormones and vitamin D.

**Emulsion test.** Shake the sample with ethanol to dissolve any lipid, then pour into water and shake. A milky-white emulsion shows lipid: the lipid comes out of solution as tiny droplets that scatter light.

#### Worked example

How many water molecules are released when one triglyceride is made, and how many are needed to hydrolyse two triglycerides fully?

1. Three ester bonds form, each releasing one water: 3 water molecules.
2. Hydrolysing one triglyceride uses 3 water; two use 6.

Answer: 3 released; 6 needed.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling the bond in a triglyceride a glycosidic or peptide bond. It is an ester bond.
- Saying phospholipids have "a hydrophobic head".
- Saying unsaturated means "no double bonds". It is the other way round.
- Saying lipids store "more energy" without "per gram".
- In the emulsion test, adding water first. Dissolve in ethanol first.

### Check questions

1. Name the type of reaction that joins two α-glucose molecules and name the bond formed. (2 marks)
2. Explain how the structure of glycogen makes it suited to its role in animals. (3 marks)
3. Explain how cellulose molecules give strength to a plant cell wall. (3 marks)
4. A student suspects a drink contains sucrose but no reducing sugar. Describe how they would show this. (4 marks)
5. Compare the structure of a triglyceride with a phospholipid. (2 marks)
6. Explain why triglycerides are a better long-term energy store for a migrating bird than glycogen. (2 marks)
7. A student made a calibration curve for glucose with a colorimeter. Explain why the unknown samples must be tested with the same volume of Benedict's reagent and for the same time as the standards. (2 marks)
8. Explain why phospholipids form a bilayer in water. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Condensation (1); (1,4) glycosidic bond (1).
2. Any three: highly branched, so many ends for enzymes to hydrolyse, so glucose released quickly (1); insoluble, so no effect on water potential / no osmosis into cell (1); compact, so lots stored in small space (1); large, so cannot leave cell (1).
3. β-glucose gives straight, unbranched chains (1); chains lie parallel and are joined by many hydrogen bonds (1); forming microfibrils / fibres, giving strength (1).
4. Benedict's test on sample, heated, stays blue, so no reducing sugar (1); boil fresh sample with dilute hydrochloric acid (1); neutralise with sodium hydrogencarbonate (1); heat with Benedict's; red/orange/green precipitate shows non-reducing sugar (1).
5. Triglyceride has three fatty acids; phospholipid has two fatty acids and a phosphate-containing group (1); both have glycerol and ester bonds (1).
6. More energy released per gram (1); so lower mass to carry during flight (1). Accept: also releases more metabolic water.
7. Colour depends on volume of reagent and time / temperature as well as glucose concentration (1); so only glucose concentration varies, and the curve applies to the unknowns (1).
8. Heads are hydrophilic and face the water (1); tails are hydrophobic and point away from water, towards each other (1).

## Proteins and enzymes

Proteins do most of the work in cells. This file covers amino acids, how they join, the four levels of protein structure, and how enzymes work and what changes their rate. It includes AQA required practical 1.

### Overview

AS content. Enzyme inhibitors appear again in later topics (drugs at synapses in file 34; respiratory poisons in file 16).

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.4.1, 3.1.4.2, RP1. Edexcel A 2.9, 2.10, CP4, and 5.16 / CP12 (temperature and Q10, A-level). Edexcel B 1.3, 1.5, CP1. OCR A 2.1.2(k) to (o), (s), 2.1.4(a) to (f). OCR B 2.1.3(a) to (f). Eduqas CC1(h) to (j), CC4(a) to (h).
- OCR A names haemoglobin, insulin and pepsin (globular) and keratin, elastin and collagen (fibrous), and asks for cofactors, coenzymes, prosthetic groups and Q10. OCR B asks for enzymes and inhibitors used in medicine (2.1.3(f)).
- Edexcel B names collagen and haemoglobin, and asks for end-product inhibition.
- Eduqas names lysozyme as its induced-fit example.
- OCR A asks for thin layer chromatography of amino acids or sugars with Rf values (2.1.2(s)); AQA lists it only as a skills opportunity.

### 3.1.4.1 General properties of proteins

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.1 · Edexcel A 2.9 · Edexcel B 1.3 i to v · OCR A 2.1.2(k) to (o), (q), (s) · OCR B 2.1.3(a), (b) · Eduqas CC1(h) to (j). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Amino acids are the monomers of proteins. General structure: a central carbon joined to an amine group (–NH₂), a carboxyl group (–COOH), a hydrogen atom and an R group (side chain).
- The 20 amino acids common to all organisms differ only in their R groups.
- Two amino acids join by condensation to form a peptide bond, making a dipeptide. Many make a polypeptide. A functional protein may contain one or more polypeptides.
- Hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds and disulfide bridges hold proteins in shape.
- Primary, secondary, tertiary and quaternary structure, and how structure relates to function.
- The biuret test for proteins.

#### Explained

**Peptide bond.** The OH from the carboxyl group of one amino acid and an H from the amine group of the next form water. The C of one joins to the N of the other: –CO–NH–.

**Levels of structure.**

| Level | What it is | Bonds |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Primary | The sequence of amino acids in the polypeptide | Peptide bonds |
| Secondary | Local folding into α-helix or β-pleated sheet | Hydrogen bonds between the –NH and –C=O of the backbone |
| Tertiary | The polypeptide folds further into a precise 3D shape | Hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds, disulfide bridges between R groups (and hydrophobic interactions) |
| Quaternary | More than one polypeptide (sometimes with a prosthetic group) joined together | The same bonds as tertiary |

The primary structure decides everything else. Change one amino acid and the R groups that meet may differ, bonds form in different places, and the 3D shape changes. That is why a mutation can stop a protein working.

Disulfide bridges form between two cysteine R groups and are strong covalent bonds. Ionic and hydrogen bonds are weaker and broken by high temperature and extreme pH.

**Globular and fibrous proteins** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B, Eduqas):

| | Globular | Fibrous |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shape | Compact, roughly spherical | Long strands |
| Solubility | Soluble: hydrophilic R groups face outwards | Insoluble |
| Role | Metabolic: enzymes, hormones, transport | Structural |
| Examples | Haemoglobin (four polypeptides, each with an iron-containing haem prosthetic group, carries oxygen); insulin (hormone); enzymes | Collagen (three chains wound into a triple helix, cross-linked, high tensile strength in tendons, skin, bone); keratin (many disulfide bridges, in hair and nails); elastin (stretches and recoils in artery walls and alveoli) |

**Biuret test.** Add biuret reagent (sodium hydroxide then dilute copper(II) sulfate). Blue turns lilac/purple if peptide bonds are present.

**Chromatography** (OCR A). Spot the mixture on a thin layer plate, stand in solvent, let the solvent run, then locate spots (ninhydrin for amino acids). Rf = distance moved by spot ÷ distance moved by solvent. Compare Rf values with known standards run in the same conditions.

#### Worked example

An amino acid spot moves 3.2 cm; the solvent front moves 8.0 cm. Find Rf.

Rf = 3.2 ÷ 8.0 = 0.40. (Rf has no units and is always less than 1.)

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the primary structure is "the shape". It is the sequence.
- Saying secondary structure is held by bonds between R groups. It is hydrogen bonds in the backbone.
- Saying heat breaks peptide bonds. Denaturing breaks hydrogen and ionic bonds; the primary structure stays.
- Saying biuret detects amino acids. It detects peptide bonds, so free amino acids give no colour.

### 3.1.4.2 Many proteins are enzymes

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.2, RP1 · Edexcel A 2.10, 5.16, CP4, CP12 · Edexcel B 1.5 i to vii, CP1 · OCR A 2.1.4(a) to (f) · OCR B 2.1.3(c) to (f) · Eduqas CC4(a) to (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Each enzyme lowers the activation energy of the reaction it catalyses.
- The induced-fit model of enzyme action. Models have changed over time (lock and key came first).
- An enzyme's properties depend on the tertiary structure of its active site, which is complementary to its substrate(s), so it forms enzyme–substrate complexes. This explains specificity.
- The effects on rate of: enzyme concentration, substrate concentration, competitive and non-competitive inhibitors, pH and temperature.
- Enzymes catalyse intracellular and extracellular reactions that build structures and control functions from cells to whole organisms.
- pH = −log₁₀[H⁺].

#### Explained

**Activation energy** is the energy needed to start a reaction. When an enzyme–substrate complex forms, bonds in the substrate are strained (or, for joining reactions, substrates are held close together in the right orientation), so less energy is needed. More molecules have enough energy at body temperature, so the reaction is faster.

**Lock and key** saw the active site as a rigid shape that exactly fits the substrate. **Induced fit** says the active site is not exactly complementary at first. When the substrate binds, the active site changes shape slightly to mould around it. This puts strain on the substrate's bonds. Induced fit explains things lock and key cannot: why some enzymes bind more than one similar substrate, and how binding something elsewhere (an allosteric site) can change the active site. Eduqas uses lysozyme, which bends the bacterial cell-wall polysaccharide as it binds and so breaks a glycosidic bond.

**Factors affecting rate.**

| Factor | Effect | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Temperature (rising, below optimum) | Rate increases | Molecules have more kinetic energy, so more successful collisions and more E–S complexes form |
| Temperature (above optimum) | Rate falls fast | Vibrations break hydrogen and ionic bonds; tertiary structure and active site change shape; substrate no longer fits; enzyme is denatured (permanent) |
| pH away from optimum | Rate falls | H⁺ or OH⁻ ions affect the charges on R groups; ionic and hydrogen bonds break; active site changes shape |
| Substrate concentration | Rate rises then levels off | More collisions until all active sites are occupied at any moment; enzyme concentration then limits |
| Enzyme concentration | Rate rises, levels off if substrate becomes limiting | More active sites available |
| Competitive inhibitor | Rate lower, but the same maximum can be reached by adding more substrate | Similar shape to the substrate; binds the active site; substrate and inhibitor compete |
| Non-competitive inhibitor | Rate lower, maximum lower, adding substrate does not help | Binds elsewhere (allosteric site); changes the shape of the active site |

**Q10** (OCR A, Edexcel A). The temperature coefficient: Q10 = rate at (T + 10 °C) ÷ rate at T. For most enzymes below their optimum it is about 2: the rate doubles for every 10 °C rise.

**Cofactors** (OCR A). Some enzymes need a non-protein helper. Inorganic ion cofactors: Cl⁻ for amylase. Coenzymes are organic and often made from vitamins: NAD, FAD, coenzyme A. A prosthetic group is a cofactor permanently bound: Zn²⁺ in carbonic anhydrase.

**Inhibition in metabolism** (OCR A, Edexcel B). In end-product inhibition, the last product of a pathway inhibits the first enzyme non-competitively, so production slows when the product builds up. This is negative feedback. Some inhibitors are reversible (bind weakly); others bind permanently and are poisons, such as cyanide (inhibits cytochrome oxidase in respiration). Many medicines are inhibitors: some antivirals inhibit viral enzymes such as reverse transcriptase; ACE inhibitors lower blood pressure.

**Intracellular and extracellular.** Catalase works inside cells, breaking down hydrogen peroxide. Amylase and trypsin are made in cells and secreted to work outside them in the gut.

#### Worked example

1. A solution has [H⁺] = 3.2 × 10⁻⁵ mol dm⁻³. Find the pH.

pH = −log₁₀(3.2 × 10⁻⁵) = 4.49 (to 3 s.f., 4.49).

2. An enzyme releases 12 cm³ of oxygen per minute at 25 °C and 21 cm³ per minute at 35 °C. Calculate Q10.

Q10 = 21 ÷ 12 = 1.75.

3. Finding an initial rate from a curved graph: draw a tangent to the curve at time 0 and find its gradient (change in y ÷ change in x). If the tangent passes through (0, 0) and (40 s, 30 cm³), the initial rate = 30 ÷ 40 = 0.75 cm³ s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying enzymes are "killed" by heat. They are proteins, not alive: they are denatured.
- Saying the active site and substrate are "the same shape". They are complementary.
- Saying low temperature denatures enzymes. Low temperature just lowers kinetic energy.
- Saying a non-competitive inhibitor "blocks" the active site.
- Measuring the rate at the end of a reaction instead of the initial rate. As substrate is used up the rate falls, so compare initial rates.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 1** (also Edexcel A CP4 and CP12, Edexcel B CP1, OCR A PAG4): investigate the effect of a named variable on the rate of an enzyme-controlled reaction.

A typical method: catalase (from potato or yeast) breaking down hydrogen peroxide, collecting oxygen in an inverted measuring cylinder or gas syringe; or amylase digesting starch, sampling every 30 s into iodine on a spotting tile and timing how long until no blue-black colour.

- Independent variable: for example temperature (water baths at 20, 30, 40, 50, 60 °C) or pH (buffer solutions).
- Control variables: enzyme concentration and volume, substrate concentration and volume, pH (use a buffer) or temperature (water bath), time allowed to equilibrate before mixing.
- Control: boiled (denatured) enzyme to show any change is due to the enzyme.
- Repeat each condition at least three times and calculate a mean; identify anomalies.
- Rate = 1 ÷ time, or volume ÷ time; use a tangent at time 0 for the initial rate.
- Uncertainty: half the smallest division on the measuring instrument; percentage uncertainty = uncertainty ÷ reading × 100.
- Risks: hydrogen peroxide is an irritant (wear eye protection); hot water.

### Check questions

1. Draw the general structure of an amino acid and label the amine group, carboxyl group and R group. (2 marks)
2. Explain how a change in a single amino acid can stop an enzyme working. (4 marks)
3. Describe the induced-fit model of enzyme action. (3 marks)
4. Explain why the rate of an enzyme reaction stops increasing at high substrate concentrations. (2 marks)
5. A drug binds to the active site of an enzyme. Explain how increasing the substrate concentration would affect the inhibition. (3 marks)
6. Explain why collagen is suited to its role in tendons. (3 marks)
7. A student used amylase and starch. At 30 °C the starch was gone in 160 s; at 40 °C in 70 s. Calculate Q10, using rate = 1/time. (2 marks)
8. Give two variables a student should control when investigating the effect of pH on catalase, and say how. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Central carbon with H; NH₂ on one side and COOH on the other (1); R group labelled (1).
2. Change in primary structure (1); R groups differ, so bonds (hydrogen / ionic / disulfide) form in different places (1); tertiary structure / active site shape changes (1); substrate no longer complementary, so fewer / no E–S complexes (1).
3. Active site not exactly complementary at first (1); substrate binding causes active site to change shape (1); to mould around substrate, straining its bonds / lowering activation energy (1).
4. All active sites occupied / saturated (1); enzyme concentration becomes limiting (1).
5. Competitive inhibitor (1); more substrate means substrate more likely to collide with active site than inhibitor (1); so inhibition decreases / rate approaches maximum (1).
6. Three polypeptide chains wound into a triple helix (1); hydrogen bonds / cross-links between chains (1); high tensile strength / does not stretch (1). Accept: insoluble.
7. Rate at 40 °C = 1/70 = 0.0143; rate at 30 °C = 1/160 = 0.00625 (1); Q10 = 2.3 (1).
8. Any two with method (1 each): temperature, water bath; hydrogen peroxide concentration / volume, measure with syringe from same stock; catalase concentration / mass of potato, same mass / same size discs; time.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/03-nucleic-acids-atp-water-and-inorganic-ions.md -->

## Nucleic acids, DNA replication, ATP, water and inorganic ions

DNA and RNA carry genetic information, ATP carries energy, and water and ions make life's chemistry possible. This file covers their structures and properties and how DNA copies itself.

### Overview

AS content. How DNA codes for proteins (transcription and translation) is in file 12.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.5 to 3.1.8. Edexcel A 1.2, 2.5, 2.11, 5.6. Edexcel B 1.4 i, ii, 1.6, 1.7, 4.2 vi. OCR A 2.1.2(a), (p), 2.1.3(a) to (e). OCR B 2.1.2(a), 2.1.4(a) to (d). Eduqas CC1(a), (b), CC5(a) to (h), C1.1(a).
- Edexcel A, Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for the Meselson–Stahl experiment by name. AQA asks you to evaluate the work that validated the Watson–Crick model, which is the same experiment.
- OCR A asks for purines and pyrimidines and a practical: purifying DNA by precipitation (2.1.3(d)).
- OCR A lists more ions: Ca²⁺, Na⁺, K⁺, H⁺, NH₄⁺, NO₃⁻, HCO₃⁻, Cl⁻, PO₄³⁻, OH⁻. Eduqas lists Mg²⁺, Fe²⁺, Ca²⁺, PO₄³⁻. Edexcel B asks for plant ions (nitrate, calcium, magnesium, phosphate) and animal ions; plant mineral uses are expanded in file 41.

### 3.1.5.1 Structure of DNA and RNA

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.1 · Edexcel A 2.5 · Edexcel B 1.4 i · OCR A 2.1.3(a), (b), (d) · OCR B 2.1.4(a), (c) · Eduqas CC5(a), (e) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- DNA holds genetic information; RNA transfers it from DNA to the ribosomes. Ribosomes are made of RNA and protein.
- DNA and RNA are polymers of nucleotides. A nucleotide = a pentose sugar + a nitrogen-containing base + a phosphate group.
- DNA nucleotide: deoxyribose, phosphate, and adenine, cytosine, guanine or thymine.
- RNA nucleotide: ribose, phosphate, and adenine, cytosine, guanine or uracil.
- Nucleotides join by condensation, forming phosphodiester bonds.
- DNA is a double helix of two polynucleotide strands held by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. RNA is a relatively short single strand.
- DNA's simplicity made many scientists doubt it could carry the genetic code.

#### Explained

**The backbone.** The phosphate of one nucleotide bonds to the sugar of the next, giving a sugar–phosphate backbone with the bases sticking out.

**Pairing.** A pairs with T (two hydrogen bonds); C pairs with G (three hydrogen bonds). Adenine and guanine are purines (two rings); cytosine, thymine and uracil are pyrimidines (one ring). Each pair is one purine plus one pyrimidine, so the rungs are all the same width and the helix is regular.

**Antiparallel strands.** The two strands run in opposite directions (one 5′ to 3′, the other 3′ to 5′).

**Why DNA suits its job.**

- Very long, so it stores a lot of information.
- Two strands, each a template, so it can be copied accurately.
- Bases protected inside the helix by the backbone; many hydrogen bonds make it stable.
- Base sequence can vary without limit, so it can code for any protein.

**DNA compared with RNA** (Eduqas):

| | DNA | RNA |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sugar | Deoxyribose | Ribose |
| Bases | A, T, C, G | A, U, C, G |
| Strands | Two, double helix | One |
| Length | Very long | Short |

**Why scientists doubted DNA.** It has only four kinds of nucleotide, while proteins have 20 amino acids. Proteins seemed the more likely carrier of complex information until experiments in the 1940s and 1950s showed DNA was the genetic material.

**Purifying DNA** (OCR A practical). Grind tissue (onion or kiwi) with a solution of detergent and salt; detergent breaks membranes, salt helps the DNA clump. Add protease to break down histone proteins. Filter, then pour ice-cold ethanol gently down the side. DNA is insoluble in cold ethanol and precipitates as white strands at the boundary.

#### Worked example

In a DNA sample, 21% of bases are thymine. What percentage are guanine?

1. A pairs with T, so A = 21%. A + T = 42%.
2. C + G = 100 − 42 = 58%.
3. C = G, so G = 29%.

Answer: 29%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying DNA contains ribose or uracil.
- Saying the two strands are held by "phosphodiester bonds". Strands are held together by hydrogen bonds; phosphodiester bonds are within each strand.
- Using base pairing rules for RNA in a single strand. In single-stranded RNA there is no rule that A = U.

### 3.1.5.2 DNA replication

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.2 · Edexcel A 2.11 · Edexcel B 1.4 ii · OCR A 2.1.3(e) · OCR B 2.1.4(d) · Eduqas CC5(h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Semi-conservative replication keeps genetic information the same between generations of cells.
- Steps: DNA helicase unwinds the double helix and breaks the hydrogen bonds between bases; free DNA nucleotides are attracted to exposed complementary bases on both template strands; DNA polymerase joins adjacent nucleotides by condensation, forming phosphodiester bonds.
- Evaluate the work that confirmed the Watson–Crick model.

#### Explained

Semi-conservative means each new molecule has one original strand and one new strand.

DNA polymerase can only add nucleotides in one direction (to the 3′ end of the growing strand). Because the strands are antiparallel, one new strand is made continuously and the other in short fragments that are later joined by ligase. (Only OCR and Edexcel B mention this; it is useful for explaining why the active site of DNA polymerase is specific to one orientation.)

**Meselson and Stahl (1958).** Two models were possible: conservative (the original molecule stays whole; the copy is entirely new) and semi-conservative.

1. Bacteria were grown for many generations on a medium with heavy nitrogen (¹⁵N), so all their DNA was heavy.
2. They were moved to light nitrogen (¹⁴N) and allowed to divide once. DNA was extracted and centrifuged in a solution that forms a density gradient.
3. After one generation, there was one band at intermediate density. This ruled out conservative replication, which would give one heavy and one light band.
4. After two generations, there were two bands, intermediate and light, in equal amounts. This is exactly what semi-conservative replication predicts.

Why it convinced people: a clear prediction from each model, a single variable changed, a result that matched only one model, and others could repeat it.

#### Worked example

Bacteria with all-¹⁵N DNA are moved to ¹⁴N and divide three times. What fraction of DNA molecules are hybrid (¹⁵N/¹⁴N)?

1. One molecule becomes 2, then 4, then 8.
2. The two original heavy strands are always in separate molecules: 2 hybrid molecules.
3. Fraction = 2 ÷ 8 = 1/4. The other 3/4 are light.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying DNA polymerase breaks hydrogen bonds or pairs bases. Helicase separates strands; bases pair by themselves; polymerase forms phosphodiester bonds.
- Saying DNA polymerase forms hydrogen bonds.
- Saying "half the DNA is new" instead of "each molecule has one old and one new strand".

### 3.1.6 ATP

*Refs: AQA 3.1.6 · Edexcel A 5.6 · Edexcel B 4.2 vi, 5.4 iv · OCR A 2.1.3(c) · OCR B 2.1.4(b) · Eduqas CC5(b) to (d), C1.1(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- ATP (adenosine triphosphate) is a nucleotide derivative: ribose + adenine + three phosphate groups.
- ATP hydrolase catalyses ATP + water → ADP + Pi (inorganic phosphate), releasing energy.
- Hydrolysis of ATP can be coupled to energy-requiring reactions.
- The Pi released can phosphorylate other compounds, often making them more reactive.
- ATP is remade by condensation of ADP and Pi, catalysed by ATP synthase, during photosynthesis and respiration.

#### Explained

ATP is the cell's immediate energy source, not a long-term store. Why it suits this:

- Energy is released in small, manageable amounts, so little is wasted as heat.
- Hydrolysis is a single, fast reaction.
- ATP is soluble and moves easily within the cell.
- It is quickly remade.
- It can phosphorylate substances, such as glucose at the start of glycolysis, making them more reactive.

Uses: active transport (changing the shape of carrier proteins), muscle contraction, DNA and protein synthesis, nerve impulse recovery, secretion by exocytosis.

ATP cannot pass out of cells, so every cell makes its own. A person turns over roughly their own body mass in ATP each day, which shows it is constantly recycled.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "breaking the bond releases energy". Bond breaking needs energy; the hydrolysis reaction as a whole releases energy.
- Saying ATP is "a store of energy" like glycogen.
- Calling ATP synthase "ATP hydrolase" or the reverse.

### 3.1.7 Water

*Refs: AQA 3.1.7 · Edexcel A 1.2 · Edexcel B 1.7 i · OCR A 2.1.2(a) · OCR B 2.1.2(a) · Eduqas CC1(b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

Water is a major component of cells. It:

- is a metabolite in many reactions, including condensation and hydrolysis
- is an important solvent in which metabolic reactions occur
- has a relatively high specific heat capacity, buffering temperature changes
- has a relatively large latent heat of vaporisation, giving cooling with little water lost
- has strong cohesion between molecules, which supports columns of water in xylem and gives surface tension.

#### Explained

All of these come from water being polar. Oxygen pulls electrons more strongly than hydrogen, so O is slightly negative (δ−) and each H slightly positive (δ+). Hydrogen bonds form between the O of one molecule and the H of another.

| Property | Cause | Importance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Solvent | Polar molecules and ions dissolve as water clusters around them | Reactions happen in solution; transport in blood, xylem and phloem |
| High specific heat capacity | Hydrogen bonds absorb a lot of energy before molecules speed up | Stable temperature in cells and aquatic habitats |
| High latent heat of vaporisation | Many hydrogen bonds must break to evaporate water | Sweating and transpiration cool organisms well |
| Cohesion | Hydrogen bonds between water molecules | Continuous columns in xylem; surface tension lets small insects walk on ponds |
| Ice is less dense than liquid water (OCR, Edexcel B) | Hydrogen bonds hold molecules apart in a lattice | Ice floats and insulates water below |
| Metabolite | | Hydrolysis, photosynthesis (photolysis) |

#### Common mistakes

- Saying water molecules are held by "hydrogen bonds within the molecule". Hydrogen bonds are between molecules.
- Saying high specific heat capacity means water "heats up quickly". It is the opposite.

### 3.1.8 Inorganic ions

*Refs: AQA 3.1.8 · Edexcel B 1.6 i · OCR A 2.1.2(p) · Eduqas CC1(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Inorganic ions are in solution in the cytoplasm and body fluids, some in high and some in very low concentrations. Each has a specific role.
- Recognise their roles in: hydrogen ions and pH; iron ions in haemoglobin; sodium ions in co-transport of glucose and amino acids; phosphate ions in DNA and ATP.

#### Explained

| Ion | Role |
| --- | --- |
| H⁺ | Determines pH, which affects enzyme shape; proton gradients drive ATP synthesis; Bohr effect |
| Fe²⁺ | In haem, binds oxygen in haemoglobin |
| Na⁺ | Co-transport of glucose and amino acids into epithelial cells; nerve impulses |
| PO₄³⁻ | Part of nucleotides, DNA, RNA, ATP and phospholipids |
| Ca²⁺ (OCR A, Eduqas, Edexcel B) | Neurotransmitter release; muscle contraction; blood clotting; bone; calcium pectate in plant middle lamella |
| K⁺ (OCR A) | Nerve impulses; stomatal opening |
| Cl⁻ (OCR A) | Chloride shift; cofactor for amylase |
| HCO₃⁻ (OCR A) | Carries CO₂ in blood; buffers pH |
| NO₃⁻, NH₄⁺ (OCR A, Edexcel B) | Nitrogen source for amino acids and nucleotides in plants |
| Mg²⁺ (Eduqas, Edexcel B) | Part of chlorophyll |
| OH⁻ (OCR A) | Affects pH; involved in bonding in proteins |

#### Common mistakes

- Saying iron is in the globin chains. It is in the haem group.
- Writing "sodium" where "sodium ions" is needed; ions carry the charge that makes them work.

### Check questions

1. Give two differences between a DNA nucleotide and an RNA nucleotide. (2 marks)
2. A DNA molecule has 1800 bases in total, and 20% are cytosine. How many adenine bases are there? (2 marks)
3. Describe the roles of DNA helicase and DNA polymerase in replication. (3 marks)
4. Meselson and Stahl moved ¹⁵N bacteria to ¹⁴N medium. Explain why the result after one generation ruled out conservative replication but did not by itself prove semi-conservative replication. (3 marks)
5. Give three properties of ATP that make it suited to being the immediate energy source in cells. (3 marks)
6. Explain how sweating cools the body. (2 marks)
7. Explain how cohesion between water molecules helps transport in plants. (2 marks)
8. State the role of each ion: (a) iron ions in red blood cells; (b) phosphate ions in DNA. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any two (1 each): deoxyribose vs ribose; thymine vs uracil; (accept: DNA nucleotides form double-stranded polymer).
2. C = G = 20%, so A + T = 60%, A = 30% (1); 0.30 × 1800 = 540 (1).
3. Helicase unwinds the helix and breaks hydrogen bonds between bases (1); free nucleotides pair with complementary bases on each template strand (1); DNA polymerase joins adjacent nucleotides by condensation / phosphodiester bonds (1).
4. Conservative would give one heavy and one light band (1); a single intermediate band was found (1); but dispersive replication (mixed segments) would also give intermediate DNA, so the second generation was needed (1).
5. Any three: releases energy in small amounts; single hydrolysis step, so quick; soluble / easily moved; quickly resynthesised; can phosphorylate other molecules.
6. Water evaporates using latent heat from the skin (1); high latent heat of vaporisation, so a lot of heat is removed for little water lost (1).
7. Hydrogen bonds between water molecules (1); water pulled up as a continuous column when water evaporates from leaves (1).
8. (a) Part of haem / haemoglobin, binds oxygen (1). (b) Part of nucleotides / sugar-phosphate backbone (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/04-cell-structure-and-microscopy.md -->

## Cell structure, viruses and methods of studying cells

Cells are the basic units of life. This file covers the organelles of eukaryotic cells, prokaryotic cells and viruses, microscopes and magnification, and cell fractionation.

### Overview

AS content. Gram staining and culturing bacteria are in file 28; how viruses cause disease is in file 27; cell membranes in file 06.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.1.1 to 3.2.1.3. Edexcel A 3.1 to 3.5, 3.13, 4.7, 4.8, 6.5. Edexcel B 2.1 i to iii, v to vii, 2.2 i, CP2. OCR A 2.1.1(a) to (k), 2.1.6(h), (i). OCR B 2.1.1(a) to (d), (g) to (i), (o). Eduqas CC2(a) to (d), CC5(o).
- OCR A asks for the cytoskeleton (2.1.1(j)) and preparing and drawing slides (2.1.1(b) to (d)). Edexcel B CP2 and OCR PAG1 use stage and eyepiece micrometers.
- Edexcel A names extra plant organelles: amyloplasts, plasmodesmata, pits, middle lamella, tonoplast (4.7). These are included below.
- OCR B 2.1.1(a), (b), (e), (f) use blood smears, differential staining, haemocytometers and flow cytometry: file 49.
- Edexcel B 2.2 i classifies viruses by nucleic acid; this is included here, with the lytic cycle in file 27.

### 3.2.1.1 Structure of eukaryotic cells

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.1 · Edexcel A 3.2, 3.3, 3.5, 3.13, 4.7, 4.8 · Edexcel B 2.1 i, ii, v · OCR A 2.1.1(g) to (j), 2.1.6(h), (i) · OCR B 2.1.1(g), (o) · Eduqas CC2(a), (c), (d), CC5(o). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

The structure of eukaryotic cells, limited to the standard cells of plants, animals, fungi and protoctists, and the function of:

- cell-surface membrane
- nucleus (with chromosomes, nuclear envelope with pores, and nucleolus)
- mitochondria
- chloroplasts (in plants and algae)
- Golgi apparatus and Golgi vesicles
- lysosomes (a type of Golgi vesicle that releases lysozymes)
- ribosomes
- rough and smooth endoplasmic reticulum
- cell wall (cellulose in plants and algae; chitin in fungi)
- cell vacuole (in plants).

Also: in multicellular organisms, cells are specialised and organised into tissues, organs and organ systems. Recognise organelles in electron micrographs.

#### Explained

| Organelle | Structure | Function |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nucleus | Double membrane (nuclear envelope) with pores; chromatin (DNA with histones); one or more nucleoli | Holds the genetic information; controls the cell; nucleolus makes ribosomal RNA and assembles ribosomes; pores let mRNA out |
| Mitochondrion | Double membrane; inner membrane folded into cristae; matrix with enzymes, ribosomes and DNA | Aerobic respiration; makes ATP |
| Chloroplast | Double membrane; thylakoids stacked into grana; fluid stroma with enzymes, starch grains, ribosomes and DNA | Photosynthesis |
| Rough ER | Flattened membrane sacs (cisternae) covered with ribosomes | Makes and transports proteins |
| Smooth ER | Membrane sacs without ribosomes | Makes and processes lipids, including steroids |
| Golgi apparatus | Stack of flattened membrane sacs, with vesicles | Modifies proteins (adds carbohydrate to make glycoproteins), packages them into vesicles; makes lysosomes |
| Golgi vesicles | Small membrane sacs | Carry proteins to the cell-surface membrane for secretion |
| Lysosome | Vesicle containing lysozymes (hydrolytic enzymes) | Hydrolyse pathogens taken in by phagocytes, worn-out organelles, and dead cells |
| Ribosome | Two subunits of RNA and protein, no membrane; 80S in eukaryotic cytoplasm, 70S in prokaryotes, mitochondria and chloroplasts | Site of protein synthesis |
| Cell wall | Cellulose microfibrils (plants, algae); chitin (fungi) | Strength; stops the cell bursting when turgid |
| Vacuole | Large, surrounded by the tonoplast, filled with cell sap | Keeps cell turgid; stores pigments and some solutes |
| Centrioles (OCR A, Edexcel) | Two bundles of microtubules at right angles; not in most plant cells | Organise the spindle in cell division |
| Cytoskeleton (OCR A) | Network of microfilaments, microtubules and intermediate filaments | Support; moves organelles and vesicles; cell movement; forms cilia, flagella and the spindle |
| Cilia and flagella (OCR A) | Microtubules in a 9 + 2 arrangement | Move fluid past cells (cilia) or move the cell (flagella) |

Extra plant structures (Edexcel A):

- Amyloplasts store starch (abundant in potato tubers).
- Plasmodesmata are channels through cell walls that join the cytoplasm of neighbouring cells.
- Pits are thin regions of the cell wall where water can pass between cells.
- The middle lamella, made mostly of calcium pectate, sticks neighbouring plant cells together.

**Making and secreting a protein** (AQA, OCR A, Edexcel A). The gene is transcribed in the nucleus; mRNA leaves through a nuclear pore; ribosomes on the rough ER translate it; the polypeptide enters the ER and folds; it is carried in a vesicle to the Golgi apparatus; the Golgi modifies it (for example adds carbohydrate) and packages it; a vesicle carries it to the cell-surface membrane; the vesicle fuses with the membrane and releases the protein by exocytosis. ATP from mitochondria powers the transport. (Eduqas CC5(o): some polypeptides are joined or modified after translation, as in haemoglobin or insulin.)

**Specialised cells** (all boards). The organelles a cell has match its job. Cells in a pancreas that secrete enzymes have lots of rough ER and Golgi; sperm and muscle cells have many mitochondria; root hair cells have no chloroplasts. Examples OCR A names: erythrocytes (no nucleus, biconcave, full of haemoglobin), neutrophils (lobed nucleus, many lysosomes), squamous and ciliated epithelia, palisade cells, guard cells, root hair cells.

**Levels of organisation.** Cells → tissues (similar cells working together, such as squamous epithelium, xylem) → organs (several tissues for one or more functions, such as stomach, leaf) → organ systems (organs working together, such as the digestive system).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying mitochondria "make energy". They release energy from respiratory substrates and make ATP.
- Saying ribosomes are organelles with a membrane.
- Saying plant cells do not have mitochondria.
- Mixing up lysosomes (vesicles) and lysozymes (the enzymes inside).
- Describing protein secretion with the Golgi before the rough ER.

### 3.2.1.2 Structure of prokaryotic cells and of viruses

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.2 · Edexcel A 3.1, 3.4, 6.5 · Edexcel B 2.1 iii, 2.2 i · OCR A 2.1.1(k) · OCR B 2.1.1(h) · Eduqas CC2(b), (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Prokaryotic cells are much smaller than eukaryotic cells. They have cytoplasm without membrane-bound organelles; smaller ribosomes (70S); no nucleus, with a single circular DNA molecule not associated with proteins; and a cell wall containing murein (a glycoprotein, also called peptidoglycan).
- Many have one or more plasmids, a capsule around the cell and one or more flagella.
- Viruses are acellular and non-living. Viral particles have genetic material, a capsid and attachment proteins. (The material asked for at AQA AS is limited to these.)

#### Explained

| Feature | Prokaryote | Eukaryote |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Size | About 0.5 to 5 µm | About 10 to 100 µm |
| Nucleus | None; DNA free in cytoplasm | Present |
| DNA | Circular, not bound to histones; plasmids | Linear, in chromosomes bound to histones |
| Ribosomes | 70S | 80S in cytoplasm |
| Organelles with membranes | None | Many |
| Cell wall | Murein | Cellulose (plants), chitin (fungi), none (animals) |

Plasmids often carry genes for antibiotic resistance and can be passed between bacteria. The capsule (slime layer) protects against drying and helps bacteria avoid phagocytes.

**Viruses.** A virus particle has a nucleic acid core (DNA or RNA, never both) inside a protein coat called a capsid. Some have a lipid envelope taken from the host's membrane. Attachment proteins on the surface bind to receptors on host cells, which is why each virus infects only certain cell types. Viruses cannot reproduce outside a host cell. Edexcel B classifies viruses by nucleic acid and structure: DNA viruses (such as λ phage, a bacteriophage), RNA viruses (such as tobacco mosaic virus, Ebola) and retroviruses (such as HIV, which carry reverse transcriptase).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bacteria have "no DNA" or "no ribosomes".
- Saying viruses are prokaryotes. They are not cells at all.
- Saying the bacterial cell wall is made of cellulose.

### 3.2.1.3 Methods of studying cells

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.3 · Edexcel B 2.1 vi, vii, CP2 · OCR A 2.1.1(a) to (f) · OCR B 2.1.1(c), (d), (i) · Eduqas CC2(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The principles and limitations of optical microscopes, transmission electron microscopes (TEM) and scanning electron microscopes (SEM).
- Measuring the size of an object with an optical microscope using an eyepiece graticule calibrated with a stage micrometer.
- The difference between magnification and resolution.
- magnification = size of image ÷ size of real object.
- The principles of cell fractionation and ultracentrifugation to separate organelles.
- Appreciate that there was a considerable period when artefacts in electron micrographs were mistaken for cell organelles.

#### Explained

**Magnification** is how many times larger the image is than the object. **Resolution** is the minimum distance apart two objects can be and still be seen as separate. Magnifying beyond the resolution limit just makes a bigger blur.

| | Light (optical) | TEM | SEM |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Beam | Light | Electrons pass through | Electrons bounce off the surface |
| Resolution | About 200 nm | About 0.1 nm in practice | About 10 nm (lower than TEM) |
| Image | 2D, colour | 2D, black and white, internal detail | 3D surface, black and white |
| Specimen | Living or dead | Dead; vacuum; very thin sections; stained with heavy metals | Dead; vacuum; coated with metal |
| Limits | Cannot see small organelles | Complex preparation may create artefacts; cannot view living cells | Lower resolution than TEM; no internal detail |

Electrons have a much shorter wavelength than light, which is why electron microscopes resolve finer detail.

**Artefacts** are things that appear in a preparation but are not in the living cell, caused by fixing, staining or cutting. Scientists checked whether a structure was real by preparing specimens in different ways and seeing whether it always appeared.

**Graticule calibration.**

1. Line up the eyepiece graticule (a scale with arbitrary units) with the stage micrometer (a slide with a scale of known size, often 1 division = 10 µm).
2. Count how many graticule divisions match a known length on the micrometer.
3. One graticule division = that length ÷ number of divisions.
4. Recalibrate at each objective lens, because the graticule stays the same size but the specimen is magnified more.

**Slides and staining** (OCR A, Edexcel B, OCR B). Dry mounts (pollen, hair), wet mounts (onion epidermis in water, with a coverslip lowered at an angle to avoid bubbles), squash preparations (root tip) and sections. Stains increase contrast because most cell structures are transparent: iodine stains starch, methylene blue stains DNA, eosin stains cytoplasm. Differential staining shows different structures in different colours (as in Gram staining). Biological drawings: sharp pencil, clear continuous lines, no shading, label lines ruled and not crossing, magnification or scale stated.

**Cell fractionation.**

1. Homogenise the tissue (blender) to break cells open.
2. Keep the solution ice-cold (reduces enzyme activity that would break down organelles), isotonic (same water potential, so organelles do not gain or lose water and burst or shrivel) and buffered (keeps pH constant so proteins are not denatured).
3. Filter to remove large debris.
4. Ultracentrifugation: spin at a low speed; the heaviest organelles (nuclei) form a pellet. Remove the supernatant and spin it faster; the next pellet is mitochondria (and chloroplasts in plants); faster again gives ER, Golgi and lysosomes; fastest gives ribosomes.

#### Worked example

1. An image of a mitochondrion is 24 mm long. The magnification is ×8000. How long is the real mitochondrion in µm?

Real size = image ÷ magnification = 24 mm ÷ 8000 = 0.003 mm = 3 µm.

2. At ×400, 25 eyepiece graticule divisions line up with 0.1 mm on the stage micrometer. A cell is 18 graticule divisions long. How long is it?

One division = 0.1 mm ÷ 25 = 0.004 mm = 4 µm. Cell = 18 × 4 = 72 µm.

#### Common mistakes

- Defining resolution as "how clear the image is".
- Forgetting to convert units: 1 mm = 1000 µm; 1 µm = 1000 nm.
- Saying the solution must be cold "to stop the organelles moving".
- Saying the TEM gives a 3D image.

### Check questions

1. Describe how a protein made on the rough ER is secreted from the cell. (4 marks)
2. Give three structures found in a bacterial cell but not in an animal cell. (3 marks)
3. Explain why a TEM gives higher resolution than an optical microscope. (1 mark)
4. Give two limitations of a TEM. (2 marks)
5. Explain why tissue is homogenised in a cold, isotonic, buffered solution before cell fractionation. (3 marks)
6. A ribosome has a diameter of 25 nm. In a micrograph it measures 2 mm. Calculate the magnification. (2 marks)
7. Explain why cells in the pancreas that secrete enzymes contain many mitochondria and a large Golgi apparatus. (2 marks)
8. Describe the structure of a virus particle. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Polypeptide enters rough ER / folds (1); transported in vesicle to Golgi (1); Golgi modifies (e.g. adds carbohydrate) and packages into vesicles (1); vesicle fuses with cell-surface membrane; exocytosis (1).
2. Any three: cell wall (of murein); plasmid; capsule; flagellum; circular DNA free in cytoplasm; 70S ribosomes (accept "smaller ribosomes").
3. Electrons have a shorter wavelength than light (1).
4. Any two: cannot view living cells / vacuum; very thin section needed; artefacts from preparation; black and white image; expensive / large.
5. Cold: reduces enzyme activity that would digest organelles (1); isotonic: no osmosis, so organelles do not burst or shrivel (1); buffer: keeps pH constant so enzymes / proteins not denatured (1).
6. 2 mm = 2 000 000 nm (1); 2 000 000 ÷ 25 = ×80 000 (1).
7. Mitochondria release ATP for vesicle transport / exocytosis / protein synthesis (1); Golgi modifies and packages many enzymes into vesicles (1).
8. Nucleic acid (DNA or RNA) (1); capsid / protein coat (1); attachment proteins (1). Accept: lipid envelope in some.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/05-cell-cycle-mitosis-and-cancer.md -->

## The cell cycle, mitosis, binary fission and cancer

All cells arise from other cells. This file covers the eukaryotic cell cycle, the stages of mitosis, how prokaryotes and viruses replicate, uncontrolled division in cancer, and AQA required practical 2.

### Overview

AS content. Meiosis is in file 13. The genetics of cancer (oncogenes, tumour suppressor genes, methylation) is in file 24; cancer detection and treatment for OCR B in file 44.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.2, RP2. Edexcel A 3.10, CP5. Edexcel B 2.3 i to iii, CP3. OCR A 2.1.6(a) to (e). OCR B 3.1.1(a), (b). Eduqas C2.2(a) to (c).
- OCR A asks for cell-cycle checkpoints and how the cycle is regulated (2.1.6(b)); included below.
- AQA alone names binary fission and viral replication in this section. Viral replication for Edexcel B and OCR B is in file 27.

### 3.2.2 All cells arise from other cells

*Refs: AQA 3.2.2, RP2 · Edexcel A 3.10, CP5 · Edexcel B 2.3 i to iii, CP3 · OCR A 2.1.6(a) to (e) · OCR B 3.1.1(a), (b) · Eduqas C2.2(a) to (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- In multicellular organisms not all cells keep the ability to divide.
- Eukaryotic cells that can divide go through a cell cycle: interphase (DNA replication) followed by nuclear division (mitosis) and then division of the cytoplasm (cytokinesis).
- Mitosis produces two genetically identical daughter cells. Its stages: prophase, metaphase, anaphase, telophase.
- Mitosis is controlled by genes. Uncontrolled division can lead to tumours and cancers; many cancer treatments target the cell cycle.
- Binary fission in prokaryotes: replication of the circular DNA and plasmids, then division of the cytoplasm to give two daughter cells, each with one copy of the circular DNA and a variable number of plasmids.
- Viruses do not undergo cell division. After injecting their nucleic acid, the host cell's machinery replicates the virus.
- Recognise the stages of the cell cycle and explain the appearance of cells in each stage of mitosis.
- Calculate the mitotic index.

#### Explained

**Interphase** is most of the cycle. It has three parts:

- G1: the cell grows, makes organelles and proteins.
- S: DNA replicates. Each chromosome is now two identical sister chromatids joined at a centromere.
- G2: the cell grows more, makes proteins for division, and checks the copied DNA.

Some cells leave the cycle and enter G0, where they stop dividing (most neurones, for example).

**Mitosis.**

| Stage | What happens |
| --- | --- |
| Prophase | Chromosomes condense and become visible; the nuclear envelope breaks down; centrioles (in animal cells) move to opposite poles and spindle fibres form |
| Metaphase | Chromosomes line up on the equator of the cell; spindle fibres attach to each centromere |
| Anaphase | Centromeres divide; spindle fibres shorten and pull sister chromatids to opposite poles, centromere first (V shapes) |
| Telophase | Chromatids (now chromosomes) reach the poles and uncoil; a nuclear envelope forms around each set |

Cytokinesis then splits the cytoplasm: a cleavage furrow in animal cells; a cell plate forming a new wall in plant cells.

**Why mitosis matters.** It gives genetically identical cells for growth, repair of damaged tissue, replacing worn-out cells, and asexual reproduction (in yeast, many plants, and some animals).

**Regulation and checkpoints** (OCR A). Proteins check the cell before it moves on. The G1 checkpoint checks cell size, nutrients, growth factors and DNA damage. The G2 checkpoint checks that DNA has replicated fully and without damage. The metaphase (spindle assembly) checkpoint checks every chromosome is attached to the spindle before anaphase. If damage cannot be repaired, the cell may enter G0 or undergo apoptosis.

**Cancer.** Cancer starts when mutations in the genes that control the cell cycle let a cell divide without control. Its descendants form a mass called a tumour. Benign tumours stay in one place; malignant tumours invade and spread (metastasis). Many treatments target dividing cells:

- some drugs stop DNA replication in S phase
- some stop spindle formation, so cells stall in metaphase
- radiation damages DNA so cells fail the checkpoints.

Normal cells that divide often (hair follicles, gut lining, bone marrow) are also affected, which is why chemotherapy has side effects.

**Binary fission.** The circular DNA replicates; the two copies move to opposite ends; plasmids replicate; the cytoplasm divides and a new wall forms between the two cells. There is no spindle and no mitosis.

**Viral replication.** A virus's attachment proteins bind to receptors on a host cell; the nucleic acid enters; the host's ribosomes, enzymes and nucleotides make new viral nucleic acid and proteins; new particles assemble and are released, often bursting the cell (lysis).

**Mitotic index** = number of cells with visible chromosomes (in mitosis) ÷ total number of cells counted. A high index means rapid division, as in tumours or root tips.

#### Worked example

1. In a field of view of a root tip, 36 cells are in mitosis and 164 are in interphase. Calculate the mitotic index.

Total = 36 + 164 = 200. Mitotic index = 36 ÷ 200 = 0.18 (18%).

2. A cell cycle lasts 20 hours. In a sample of 400 cells, 30 are in metaphase. Estimate how long metaphase lasts.

Fraction in metaphase = 30 ÷ 400 = 0.075. Time = 0.075 × 20 h = 1.5 h = 90 minutes. This assumes cells are spread evenly through the cycle, so the fraction in a stage is proportional to its duration.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying DNA replicates during prophase. It replicates in S phase of interphase.
- Calling interphase a "resting" stage. The cell is very active.
- Saying chromosomes, rather than sister chromatids, separate in anaphase.
- Saying "cancer is caused by mitosis". It is caused by mutations that make mitosis uncontrolled.
- Saying viruses divide by binary fission.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 2** (also Edexcel A CP5, Edexcel B CP3, OCR A 2.1.6(d) and PAG1, OCR B 3.1.1(b)): preparation of stained squashes of cells from plant root tips; set-up and use of an optical microscope to identify the stages of mitosis; measurement of the mitotic index.

1. Cut the last 5 mm of a garlic or onion root tip (the meristem, where cells divide).
2. Put it in warm hydrochloric acid (about 1 mol dm⁻³ at 60 °C) for a few minutes. This breaks down the middle lamella so cells separate, and stops division.
3. Rinse in water, then place on a slide and add a stain that binds DNA, such as toluidine blue or acetic orcein.
4. Break up the tip with a mounted needle, add a coverslip, and press down firmly through filter paper (squash) to get a single layer of cells; do not twist the coverslip.
5. Find cells under low power, then switch to high power. Count cells in each stage and calculate the mitotic index.

Points examiners ask about: why the tip is used (only part where cells divide); why squash (single layer so light passes through and cells are seen separately); why stain (chromosomes otherwise transparent); safety (hydrochloric acid is corrosive: eye protection; stain is an irritant). To make counts reliable, count several fields of view chosen at random, and decide in advance how to count cells on the edge of the field.

### Check questions

1. Describe what happens to the chromosomes during prophase and metaphase. (3 marks)
2. Explain why the DNA content of a cell doubles during interphase. (2 marks)
3. A drug stops spindle fibres forming. Explain why it can be used to treat cancer and why it causes hair loss. (3 marks)
4. Describe binary fission. (3 marks)
5. Explain why viruses are not described as dividing. (2 marks)
6. In a root tip squash 45 of 300 cells were in mitosis. Calculate the mitotic index. (1 mark)
7. Explain why the root tip is heated in hydrochloric acid in a root tip squash. (2 marks)
8. A student counted cells in only one field of view. Suggest why their mitotic index might not represent the whole root tip. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Prophase: chromosomes condense / become visible (1); nuclear envelope breaks down / spindle forms (1). Metaphase: chromosomes line up on equator, attached to spindle by centromeres (1).
2. DNA replication (in S phase) (1); so that each daughter cell gets a full / identical set of genetic material (1).
3. Chromatids not separated, so mitosis stops / cells cannot divide (1); cancer cells divide rapidly, so most affected (1); hair follicle cells also divide rapidly (1).
4. Circular DNA replicates (1); plasmids replicate (1); cytoplasm divides into two cells, each with one copy of circular DNA and variable plasmids (1).
5. Viruses are not cells / have no cytoplasm or organelles (1); they are replicated by the host cell's machinery after injecting nucleic acid (1).
6. 45 ÷ 300 = 0.15 (1).
7. Hydrolyses / breaks down middle lamella so cells separate (1); stops cells dividing / allows stain to enter (1).
8. Division rate may differ in different parts of the tip / not representative (1); sample too small, so chance has larger effect; count several random fields (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/06-membranes-and-transport-across-them.md -->

## Cell membranes and transport across them

Every cell is wrapped in a membrane that controls what enters and leaves. This file covers the fluid mosaic model and the five ways substances cross membranes, with AQA required practicals 3 and 4.

### Overview

AS content. Co-transport in the ileum is applied in file 09; active transport in root hair cells in file 11; ion movement in neurones in file 19.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.3, RP3, RP4. Edexcel A 2.2 to 2.4, CP3. Edexcel B 4.2 i to v, CP5, CP6. OCR A 2.1.5(a) to (e). OCR B 2.1.1(j) to (n), 2.1.2(g), (h). Eduqas CC3(a) to (c).
- OCR A and OCR B ask for the roles of membranes inside cells (compartments, sites of reactions) and cell signalling with membrane receptors (OCR A 2.1.5(a), (b); cell signalling is expanded in file 32).
- Edexcel A asks how the fluid mosaic model was developed from earlier models (2.2 ii); included below.
- Edexcel B asks how a molecule's size, charge and solubility decide how it crosses (4.2 iii).
- OCR A asks for the effect of solvents and temperature on permeability (PAG8); Edexcel A CP3 uses alcohol or temperature; Edexcel B CP5 uses temperature.

### 3.2.3 Transport across cell membranes

*Refs: AQA 3.2.3, RP3, RP4 · Edexcel A 2.2, 2.3, 2.4, CP3 · Edexcel B 4.2 i to v, CP5, CP6 · OCR A 2.1.5(a) to (e) · OCR B 2.1.1(j) to (n), 2.1.2(g), (h) · Eduqas CC3(a) to (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The basic structure of all cell membranes, including cell-surface membranes and the membranes around organelles, is the same.
- The arrangement of phospholipids, proteins, glycoproteins and glycolipids in the fluid mosaic model. Cholesterol may also be present.
- Movement across membranes by simple diffusion (the limits set by the phospholipid bilayer), facilitated diffusion (channel and carrier proteins), osmosis (water potential), active transport (carrier proteins and ATP) and co-transport (sodium ions and glucose).
- How surface area, number of channel or carrier proteins and differences in gradients of concentration or water potential affect the rate of movement.
- How cells may be adapted for rapid transport across their internal or external membranes by more surface area or more channel and carrier proteins.
- Explain the adaptations of specialised cells for rapid transport; use the fluid mosaic model to explain the properties of membranes.

#### Explained

**The fluid mosaic model.**

- A phospholipid bilayer about 7 nm thick: hydrophilic heads outward, hydrophobic tails inward.
- Fluid: phospholipids and many proteins move about within their layer.
- Mosaic: proteins scattered through it like tiles. Intrinsic proteins span the bilayer (channels, carriers); extrinsic proteins sit on one surface (receptors, enzymes).
- Glycoproteins and glycolipids (with carbohydrate chains on the outside) act as receptors and antigens for cell recognition, and help cells stick together.
- Cholesterol sits between phospholipids. It binds to the tails, reducing their movement, which makes the membrane less fluid at high temperatures and stops it becoming too rigid at low temperatures. It also reduces leakage of water and ions.

**How the model was developed** (Edexcel A). In the 1930s the Davson–Danielli model proposed a lipid bilayer sandwiched between two continuous layers of protein. Electron micrographs showed a "railway track" appearance that seemed to fit. Later evidence did not: membranes from different cells had different protein amounts; freeze-fracture images showed proteins embedded within the bilayer; and labelled proteins on fused cells spread across the whole new membrane in minutes, showing they move. Singer and Nicolson proposed the fluid mosaic model in 1972 to fit this evidence. This is an example of a model being changed as new evidence appears.

**Why the bilayer is a barrier.** Small, non-polar molecules (oxygen, carbon dioxide, steroid hormones) dissolve in the hydrophobic core and diffuse straight through. Ions and large polar molecules (glucose, amino acids) cannot, because the hydrophobic tails repel them. They need proteins.

**The five mechanisms.**

| Mechanism | Needs ATP? | Proteins? | Direction |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Simple diffusion | No | No | Down the concentration gradient |
| Facilitated diffusion | No | Channel or carrier | Down the gradient |
| Osmosis | No | Often aquaporins | Water from higher to lower water potential |
| Active transport | Yes | Carrier proteins | Against the gradient |
| Co-transport | Indirectly | Co-transporter | One substance down its gradient drives another against its gradient |

*Channel proteins* form water-filled pores for specific ions; some are gated (open only in response to voltage or a chemical). *Carrier proteins* bind a specific molecule and change shape to carry it across.

**Water potential (ψ)** is the tendency of water to move out of a solution. Pure water has ψ = 0 kPa, the highest possible. Adding solutes lowers ψ, so solutions have negative values. Water moves by osmosis from a higher (less negative) to a lower (more negative) water potential across a partially permeable membrane.

- Animal cells in a solution of higher ψ gain water and may burst (haemolysis in red blood cells). In lower ψ they lose water and shrink (crenate).
- Plant cells in higher ψ gain water and become turgid, but the wall stops them bursting. In lower ψ they lose water; the protoplast pulls away from the wall (plasmolysis).

**Active transport.** A molecule binds to the carrier on one side; ATP is hydrolysed and the phosphate attaches to the carrier, changing its shape; the molecule is released on the other side. Example: the sodium–potassium pump moves 3 Na⁺ out and 2 K⁺ in per ATP.

**Co-transport in the ileum.** Sodium ions are actively pumped out of the epithelial cell into the blood, keeping the Na⁺ concentration inside the cell low. Na⁺ then diffuses into the cell from the gut lumen through a co-transporter protein, carrying glucose (or an amino acid) with it, even against glucose's own gradient. Glucose then leaves the other side of the cell by facilitated diffusion into the blood.

**Bulk transport** (OCR, Edexcel, Eduqas). Endocytosis takes in large particles or fluids by folding the membrane around them into a vesicle (phagocytosis for solids; pinocytosis for liquids). Exocytosis releases substances when a vesicle fuses with the membrane. Both need ATP.

**Factors affecting rate.**

- Simple diffusion: steeper gradient, larger surface area, thinner membrane, higher temperature → faster (Fick's law: rate ∝ surface area × difference in concentration ÷ thickness).
- Facilitated diffusion and active transport: also limited by the number of channel or carrier proteins. When all carriers are in use, raising the concentration has no further effect.
- Adaptations: microvilli on epithelial cells of the ileum and proximal tubule give a large surface area; these cells also have many carrier proteins and many mitochondria for active transport.

**Effect of temperature and solvents on permeability.** Above about 40 °C the phospholipids move more and membrane proteins denature, leaving gaps; the membrane becomes much more permeable. Ethanol and other organic solvents dissolve the lipids, also increasing permeability.

#### Worked example

1. Cell X has ψ = −400 kPa and cell Y has ψ = −650 kPa. Which way does water move?

From X to Y, because X has the higher (less negative) water potential.

2. Potato cylinders were put in sucrose solutions. Percentage change in mass: 0.0 mol dm⁻³: +14%; 0.2: +6%; 0.4: −3%; 0.6: −11%. Estimate the concentration with no change in mass and explain what it shows.

Plot % change against concentration and read where the line crosses zero: between 0.2 and 0.4, roughly 0.33 mol dm⁻³ (by linear interpolation: 0.2 + 0.2 × 6/9 = 0.33). At this concentration the solution has the same water potential as the potato cells. Its value can then be read from a table that converts sucrose concentration to water potential.

3. Percentage change: start mass 2.50 g, end mass 2.85 g. Change = 0.35 g; 0.35 ÷ 2.50 × 100 = +14%. Use percentage change because cylinders start at slightly different masses.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying water moves "from a high to a low concentration". Use water potential.
- Saying water potential "increases" when more solute is added. It decreases (becomes more negative).
- Saying plant cells burst in pure water.
- Saying active transport uses "energy from ATP" with no mention of carrier proteins changing shape.
- Saying facilitated diffusion needs ATP.
- Saying co-transport moves sodium against its gradient. Sodium moves down its gradient; glucose may move against its gradient.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 3** (also Edexcel B CP6, OCR A PAG8 / 2.1.5(e), OCR B 2.1.2(h)): produce a dilution series of a solute to find the water potential of a plant tissue.

1. Make a dilution series of sucrose (or salt) from a 1.0 mol dm⁻³ stock: for 10 cm³ of 0.4 mol dm⁻³ use 4 cm³ stock + 6 cm³ distilled water.
2. Cut equal cylinders of potato with a cork borer, blot dry, weigh each.
3. Put one cylinder in each concentration for the same time (for example 30 minutes or overnight) at the same temperature.
4. Remove, blot gently the same way, reweigh. Calculate percentage change in mass.
5. Plot percentage change against concentration; the x-intercept is the concentration with the same ψ as the cells.

Control variables: size and surface area of the cylinders, same potato (same variety), time, temperature, volume of solution, blotting method. Repeat at each concentration.

**AQA required practical 4** (also Edexcel A CP3, Edexcel B CP5, OCR A PAG8 / 2.1.5(c), OCR B 2.1.1(m)): investigate the effect of a named variable on the permeability of cell-surface membranes.

1. Cut equal pieces of beetroot and rinse them to remove pigment from damaged cells.
2. Put each piece in the same volume of water at a different temperature (or in different ethanol concentrations) for the same time.
3. Remove the beetroot. Measure the absorbance of the coloured liquid with a colorimeter (blue-green filter, zeroed with distilled water).
4. Higher absorbance means more betalain pigment has leaked out of the vacuoles, so the tonoplast and cell-surface membrane are more permeable.

Explanation of results: above a threshold temperature proteins denature and phospholipids move more, so pigment leaks out; ethanol dissolves lipids. Risks: ethanol is flammable; hot water; sharp knives.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the cell-surface membrane is described as "fluid mosaic". (2 marks)
2. Explain how cholesterol affects the properties of a membrane. (2 marks)
3. Glucose enters epithelial cells of the ileum against its concentration gradient. Describe how. (4 marks)
4. Explain why oxygen can diffuse across a membrane but sodium ions cannot without a protein. (2 marks)
5. Red blood cells were put in a solution with a water potential of −2000 kPa. Their own water potential is −700 kPa. Describe and explain what happens. (3 marks)
6. Explain why the rate of facilitated diffusion stops increasing at high concentrations. (2 marks)
7. In required practical 4, explain why the beetroot pieces are rinsed before the experiment. (2 marks)
8. Describe one piece of evidence that led scientists to reject the Davson–Danielli model. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Fluid: phospholipids / proteins can move (1); mosaic: proteins scattered through the bilayer (1).
2. Binds to phospholipid tails / restricts movement, reducing fluidity at high temperatures (1); reduces leakage of water / ions (1). Accept: keeps membrane fluid at low temperatures.
3. Sodium ions actively transported out of cell into blood (1); Na⁺ concentration in cell lower than in lumen (1); Na⁺ diffuses into cell through a co-transporter protein (1); carrying glucose with it (1).
4. Oxygen is small and non-polar, so dissolves in / passes through the hydrophobic core (1); sodium ions are charged so are repelled by hydrophobic tails (1).
5. Solution has lower water potential than cells (1); water leaves cells by osmosis (1); cells shrink / crenate (1).
6. All channel / carrier proteins in use / saturated (1); number of proteins becomes the limiting factor (1).
7. To remove pigment released from cells cut / damaged during preparation (1); so only pigment leaking due to the variable is measured (1).
8. Any one, with explanation (2): freeze-fracture showed proteins within the bilayer, not only in outer layers; fused-cell experiments showed proteins move, so not a fixed layer; membranes have different amounts of protein.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/07-immune-system.md -->

## Cell recognition and the immune system

The immune system tells "self" from "non-self" and destroys pathogens. This file covers antigens, phagocytes, T and B lymphocytes, antibodies, vaccination, monoclonal antibodies, and HIV.

### Overview

AQA AS content. Pathogens, disease transmission, plant defences and antibiotics are in file 27.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.4. Edexcel A 6.7 to 6.9, 6.12. Edexcel B 6.7 i to vii. OCR A 4.1.1(d) to (l). OCR B 3.2.2(a) to (e), (g), (h), 3.2.3(a), (b), (d), 4.2.1(h). Eduqas OA.3(a) to (j) (Option A only).
- OCR A asks for blood smears, opsonins, agglutinins, anti-toxins and autoimmune diseases (4.1.1(e), (i), (k)). OCR B asks for allergies (3.2.2(h)). OCR B and Edexcel A use the terms cell-mediated and humoral; AQA uses them too.
- AQA alone asks for ELISA and HIV structure in this section; Edexcel A, OCR B and Eduqas cover HIV under disease (file 27).
- Edexcel A names T helper, T killer and T memory cells, and B effector (plasma) and B memory cells.
- Eduqas asks about the ethics and effectiveness of vaccination programmes (OA.3(i), (j)); OCR B asks about vaccine ethics and herd immunity.

### 3.2.4 Cell recognition and the immune system

*Refs: AQA 3.2.4 · Edexcel A 6.7, 6.8, 6.9, 6.12 · Edexcel B 6.7 i to vii · OCR A 4.1.1(d) to (l) · OCR B 3.2.2(a) to (e), (g), (h), 3.2.3(a), (b), (d), 4.2.1(h) · Eduqas OA.3(a) to (j). Level: AS & A (AQA, OCR A, OCR B); A-level only (Edexcel A, Edexcel B, Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- Each type of cell has specific molecules on its surface that identify it, including proteins that let the immune system identify pathogens, cells from other organisms of the same species, abnormal body cells and toxins.
- Definition of antigen; antigenic variability.
- Phagocytosis of pathogens; the subsequent destruction of ingested pathogens by lysozymes.
- The response of T lymphocytes to a foreign antigen (the cellular response): the role of antigen-presenting cells; helper T cells (TH) stimulating cytotoxic T cells (TC), B cells and phagocytes.
- The response of B lymphocytes (the humoral response): clonal selection; plasma cells releasing monoclonal antibodies; memory cells.
- The structure of an antibody; the formation of an antigen–antibody complex leading to destruction of the antigen, limited to agglutination and phagocytosis of bacterial cells.
- Primary and secondary immune responses.
- Active and passive immunity; vaccination; herd immunity; ethics of vaccines.
- Monoclonal antibodies: targeting medication to specific cell types; medical diagnosis; ELISA; ethics of their use.
- The structure of HIV and its replication in helper T cells; how HIV causes AIDS; why antibiotics do not work on viruses.

#### Explained

**Antigens.** An antigen is a molecule, usually a protein or glycoprotein on a cell surface, that triggers an immune response. The immune system learns to ignore the body's own antigens (self) and responds to others (non-self). It responds to:

- pathogens (bacteria, viruses, fungi)
- cells from other individuals of the same species (transplant rejection)
- abnormal body cells (cancer cells, virus-infected cells displaying viral antigens)
- toxins.

**Non-specific defences first** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel A, Eduqas).
