Skin (keratin, a physical barrier with acidic oils); mucous membranes and cilia trapping and moving pathogens; stomach acid; lysozyme in tears; blood clotting sealing wounds; inflammation (histamine from mast cells widens vessels and makes them leaky, so more phagocytes and fluid reach the site); fever (higher temperature slows pathogen reproduction and speeds the immune response).

**Phagocytosis.**

1. Chemicals released by pathogens attract the phagocyte (chemotaxis).
2. Receptors on the phagocyte bind to the pathogen's antigens (helped when antibodies or opsonins coat the pathogen).
3. The phagocyte engulfs the pathogen, enclosing it in a vesicle called a phagosome.
4. Lysosomes fuse with the phagosome and release lysozymes, which hydrolyse the pathogen.
5. The phagocyte displays the pathogen's antigens on its surface. It is now an antigen-presenting cell.

Neutrophils are short-lived, lobed-nucleus phagocytes that arrive first. Macrophages are larger, live longer, and are the main antigen-presenting cells.

**The cellular (cell-mediated) response.** T lymphocytes have receptors that bind one specific antigen when it is presented on another cell.

1. A helper T cell with the complementary receptor binds to the antigen on an antigen-presenting cell.
2. The helper T cell is activated and divides by mitosis to make a clone.
3. Clones of helper T cells: release cytokines that stimulate cytotoxic T cells to divide; stimulate B cells to divide and make antibodies; stimulate phagocytes to engulf pathogens; some become memory T cells.
4. Cytotoxic (killer) T cells bind to infected body cells showing the antigen and release perforin, a protein that makes holes in the cell membrane, so the cell dies.

**The humoral response.** B lymphocytes each have antibody molecules on their surface that bind one specific antigen.

1. A B cell's antibody binds to its complementary antigen; the B cell takes the antigen in and presents it.
2. A helper T cell binds to the presented antigen and activates the B cell.
3. The B cell divides by mitosis (clonal selection and expansion).
4. Most daughter cells become plasma cells, which secrete large amounts of one antibody (monoclonal). Some become memory B cells.

Plasma cells live only a few days; memory cells last for decades.

**Antibody structure.** Four polypeptide chains (two heavy, two light) held by disulfide bridges in a Y shape. Each tip has a variable region whose shape is complementary to one antigen: the antigen-binding site. The rest is the constant region, the same for all antibodies of one class, which binds to receptors on phagocytes. A hinge region lets the arms move to bind two antigens at once.

**What antibodies do.**

- Agglutination: each antibody binds two pathogens, clumping them together so phagocytes find and engulf them more easily.
- Marking for phagocytosis: antibodies bound to a pathogen act as markers that phagocytes recognise. (OCR A: antibodies that do this are opsonins.)
- Neutralising toxins (antitoxins, OCR A) and blocking viral attachment proteins.

**Primary and secondary responses.**

| | Primary | Secondary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| When | First exposure | Later exposure to the same antigen |
| Speed | Slow: few B and T cells match the antigen; they must be selected and cloned | Fast: many memory cells divide quickly |
| Antibody level | Low | Much higher, made sooner |
| Symptoms | Usually | Usually none: pathogen destroyed before it causes disease |

**Antigenic variability.** Some pathogens (influenza virus, HIV) mutate often, changing the shape of their antigens. Memory cells from an earlier infection no longer match, so a new primary response is needed and people catch the disease again. This is why a new flu vaccine is needed each year.

**Active and passive immunity.**

| | Active | Passive |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How | Body makes its own antibodies after exposure to an antigen | Antibodies given from outside |
| Natural example | Catching a disease | Antibodies across the placenta; in breast milk |
| Artificial example | Vaccination | Injection of antibodies (antitetanus, antivenom) |
| Memory cells | Yes, long-lasting | No; antibodies broken down in weeks |
| Speed | Slow to develop | Immediate |

**Vaccination.** A vaccine contains antigens (dead or weakened pathogens, isolated antigens, or mRNA that makes cells produce the antigen). It causes a primary response, so memory cells form without the disease. On real infection, the secondary response is fast.

**Herd immunity.** When a large proportion of a population is immune, the pathogen cannot spread easily, so people who cannot be vaccinated (babies, people with weak immune systems) are protected too.

**Ethics of vaccines.** Testing on animals; testing on human volunteers who may suffer side effects; deciding who gets limited supplies; compulsory versus voluntary vaccination; the risk to the whole population when many people refuse (Edexcel B 6.7 vii, Eduqas OA.3(j)).

**Monoclonal antibodies** are many identical antibodies, all with the same binding site.

- *Targeted drugs*: a drug attached to an antibody that binds an antigen found only on cancer cells reaches those cells and harms fewer healthy cells. Some antibodies block a receptor on cancer cells directly.
- *Diagnosis*: pregnancy tests detect hCG (OCR B 4.2.1(h)). The test strip has mobile antibodies with coloured beads that bind hCG; the complex moves up the strip and is caught by fixed antibodies, forming a coloured line. A control line shows the test worked.
- *ELISA* (enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay) detects an antigen or antibody in a sample:
  1. The antigen (for example from HIV) is fixed to the bottom of a well.
  2. The patient's blood is added. If it contains antibodies to the antigen, they bind.
  3. The well is washed to remove unbound antibodies.
  4. A second antibody, with an enzyme attached, that binds to human antibodies is added; the well is washed again.
  5. A substrate is added. If the enzyme is present, it turns the substrate into a coloured product.
  Colour means the patient had the antibodies. Washing matters: unbound enzyme-linked antibody would give a colour even with no patient antibody (a false positive).
- *Ethics*: making monoclonal antibodies uses mice; trials of new monoclonal drugs carry risk to volunteers.

**HIV.** HIV has: an RNA genome (two strands) and the enzyme reverse transcriptase inside a protein capsid; a lipid envelope from the host cell; and attachment proteins (gp120) in the envelope.

Replication:

1. The attachment protein binds to the CD4 receptor on a helper T cell.
2. The capsid is released into the cell; it uncoats, releasing RNA and reverse transcriptase.
3. Reverse transcriptase makes a DNA copy of the viral RNA.
4. The DNA is inserted into the host cell's DNA.
5. The cell transcribes the viral DNA and makes viral proteins; new viruses assemble and bud off, taking some of the cell membrane as an envelope.

AIDS: HIV destroys helper T cells. With few helper T cells, B cells and cytotoxic T cells are not activated, so the body cannot fight other infections (opportunistic infections such as TB and pneumonia), and these cause death.

**Antibiotics** work by targeting bacterial structures and enzymes (murein cell walls, 70S ribosomes). Viruses have none of these and replicate inside host cells, so antibiotics do not affect them.

**Autoimmune disease** (OCR A). The immune system attacks the body's own antigens. Examples: type 1 diabetes (attacks β cells of the pancreas), rheumatoid arthritis (joints), lupus. Treated with immunosuppressant drugs.

**Allergies** (OCR B). An exaggerated immune response to a harmless antigen (allergen), such as pollen. B cells make IgE antibodies that attach to mast cells; on the next exposure the allergen binds, and mast cells release histamine, causing inflammation. Anaphylaxis is a severe, body-wide response treated with adrenaline.

#### Worked example

A vaccine is 90% effective. A population of 50 000 people has 80% vaccine uptake. How many people would you expect to be protected by the vaccine?

1. Vaccinated = 0.80 × 50 000 = 40 000.
2. Protected = 0.90 × 40 000 = 36 000.

Answer: 36 000, which is 72% of the population.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying antibodies "kill" pathogens. They bind and cause agglutination or mark them for phagocytes.
- Saying memory cells "remember the disease". They have receptors complementary to the antigen and divide rapidly on re-exposure.
- Saying the antigen and antibody are "the same shape".
- Saying vaccines contain antibodies (that is passive immunity).
- Saying HIV "is AIDS". HIV is the virus; AIDS is the condition that results.
- Saying antibiotics can kill viruses.
- Writing "T cells make antibodies". Plasma cells (from B cells) do.

### Check questions

1. Define the term antigen. (1 mark)
2. Describe how a phagocyte destroys a bacterium. (4 marks)
3. Describe the role of helper T cells in the immune response. (3 marks)
4. Explain why the secondary response is faster than the primary response. (3 marks)
5. Explain why people can catch influenza many times. (3 marks)
6. Describe how an ELISA test can show that a person has antibodies to HIV. Explain the purpose of washing the well. (5 marks)
7. Explain why HIV infection leads to AIDS. (3 marks)
8. A patient is bitten by a snake and given an antivenom. Explain why this does not give long-term immunity. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. A (foreign) molecule, usually a protein, that stimulates an immune response / antibody production (1).
2. Phagocyte attracted / receptors bind to antigens on bacterium (1); engulfs it into a phagosome / vesicle (1); lysosome fuses with phagosome (1); lysozymes hydrolyse the bacterium (1).
3. Receptor binds to antigen on antigen-presenting cell (1); stimulates cytotoxic T cells (1); stimulates B cells to divide / become plasma cells (1). Accept: stimulates phagocytes; forms memory cells.
4. Memory cells present (1); in larger numbers than original lymphocytes (1); divide rapidly into plasma cells, so antibodies produced faster / in greater amount (1).
5. Influenza virus mutates (1); antigen shape changes (1); memory cells / antibodies no longer complementary, so primary response needed again (1).
6. HIV antigen fixed to well (1); patient's blood / serum added; any HIV antibodies bind (1); wash (1); add enzyme-linked second antibody that binds the first, wash, add substrate (1); colour change shows antibodies present. Washing removes unbound antibodies so colour is only produced if patient antibodies bound (1).
7. HIV infects / destroys helper T cells (1); so B cells / cytotoxic T cells not activated (1); body cannot fight opportunistic infections (1).
8. Passive immunity / antibodies given, not made by body (1); no memory cells formed, and antibodies broken down (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/08-surface-area-and-gas-exchange.md -->

## Surface area to volume ratio and gas exchange

Large organisms cannot rely on diffusion across their body surface. This file covers surface area to volume ratio and how insects, fish, plant leaves and mammals exchange gases, with AQA required practical 5.

### Overview

AS content. Lung diseases (fibrosis, asthma, emphysema) appear here as AQA asks for them; their causes and treatment for OCR B are in file 44. Gas exchange in amoeba, flatworms and earthworms (Eduqas) is in file 46.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.1, 3.3.2, RP5. Edexcel A 2.1. Edexcel B 4.1, 4.3, CP7. OCR A 3.1.1(a) to (h). OCR B 2.2.3(a) to (c), (e), (f). Eduqas C3.1(a), (d) to (l).
- OCR A asks for tidal volume, vital capacity and spirometer traces (3.1.1(e)); OCR B for pulmonary ventilation (2.2.3(c)). Included below.
- OCR A, OCR A PAG2 and Edexcel B CP7 ask for dissection of an insect or fish gas exchange system; AQA RP5 allows animal or plant gas exchange or mass transport systems.
- Edexcel A asks for Fick's law and limits gas exchange to the lungs.
- Eduqas C3.1(j) to (l) asks for leaf structure and its role in photosynthesis.

### 3.3.1 Surface area to volume ratio

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1 · Edexcel B 4.1 i, ii · OCR A 3.1.1(a), (b) · Eduqas C3.1(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The relationship between the size of an organism or structure and its surface area to volume ratio.
- Changes to body shape and the development of systems in larger organisms as adaptations that make exchange easier as the ratio falls.
- The relationship between surface area to volume ratio and metabolic rate.
- Calculate surface area to volume ratios.

#### Explained

As an object gets bigger, its volume grows faster than its surface area. A 1 cm cube has SA 6 cm² and volume 1 cm³ (ratio 6:1); a 3 cm cube has SA 54 cm² and volume 27 cm³ (ratio 2:1).

Small organisms (single cells, flatworms) have a large ratio, so diffusion across the body surface supplies enough oxygen and removes enough waste. In large organisms:

- the surface is too small for the volume of cells needing supplies
- the distance from surface to the middle is too long for diffusion.

So large organisms have specialised exchange surfaces (lungs, gills, villi, leaves) and mass transport systems. Flattened or long thin shapes also increase the ratio (flatworms, leaves).

**Metabolic rate.** Small mammals lose heat fast because of their large surface area relative to volume. They have a high metabolic rate (per gram) to make heat, so need more oxygen per gram. Adaptations of small animals in cold places: compact shape, thick fur, hibernation. Large animals in hot places may have large ears (elephants) to lose heat.

**Features of efficient exchange surfaces** (OCR A, Edexcel A): large surface area; thin (short diffusion distance); steep concentration gradient kept up by ventilation and a good blood supply. **Fick's law** sums this up: rate of diffusion ∝ (surface area × difference in concentration) ÷ thickness of the surface.

#### Worked example

A cylindrical cell is 20 µm long and 4 µm in diameter. Calculate its surface area to volume ratio.

1. r = 2 µm.
2. SA = 2πr² + 2πrh = 2π(4) + 2π(2)(20) = 8π + 80π = 88π ≈ 276.5 µm².
3. V = πr²h = π(4)(20) = 80π ≈ 251.3 µm³.
4. Ratio = 88π ÷ 80π = 1.1 : 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "large organisms have a small surface area". They have a large surface area but a small surface area to volume ratio.
- Leaving out units or writing the ratio upside down.

### 3.3.2 Gas exchange

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2, RP5 · Edexcel A 2.1 · Edexcel B 4.3 i, ii, CP7 · OCR A 3.1.1(b) to (h) · OCR B 2.2.3(a) to (c), (e), (f) · Eduqas C3.1(d) to (l). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Adaptations of gas exchange surfaces: across the body surface of a single-celled organism; in the tracheal system of an insect (tracheae, tracheoles and spiracles); across the gills of fish (gill lamellae and filaments, including countercurrent flow); by the leaves of dicotyledonous plants (mesophyll and stomata).
- Structural and functional compromises between efficient gas exchange and limiting water loss, in terrestrial insects and xerophytic plants.
- The gross structure of the human gas exchange system: alveoli, bronchioles, bronchi, trachea and lungs.
- The essential features of the alveolar epithelium as a surface over which gas exchange takes place.
- Ventilation and the exchange of gases in the lungs: the mechanism of breathing, including the roles of the diaphragm and the antagonistic interaction between external and internal intercostal muscles in bringing about pressure changes in the thoracic cavity.
- Interpret information on how lung disease affects lung function; interpret data on risk factors and lung disease; evaluate how experimental data led to statutory restrictions on sources of risk factors; analyse and interpret data on pulmonary ventilation rate.

#### Explained

**Single-celled organisms.** Gases diffuse straight across the cell-surface membrane. The large surface area to volume ratio and short distance make this enough.

**Insects.**

- Air enters through spiracles (openings on the sides of the body that a valve can close).
- Tracheae (tubes stiffened by rings of chitin) branch into tracheoles, which reach right into the tissues; oxygen diffuses straight to the cells, with no blood involved.
- Movement of gases: diffusion down a concentration gradient (cells use oxygen); mass flow from ventilation movements of the abdomen; at the ends of the tracheoles, water is drawn into muscle cells during activity (by lowering the water potential with lactate), so air moves further down the tracheoles and gases diffuse faster.
- Limiting water loss: spiracles close when possible; a waxy, waterproof exoskeleton; hairs around spiracles trap moist air. The compromise: open spiracles let oxygen in but water out.

**Fish.**

- Water enters the mouth and passes over the gills, leaving under the operculum.
- Each gill has many gill filaments; each filament is covered in gill lamellae, giving a large surface area; lamellae have a thin epithelium and many capillaries.
- **Countercurrent flow:** blood flows through the lamellae in the opposite direction to the water. Blood always meets water with a higher oxygen concentration, so a diffusion gradient is maintained along the whole length of the lamella. About 80% of the oxygen in the water can be absorbed. In parallel flow (same direction) the concentrations would even out halfway, and only about 50% would be absorbed.

**Leaves of dicotyledonous plants.**

- Many stomata, mostly on the lower surface, so no cell is far from one.
- Air spaces in the spongy mesophyll, so gases diffuse quickly to and from cells; large internal surface area of mesophyll cells.
- Guard cells open the stomata in light (for CO₂ uptake) and close them when water is short. The compromise: open stomata let CO₂ in but water vapour out.
- (Eduqas) Leaf structure for photosynthesis: thin and broad (large surface for light, short CO₂ path); transparent cuticle and epidermis; palisade mesophyll packed with chloroplasts near the upper surface; veins of xylem bring water and phloem remove sugars. Stomata open when guard cells take up K⁺ ions actively, lowering their water potential; water enters by osmosis, the cells become turgid and bow apart because their inner walls are thicker.

**Xerophytes** (plants adapted to dry places, such as marram grass and cacti):

- thick waxy cuticle
- rolled leaves, sunken stomata and hairs that trap moist air, reducing the water potential gradient
- fewer stomata; small leaves or spines (smaller surface area)
- (more detail in file 11).

**The human gas exchange system.** Air passes down the trachea, which divides into two bronchi (one per lung), which branch into bronchioles, ending in alveoli. The trachea and bronchi have C-shaped cartilage rings to keep them open, ciliated epithelium and goblet cells that make mucus to trap particles; bronchioles have smooth muscle that can narrow them.

**The alveolar epithelium.**

- About 300 million alveoli: very large surface area.
- The alveolar wall is one layer of thin, flattened (squamous) cells, and the capillary wall is also one cell thick: diffusion path about 1 µm or less.
- A dense network of capillaries; blood flow carries oxygen away and brings carbon dioxide, maintaining the gradients.
- Ventilation replaces alveolar air, maintaining the gradients.
- Red blood cells are pressed flat against capillary walls, slowing flow and shortening the distance.
- Elastic fibres let alveoli stretch and recoil; surfactant stops them collapsing.

**Ventilation.**

| | Inspiration (inhalation) | Expiration (exhalation, at rest) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| External intercostal muscles | Contract: ribcage moves up and out | Relax |
| Internal intercostal muscles | Relax | Relax at rest; contract in forced expiration, pulling ribs down and in |
| Diaphragm | Contracts and flattens | Relaxes and domes up |
| Thoracic volume | Increases | Decreases |
| Pressure in lungs | Falls below atmospheric | Rises above atmospheric |
| Air | Moves in | Moves out |

Inspiration is active (needs muscle contraction). Normal expiration is mostly passive (elastic recoil of the lungs). The external and internal intercostals are antagonistic.

**Lung volumes** (OCR A, OCR B). A spirometer records a trace of volume against time.

- Tidal volume: volume of air breathed in or out in one normal breath (about 0.5 dm³).
- Vital capacity: the largest volume that can be breathed out after the deepest breath in.
- Residual volume: air left in the lungs after the most forceful exhalation; cannot be measured with a spirometer.
- Pulmonary ventilation rate (dm³ min⁻¹) = tidal volume (dm³) × breathing rate (breaths min⁻¹).
- Oxygen uptake: in a spirometer with soda lime absorbing CO₂, the trace falls over time; the gradient gives the rate of oxygen use.

**Lung disease** (data-handling skills). Diseases reduce gas exchange or ventilation:

- Pulmonary fibrosis: scar tissue thickens the alveolar walls (longer diffusion distance) and makes lungs less elastic (lower tidal volume and vital capacity).
- Asthma: airways inflamed; smooth muscle contracts and extra mucus is made, so airways narrow and less air flows (lower FEV₁, the volume breathed out forcefully in the first second).
- Emphysema: elastic fibres destroyed and alveolar walls break down; fewer, larger air spaces mean a much smaller surface area; lungs do not recoil, so air is trapped.

**Risk factors and evidence.** When interpreting data, separate correlation from cause. Large studies of smokers and non-smokers showed a strong correlation between smoking and lung cancer; animal experiments and identification of carcinogens in tar gave a mechanism. Together these led to laws restricting advertising and smoking in public places. Air pollution and occupational dusts (asbestos) led to similar laws.

#### Worked example

1. A person's tidal volume is 0.45 dm³ and their breathing rate is 16 breaths per minute. Calculate their pulmonary ventilation.

0.45 × 16 = 7.2 dm³ min⁻¹.

2. After exercise their pulmonary ventilation is 36 dm³ min⁻¹ and their breathing rate is 24 min⁻¹. Calculate the new tidal volume.

36 ÷ 24 = 1.5 dm³.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying insects carry oxygen in their blood.
- Saying countercurrent flow means "water and blood flow in opposite directions" without explaining the gradient along the whole lamella.
- Saying the lungs inflate "because air rushes in". Volume increases first, so pressure falls, and then air moves in.
- Saying the internal intercostals contract during normal breathing out.
- Saying the alveolar wall is "one cell thick so it's thin" without linking it to a short diffusion distance.
- Saying stomata are open "to let oxygen in" during photosynthesis.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 5** (also Edexcel B CP7, OCR A 3.1.1(g) and PAG2): dissection of animal or plant gas exchange or mass transport system or of an organ within such a system. (Heart dissection is described in file 10.)

*Fish gills.* Cut away the operculum with scissors to expose the gills; cut out a gill arch; put it in water and look with a hand lens or under a microscope at the filaments and lamellae, which float apart in water and stick together in air.

*Insect tracheal system* (locust or cockroach). Cut along the dorsal surface and pin the body open under water or saline in a dissecting dish; tracheae appear silvery because of the air inside. A section mounted on a slide shows the chitin rings.

*Lungs.* Identify trachea, bronchi and cartilage; inflate through the trachea using a tube connected to a pump (never by mouth).

Safety and ethics: sharp tools cut away from the body; wear gloves; wash hands; dispose of tissue properly; use material from animals killed for food where possible. Record findings as labelled drawings.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a large multicellular organism needs a specialised gas exchange surface. (2 marks)
2. Explain how countercurrent flow makes gas exchange in fish gills efficient. (3 marks)
3. Describe how air moves into the lungs. (4 marks)
4. Explain two ways the alveolar epithelium is adapted for rapid gas exchange. (4 marks)
5. Explain the compromise between gas exchange and water loss in a terrestrial insect. (3 marks)
6. A person has pulmonary fibrosis. Explain why they feel breathless during exercise. (3 marks)
7. A tidal volume of 0.5 dm³ and a breathing rate of 15 min⁻¹ became 1.2 dm³ and 30 min⁻¹ during exercise. Calculate the percentage increase in pulmonary ventilation. (2 marks)
8. A cube-shaped cell has sides of 2 µm. Calculate its surface area to volume ratio. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Small surface area to volume ratio (1); diffusion distance too long / body surface too small to supply all cells (1).
2. Blood and water flow in opposite directions (1); blood always meets water with higher oxygen concentration (1); so a diffusion gradient is maintained along the whole lamella / more oxygen absorbed (1).
3. External intercostal muscles contract, ribs move up and out (1); diaphragm contracts and flattens (1); thoracic volume increases (1); pressure falls below atmospheric so air moves in (1).
4. Any two features with explanation (2 each): thin / one cell thick, short diffusion distance; many alveoli, large surface area; capillary network / blood flow maintains gradient; ventilation maintains gradient.
5. Spiracles open for oxygen to diffuse in (1); water vapour diffuses out at the same time (1); spiracles close / waxy cuticle / hairs reduce water loss but also limit oxygen supply (1).
6. Thicker alveolar walls / scar tissue, so longer diffusion distance (1); less elastic, so smaller tidal volume / less air ventilated (1); less oxygen diffuses into blood for respiration in muscles (1).
7. Before 7.5 dm³ min⁻¹; after 36 dm³ min⁻¹ (1); increase = 28.5 ÷ 7.5 × 100 = 380% (1).
8. SA = 6 × 4 = 24 µm²; V = 8 µm³ (1); ratio 3 : 1 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/10-mass-transport-in-animals.md -->

## Mass transport in animals: haemoglobin, circulation, heart and tissue fluid

Mass transport moves substances in bulk around large organisms. This file covers haemoglobin and oxygen dissociation, the mammalian circulatory system, the heart and cardiac cycle, blood vessels and tissue fluid.

### Overview

AS content for most boards. How the heartbeat is started and controlled (SAN, AVN, ECGs) is in file 18. Cardiovascular disease is in file 38. Open circulations and other animals' blood pigments are in file 46. Blood cells, blood pressure measurement and first aid are in file 49.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.4.1, RP5. Edexcel A 1.1, 1.3, 1.4, 7.8. Edexcel B 4.4 i to iii, v, vii, 4.5, 4.6. OCR A 3.1.2(a) to (f), (i), (j). OCR B 2.2.1(a) to (c), 2.2.2(a) to (d), 4.1.2(g) to (i). Eduqas C3.2(b) to (h).
- OCR A and Eduqas ask for carbon dioxide transport and the chloride shift (OCR A 3.1.2(i); Eduqas C3.2(f)); included below.
- Edexcel B asks for myoglobin (4.5 iii) and fetal haemoglobin; OCR A for fetal haemoglobin. Included below.
- OCR A and Edexcel B ask for the lymphatic system.
- AQA asks about cardiovascular disease risk data here; that is in file 38 with Edexcel A Topic 1.
- AQA alone uses "required practical 5" for heart dissection; OCR A 3.1.2(e) and PAG2 also require it.

### 3.3.4.1 Mass transport in animals

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4.1, RP5 · Edexcel A 1.1, 1.3, 1.4, 7.8 · Edexcel B 4.4 i to iii, v, vii, 4.5 i to iv, 4.6 i, ii · OCR A 3.1.2(a) to (f), (i), (j) · OCR B 2.2.1(a) to (c), 2.2.2(a) to (d), 4.1.2(g) to (i) · Eduqas C3.2(b) to (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The haemoglobins are a group of chemically similar molecules found in many different organisms. Haemoglobin is a protein with a quaternary structure.
- The role of haemoglobin and red blood cells in transporting oxygen; loading, transport and unloading of oxygen; the oxygen dissociation curve; the cooperative nature of oxygen binding; the effects of carbon dioxide concentration (Bohr effect).
- Many animals are adapted to their environment by having haemoglobin with different affinities for oxygen.
- The general pattern of blood circulation in a mammal, limited to: the coronary arteries; the blood vessels entering and leaving the heart, lungs and kidneys.
- The gross structure of the human heart; pressure and volume changes and associated valve movements during the cardiac cycle.
- The structure of arteries, arterioles and veins in relation to their function; the structure of capillaries and the importance of capillary beds as exchange surfaces.
- The formation of tissue fluid and its return to the circulatory system.
- Analyse and interpret data on the cardiac cycle; calculate cardiac output = stroke volume × heart rate.

#### Explained

**Haemoglobin.** Four polypeptide chains (two α and two β), each with a haem group containing Fe²⁺. Each haem binds one O₂ molecule, so one haemoglobin carries up to four O₂. Oxygen plus haemoglobin forms oxyhaemoglobin. Red blood cells have no nucleus and few organelles, leaving more room for haemoglobin, and a biconcave shape for a large surface area.

- *Loading* (association): in the lungs, where the partial pressure of oxygen (pO₂) is high, haemoglobin has a high affinity and becomes nearly saturated.
- *Unloading* (dissociation): in respiring tissues, where pO₂ is low, haemoglobin releases oxygen.

**The oxygen dissociation curve** plots percentage saturation of haemoglobin against pO₂. It is S-shaped (sigmoid):

1. At low pO₂ the first O₂ molecule binds with difficulty, because the haem groups are buried inside the molecule.
2. Binding of the first O₂ changes the shape (quaternary structure) of the haemoglobin, uncovering the other haem groups. The second and third bind much more easily. This is **cooperative binding**, and explains the steep middle part.
3. Near saturation it gets harder for the fourth O₂ to find a site, so the curve flattens.

The steep middle matters: a small fall in pO₂ in tissues releases a lot of oxygen.

**The Bohr effect.** At high CO₂ concentration (respiring tissues), haemoglobin has a lower affinity for oxygen. CO₂ dissolves to form carbonic acid; the H⁺ ions lower pH and change haemoglobin's shape. The curve shifts to the right. So more oxygen is unloaded where it is most needed. In the lungs, CO₂ is low, the curve is to the left, and loading is easy.

**Haemoglobins of different affinities.**

| Animal | Curve | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lugworm, llama, animals at altitude | Left of human | Live where pO₂ is low; high affinity lets haemoglobin load enough oxygen |
| Small mammals (mouse) | Right of human | High metabolic rate; lower affinity unloads oxygen more readily |
| Fetus (OCR A, Edexcel B) | Left of adult | Fetal haemoglobin must take oxygen from the mother's haemoglobin across the placenta, where pO₂ is low |
| Myoglobin (Edexcel B) | Far left, hyperbolic (one chain) | Stores oxygen in muscle; releases it only when pO₂ is very low, during hard exercise |

**Carbon dioxide transport** (OCR A, Eduqas, Edexcel B 4.5). About 5% dissolves in plasma; about 10% binds to haemoglobin as carbaminohaemoglobin; about 85% travels as hydrogencarbonate ions:

1. CO₂ diffuses into red blood cells.
2. Carbonic anhydrase catalyses CO₂ + H₂O → H₂CO₃ (carbonic acid).
3. H₂CO₃ dissociates into H⁺ and HCO₃⁻.
4. HCO₃⁻ diffuses out into the plasma. Chloride ions (Cl⁻) move in to balance the charge: the **chloride shift**.
5. H⁺ binds to haemoglobin, forming haemoglobinic acid. This buffers the blood pH and makes haemoglobin release its oxygen (the Bohr effect).

In the lungs the process reverses and CO₂ diffuses out.

**The circulation.** Mammals have a closed, double circulation: blood passes through the heart twice per circuit, once to the lungs (pulmonary) and once to the body (systemic). The heart raises blood pressure again after the lungs, so blood reaches the body quickly at high pressure, while the low pressure in the lungs avoids damage to capillaries there.

Vessels to know:

| Organ | In | Out |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Heart muscle | Coronary arteries (from the aorta) | Coronary veins |
| Lungs | Pulmonary artery (deoxygenated, from right ventricle) | Pulmonary vein (oxygenated, to left atrium) |
| Kidneys | Renal artery | Renal vein |
| Rest of body | Aorta (from left ventricle) | Vena cava (to right atrium) |

**The heart.** Four chambers. The right side pumps deoxygenated blood to the lungs; the left side pumps oxygenated blood to the body. The left ventricle wall is much thicker, because it must pump blood all round the body at high pressure. The atria have thin walls; they only pump blood into the ventricles.

- Atrioventricular valves (tricuspid on the right, bicuspid or mitral on the left) between atria and ventricles, held by tendinous cords so they do not turn inside out.
- Semilunar valves at the base of the aorta and pulmonary artery.
- Valves open when pressure behind them is higher than in front, and close when pressure in front is higher; they stop backflow.
- A septum separates the two sides so oxygenated and deoxygenated blood do not mix.

**The cardiac cycle** (about 0.8 s at rest).

| Stage | What happens | Valves |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Atrial systole | Atria contract; pressure in atria rises above ventricles; blood pushed into ventricles | AV open; semilunar closed |
| Ventricular systole | Ventricles contract from the bottom up; ventricular pressure rises above atrial pressure | AV close (first heart sound "lub"); when ventricular pressure exceeds pressure in the arteries, semilunar valves open and blood is ejected |
| Diastole | Ventricles and atria relax; ventricular pressure falls below arterial pressure | Semilunar close (second sound "dub"); when ventricular pressure falls below atrial pressure, AV valves open and blood flows passively into the ventricles |

On a pressure graph: the AV valves close where ventricular pressure crosses above atrial pressure; the semilunar valves open where ventricular pressure crosses above aortic pressure; they close where it falls below; AV valves open where ventricular pressure falls below atrial pressure.

**Cardiac output** = stroke volume × heart rate. Stroke volume is the volume pumped by one ventricle per beat.

**Blood vessels.**

| Vessel | Structure | Function link |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Artery | Thick muscle layer; thick elastic layer; thick wall; small lumen; folded endothelium; no valves except at the base of the aorta and pulmonary artery | Elastic tissue stretches during systole and recoils during diastole, smoothing flow and keeping pressure high; thick wall resists bursting |
| Arteriole | Thicker muscle layer relative to elastic layer | Muscle contracts (vasoconstriction) or relaxes to control blood flow into capillary beds |
| Capillary | Wall one cell thick (endothelium); narrow lumen; many branches; gaps between cells | Short diffusion distance; large surface area; slow flow; tissue fluid forms through the gaps |
| Vein | Thin muscle and elastic layers; large lumen; valves | Low pressure, so little muscle or elastic tissue needed; valves stop backflow; contraction of surrounding skeletal muscle squeezes blood towards the heart |

**Tissue fluid** surrounds cells and is how substances pass between blood and cells.

1. At the arteriole end of a capillary, hydrostatic pressure (from the heart) is higher than the opposing forces, so water and small molecules (glucose, amino acids, ions, oxygen) are forced out through the gaps: ultrafiltration. Large plasma proteins and blood cells stay in.
2. Proteins in the blood give the plasma a lower water potential (an oncotic pressure).
3. At the venule end, hydrostatic pressure has fallen (fluid has left, and friction lowers it), so water moves back into the capillary by osmosis, carrying CO₂ and urea.
4. Not all fluid returns. The excess drains into lymph capillaries and is carried in lymph vessels, which have valves, back to the blood via ducts that join veins near the heart. Lymph is moved by contraction of nearby muscles.

If blood pressure is high, more fluid is forced out and less returns, so fluid builds up in tissues (oedema). A diet very low in protein also causes oedema, because the plasma water potential is higher.

#### Worked example

1. A person's heart rate is 72 beats min⁻¹ and stroke volume is 70 cm³. Calculate cardiac output in dm³ min⁻¹.

72 × 70 = 5040 cm³ min⁻¹ = 5.04 dm³ min⁻¹.

2. On a cardiac cycle graph, one cycle lasts 0.75 s. Calculate the heart rate.

60 ÷ 0.75 = 80 beats min⁻¹.

3. At pO₂ = 12 kPa haemoglobin is 98% saturated; at 3 kPa it is 40% saturated. Each 100 cm³ of fully saturated blood carries 20 cm³ of oxygen. How much oxygen does 100 cm³ of blood unload?

(98 − 40)% of 20 = 0.58 × 20 = 11.6 cm³.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a shift to the right means "higher affinity". It means lower affinity: at any pO₂, less oxygen is bound.
- Saying the first oxygen binds easily. The first is hardest; then cooperative binding makes the next ones easier.
- Saying the left ventricle is thicker "to pump more blood". Both ventricles pump the same volume; the left pumps at higher pressure.
- Saying arteries "pump" blood. Their elastic recoil maintains pressure; the heart pumps.
- Saying "valves open because the heart contracts". Valves move because of pressure differences.
- Saying tissue fluid returns by "diffusion". Water returns by osmosis.
- Saying pulmonary veins carry deoxygenated blood.

#### Practical

**Heart dissection** (AQA RP5, OCR A 3.1.2(e) and PAG2).

1. Identify the front (ventral) side: the coronary arteries run diagonally across it.
2. Find the aorta, pulmonary artery, vena cava and pulmonary veins at the top. Push a blunt probe or glass rod into vessels to find which chamber each joins.
3. Cut through the wall of the right ventricle from the pulmonary artery downwards; then do the same on the left. Compare wall thicknesses.
4. Find the AV valves and tendinous cords, and the semilunar valves (cup-shaped pockets) at the base of the arteries.
5. Make a labelled drawing.

Safety: scalpel cuts away from the body; gloves; disinfect surfaces; wash hands. Ethics: use hearts from animals killed for food.

### Check questions

1. Explain the S-shape of the oxygen dissociation curve. (3 marks)
2. Explain how the Bohr effect helps supply oxygen to muscles during exercise. (3 marks)
3. The lugworm lives in burrows where water has a low oxygen concentration. Its haemoglobin dissociation curve is to the left of human haemoglobin. Explain the advantage. (2 marks)
4. Describe the events of ventricular systole, including the valves. (3 marks)
5. Explain how the structure of an artery relates to its function. (3 marks)
6. Explain how tissue fluid is formed and how most of it returns to the blood. (5 marks)
7. A person's cardiac output is 6.3 dm³ min⁻¹ and heart rate 90 beats min⁻¹. Calculate stroke volume in cm³. (2 marks)
8. Describe the role of carbonic anhydrase and explain the chloride shift. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. First O₂ binds with difficulty (haem groups not exposed) (1); binding changes quaternary structure / shape, making other sites easier to bind: cooperative binding, giving the steep part (1); last O₂ harder because few sites left, so curve levels off (1).
2. Muscles respire faster, releasing more CO₂ (1); CO₂ / H⁺ lowers haemoglobin's affinity / shape changes / curve shifts right (1); more oxygen unloaded to muscles (1).
3. Haemoglobin has higher affinity for oxygen (1); so can load / become saturated at low pO₂ (1).
4. Ventricles contract and pressure rises (1); AV valves close when ventricular pressure exceeds atrial pressure (1); semilunar valves open when ventricular pressure exceeds arterial pressure; blood leaves (1).
5. Elastic tissue stretches and recoils, smoothing flow / maintaining pressure (1); thick muscle / wall withstands high pressure (1); folded endothelium allows stretching; small lumen maintains pressure (1).
6. High hydrostatic pressure at arteriole end (1); forces water and small molecules out through gaps (1); large proteins / cells remain, so plasma has lower water potential (1); at venule end hydrostatic pressure lower, so water returns by osmosis (1); excess drains into lymph (1).
7. 6300 cm³ ÷ 90 (1) = 70 cm³ (1).
8. Carbonic anhydrase catalyses CO₂ + water → carbonic acid (1); which dissociates to H⁺ and HCO₃⁻; HCO₃⁻ diffuses out of red blood cell (1); Cl⁻ moves in to balance charge (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/09-digestion-and-absorption.md -->

## Digestion and absorption

Large food molecules must be hydrolysed into small ones before they can be absorbed into the blood. This file covers the enzymes of mammalian digestion and how the products cross the ileum wall.

### Overview

AS content for AQA. Only AQA and Eduqas examine human digestion in detail. Eduqas's wider study of nutrition (saprotrophs, herbivores, parasites) is in file 45.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.3. Eduqas C3.3(g), (h).
- Edexcel, OCR A and OCR B do not have a digestion section; they meet digestive enzymes only as examples of extracellular enzymes (file 02) and co-transport (file 06).
- Eduqas also asks for the structure of the gut wall and for digestion in the mouth, stomach and duodenum with named enzymes (C3.3(g), (h)); these are included below.

### 3.3.3 Digestion and absorption

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3 · Eduqas C3.3(g), (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- During digestion, large biological molecules are hydrolysed to smaller molecules that can be absorbed across cell membranes.
- Digestion in mammals of:
  - carbohydrates by amylases and membrane-bound disaccharidases
  - lipids by lipase, including the action of bile salts
  - proteins by endopeptidases, exopeptidases and membrane-bound dipeptidases.
- Mechanisms for the absorption of the products of digestion by cells lining the ileum of mammals, including co-transport mechanisms for monosaccharides and amino acids, and the role of micelles in the absorption of lipids.

#### Explained

**Carbohydrates.**

1. Salivary amylase in the mouth begins hydrolysing starch to maltose (breaking α-1,4 glycosidic bonds).
2. Amylase stops working in the acidic stomach.
3. Pancreatic amylase, secreted into the duodenum, continues hydrolysing starch to maltose.
4. Membrane-bound disaccharidases on the epithelial cells of the ileum hydrolyse disaccharides:
   - maltase: maltose → glucose + glucose
   - sucrase: sucrose → glucose + fructose
   - lactase: lactose → glucose + galactose.

Because these enzymes are fixed in the membrane, the monosaccharides are released right next to the transport proteins that absorb them.

**Lipids.**

1. Bile, made in the liver and stored in the gall bladder, is released into the duodenum. Bile salts emulsify lipids: they break large fat droplets into many small droplets, giving a much larger surface area for lipase to act on. Bile salts do not break bonds.
2. Pancreatic lipase hydrolyses the ester bonds in triglycerides, producing monoglycerides and fatty acids.
3. Bile salts, monoglycerides and fatty acids form tiny clusters called micelles.

**Proteins.**

- Endopeptidases hydrolyse peptide bonds within a polypeptide, making many shorter chains with more ends. Example: pepsin in the stomach (works at about pH 2); trypsin from the pancreas.
- Exopeptidases hydrolyse peptide bonds at the ends of polypeptides, removing single amino acids.
- Membrane-bound dipeptidases on the ileum epithelium hydrolyse dipeptides into two amino acids.

Working together, endopeptidases increase the number of ends for exopeptidases to act on, so digestion is faster.

**Absorption in the ileum.** The ileum is adapted with folds, villi and microvilli (a huge surface area), a thin epithelium one cell thick, a good blood supply in each villus (keeping glucose and amino acid concentrations low in the blood) and a lacteal (lymph vessel) in each villus for lipids. Epithelial cells have many mitochondria for active transport.

- *Glucose and galactose* (and amino acids): co-transport with sodium ions. Na⁺ is actively pumped out of the epithelial cell into the blood by the sodium–potassium pump. This keeps Na⁺ inside the cell low. Na⁺ diffuses from the lumen into the cell through a co-transporter protein, which carries glucose in at the same time, against its concentration gradient. Glucose moves out of the cell into the blood by facilitated diffusion. Amino acids are absorbed by a similar Na⁺-dependent co-transport.
- *Fructose* is absorbed by facilitated diffusion.
- *Lipids:* micelles carry monoglycerides and fatty acids to the epithelial cell surface. Micelles constantly break up and re-form, releasing monoglycerides and fatty acids, which are non-polar and diffuse across the membrane. Inside the cell they move to the smooth ER, where they are recombined into triglycerides. In the Golgi apparatus, triglycerides are combined with cholesterol and lipoproteins to form chylomicrons. Chylomicrons leave the cell by exocytosis and enter the lacteals, travelling in the lymph before reaching the blood.

**Gut wall and other regions** (Eduqas). The gut wall has four layers: mucosa (epithelium, with glands); submucosa (blood and lymph vessels); muscularis externa (circular and longitudinal muscle for peristalsis); serosa (outer connective tissue). The stomach makes hydrochloric acid (kills microorganisms, gives the low pH for pepsin) and pepsin; mucus protects the stomach wall. In the duodenum, pancreatic juice (containing amylase, lipase, trypsin and hydrogencarbonate ions that neutralise acid) and bile are added. The colon absorbs water.

#### Worked example

Lactase activity was measured in four people. A sample of lactose solution was incubated with each person's intestinal enzyme for 10 minutes, and glucose was measured: A 2.4, B 0.3, C 2.1, D 0.2 mmol dm⁻³. Interpret.

B and D produced very little glucose, so they make little lactase. They would hydrolyse little lactose; it would stay in the gut lumen, lowering the water potential there and drawing water into the gut, and gut bacteria would ferment it. These are the symptoms of lactose intolerance.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bile salts or bile "digest fats". They emulsify; lipase hydrolyses.
- Saying fatty acids enter cells by co-transport. They are lipid-soluble and diffuse.
- Calling disaccharidases "secreted into the lumen". They are bound to the membrane of the epithelial cells.
- Saying endopeptidases make "amino acids". They make shorter polypeptides.
- Saying glucose is absorbed into the lacteal. Monosaccharides and amino acids go into the blood capillaries; lipids go into the lacteals.

### Check questions

1. Name the products of the hydrolysis of lactose. (1 mark)
2. Explain the role of bile salts in the digestion and absorption of lipids. (3 marks)
3. Explain why the combined action of endopeptidases and exopeptidases is faster than exopeptidases alone. (2 marks)
4. Describe how amino acids are absorbed by co-transport into an ileum epithelial cell. (4 marks)
5. Describe what happens to monoglycerides and fatty acids after they enter an epithelial cell. (3 marks)
6. A drug inhibits the sodium–potassium pump in ileum epithelial cells. Explain how it would affect glucose absorption. (3 marks)
7. Explain why disaccharidases are bound to the cell-surface membranes of ileum epithelial cells. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Glucose and galactose (1).
2. Emulsify lipids into smaller droplets, increasing surface area for lipase (1); form micelles with monoglycerides and fatty acids (1); carry them to the epithelium / keep them in solution near the surface (1).
3. Endopeptidases hydrolyse bonds within polypeptides, making more ends (1); more ends for exopeptidases to act on (1).
4. Sodium ions actively transported out of cell into blood (1); lower Na⁺ concentration inside cell than in lumen (1); Na⁺ and amino acid bind to co-transporter (1); Na⁺ diffuses in, carrying amino acid against its gradient (1).
5. Move to smooth ER and recombined into triglycerides (1); combined with protein / cholesterol in the Golgi to form chylomicrons (1); leave by exocytosis into lacteal / lymph (1).
6. Na⁺ not pumped out, so Na⁺ concentration in cell rises (1); no Na⁺ gradient from lumen into cell (1); less glucose enters by co-transport (1).
7. Monosaccharides released close to the co-transporter / transport proteins (1); so maintains concentration gradient / fast absorption (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/11-mass-transport-in-plants.md -->

## Mass transport in plants: xylem and phloem

Plants move water and mineral ions up from the roots in xylem, and sugars from leaves to where they are needed in phloem. This file covers both, with transpiration, the potometer and adaptations to water availability.

### Overview

AS content. Sclerenchyma and plant fibres (Edexcel A) are in file 41. How xylem and phloem form from stem cells (OCR A 2.1.6(l)) is in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.4.2. Edexcel A 4.11 (part). Edexcel B 4.7 i to v, CP8. OCR A 3.1.3(a) to (f). OCR B 2.2.4(a) to (f). Eduqas C3.2(i) to (r).
- OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for the root: water entering root hairs, apoplast and symplast pathways, the endodermis and Casparian strip. AQA does not; it is included below for those boards.
- OCR A and Eduqas ask for xerophytes and hydrophytes (OCR A 3.1.3(e), Eduqas C3.2(p)); AQA mentions xerophytes only under gas exchange (file 08).
- OCR A asks for the distribution of xylem and phloem in roots, stems and leaves of dicots, and drawing stained sections (3.1.3(b)).
- Potometer practicals: OCR A PAG5 / 3.1.3(c), Edexcel B CP8, OCR B 2.2.4(c). AQA lists the potometer as a skills opportunity, not a required practical.

### 3.3.4.2 Mass transport in plants

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4.2 · Edexcel A 4.11 · Edexcel B 4.7 i to v, CP8 · OCR A 3.1.3(a) to (f) · OCR B 2.2.4(a) to (f) · Eduqas C3.2(i) to (r). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Xylem as the tissue that transports water in the stem and leaves of plants. The cohesion-tension theory of water transport in the xylem.
- Phloem as the tissue that transports organic substances in plants. The mass flow hypothesis for the mechanism of translocation.
- Recognise xylem vessels, sieve tube elements and companion cells in photographs and diagrams.
- Interpret evidence from tracer and ringing experiments and evaluate evidence for and against the mass flow hypothesis.

#### Explained

**Xylem** vessels are long tubes made from dead cells joined end to end. The end walls have broken down, so there is a continuous column. Their walls are thickened with lignin (in spiral, ring or net patterns), which stops them collapsing under tension and makes them waterproof. Pits in the walls let water move sideways between vessels.

**Water uptake in the root** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B, Eduqas).

1. Root hair cells have a large surface area. Mineral ions are actively transported into them, so their water potential is lower than the soil's; water enters by osmosis.
2. Water crosses the cortex by two pathways:
   - Apoplast pathway: through the cellulose cell walls and spaces between cells. Fast, with little resistance; water is pulled along by cohesion.
   - Symplast pathway: through the cytoplasm of cells, passing from cell to cell through plasmodesmata. (Eduqas also names the vacuolar pathway: through vacuoles as well as cytoplasm.)
3. At the endodermis, the Casparian strip (a band of waterproof suberin in the cell walls) blocks the apoplast. Water must enter the cytoplasm, so the plant controls which ions pass.
4. Endodermis cells actively pump ions into the xylem, lowering its water potential, so water follows by osmosis. This creates **root pressure**, which pushes water a short way up the xylem (it explains guttation and why cut stems "bleed").

**Cohesion-tension theory.**

1. Water evaporates from the moist walls of mesophyll cells into air spaces and diffuses out through the stomata: transpiration.
2. This lowers the water potential of the mesophyll cells, so water moves in from neighbouring cells and ultimately from the xylem.
3. Water molecules are held together by hydrogen bonds (**cohesion**), so as water leaves the top of the xylem, the whole column is pulled up.
4. Water molecules also stick to the lignified xylem walls (**adhesion**), helping the column rise (capillarity).
5. The pull creates **tension** (negative pressure) in the xylem.

Evidence for cohesion-tension: tree trunks get measurably narrower during the day, when transpiration is fastest, because tension pulls the xylem walls inwards; if a xylem vessel is broken, air is drawn in rather than water leaking out, and the plant can no longer pull water up that vessel (the column is broken).

**Transpiration** is the loss of water vapour from the leaves, mainly through stomata. Factors:

| Factor | Effect on rate | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Light intensity | Increases | Stomata open in light |
| Temperature | Increases | Water molecules have more kinetic energy, evaporate faster; air holds more water vapour so gradient is steeper |
| Humidity | Decreases | Smaller water potential gradient between the leaf and the air |
| Air movement (wind) | Increases | Moist air around the stomata is blown away, keeping the gradient steep |

**Phloem** is made of living cells.

- Sieve tube elements: joined end to end; perforated end walls called sieve plates; no nucleus and very few organelles, so little obstruction to flow.
- Companion cells: next to each sieve tube element, linked by many plasmodesmata; have a nucleus and many mitochondria; they provide ATP for active loading of sucrose and keep the sieve tube element alive.

**Translocation** moves organic substances (mainly sucrose, also amino acids) from **sources** (where they are made, such as leaves) to **sinks** (where they are used or stored, such as roots, growing tips and fruits). It can go up or down.

**Mass flow hypothesis.**

1. At the source, sucrose is actively loaded into sieve tubes (companion cells pump H⁺ out using ATP; H⁺ diffuses back in with sucrose through co-transporter proteins).
2. This lowers the water potential in the sieve tube, so water enters from the xylem by osmosis.
3. Hydrostatic pressure rises at the source end.
4. At the sink, sucrose is removed (used in respiration or converted to starch), so the water potential there is higher; water leaves the sieve tube by osmosis, lowering the pressure.
5. The pressure difference pushes the solution from source to sink: mass flow.

**Evidence.**

*Ringing experiments.* Removing a ring of bark (which contains the phloem but not the xylem) from a tree trunk makes the bark above the ring swell with sugary fluid, and tissue below dies. This shows phloem carries sugars down the trunk; the xylem still carries water because leaves above stay alive for a while.

*Tracer experiments.* Leaves supplied with ¹⁴CO₂ make radioactive sugars. Autoradiographs of stem sections show the radioactivity in the phloem. Radioactivity later appears in sinks.

*Aphids.* An aphid's mouthparts (stylet) pierce a single sieve tube; cutting off the aphid leaves the stylet behind, and sap flows out under pressure. Sap closer to the leaves has a higher sucrose concentration.

*For mass flow:* sap is under pressure; there is a sucrose concentration gradient from source to sink; metabolic poisons that stop ATP production stop translocation (needed for loading).

*Against mass flow:* sieve plates would slow flow a lot; different solutes move at different speeds and in different directions in the same phloem, which a simple pressure-driven flow cannot explain; sucrose reaches all sinks at a similar rate, not faster to the lower-concentration ones.

**Adaptations to water availability** (OCR A, OCR B, Eduqas).

- *Xerophytes* (marram grass, cacti): thick waxy cuticle; rolled leaves with stomata on the inner surface; sunken stomata in pits; hairs trapping moist air; fewer stomata; leaves reduced to spines; succulent tissue storing water; long or wide shallow roots; low water potential in cells.
- *Hydrophytes* (water lilies): stomata on the upper surface of floating leaves; little or no cuticle; air spaces (aerenchyma) that keep leaves afloat and supply oxygen to submerged parts; little xylem or lignin, because water supports the plant.
- *Mesophytes* (Eduqas): plants of moderate water supply, such as most crop plants; they close stomata and may wilt in drought.

**Distribution of vascular tissue** (OCR A). In a dicot root the xylem forms a central star (X shape) with phloem between its arms, helping the root resist pulling forces. In a stem, vascular bundles form a ring near the outside, with xylem on the inside and phloem on the outside, resisting bending. In a leaf, they form the midrib and veins, with xylem on the upper side.

#### Worked example

A potometer has a capillary tube with an internal radius of 0.5 mm. An air bubble moves 24 mm in 4 minutes. Calculate the rate of water uptake in mm³ min⁻¹.

1. Volume = πr² × length = π × 0.5² × 24 = π × 0.25 × 24 = 18.85 mm³.
2. Rate = 18.85 ÷ 4 = 4.7 mm³ min⁻¹ (2 s.f.).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a potometer measures transpiration. It measures water uptake, which is close to transpiration but some water is used in photosynthesis and to keep cells turgid.
- Saying water moves up the xylem by osmosis or by "capillary action" alone.
- Saying sucrose diffuses into sieve tubes. It is actively loaded.
- Saying phloem cells are dead (xylem vessels are dead).
- Saying translocation only goes downward.
- Saying humidity increases transpiration.

#### Practical

**Measuring water uptake with a potometer** (OCR A PAG5, Edexcel B CP8, OCR B 2.2.4(c); AQA skills opportunity).

1. Cut a shoot under water, at a slant, so no air enters the xylem.
2. Assemble the potometer under water; fit the shoot with a tight seal; seal joints with petroleum jelly.
3. Dry the leaves; let the shoot adjust to the conditions for a few minutes.
4. Introduce one air bubble; measure how far it moves in a set time. Reset with the reservoir tap.
5. Change one factor (light distance, a fan, temperature, a plastic bag for humidity) and repeat.

Control variables: same shoot or shoots of equal leaf area, same time, same other conditions. To compare plants, divide by leaf area.

### Check questions

1. Explain how water moves up the xylem according to the cohesion-tension theory. (4 marks)
2. Explain why the diameter of a tree trunk is smallest around midday. (2 marks)
3. Describe the role of the Casparian strip. (2 marks)
4. Describe the mass flow hypothesis for translocation. (5 marks)
5. Give one piece of evidence against the mass flow hypothesis. (1 mark)
6. Explain why a ring of bark removed from a tree causes swelling above the ring. (2 marks)
7. A potometer bubble moves 15 mm in 3 min. The capillary radius is 0.4 mm. Calculate the rate of water uptake in mm³ min⁻¹. (2 marks)
8. Explain two ways marram grass is adapted to reduce water loss. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Water evaporates from leaves / transpiration (1); lowers water potential, water drawn out of xylem (1); cohesion: hydrogen bonds between water molecules pull a continuous column up (1); adhesion to xylem walls; creates tension (1).
2. Transpiration fastest at midday (1); tension in xylem pulls walls inwards (1).
3. Waterproof band in endodermis cell walls blocks apoplast pathway (1); water / ions must pass through cytoplasm, so their entry is controlled (1).
4. Sucrose actively loaded into sieve tubes at source (1); lowers water potential, water enters by osmosis (1); high hydrostatic pressure at source (1); sucrose removed at sink, water leaves, lower pressure (1); pressure gradient moves solution to sink (1).
5. Any one: sieve plates would hinder flow; solutes move in different directions / at different rates; sucrose delivered to sinks at similar rates regardless of concentration.
6. Phloem removed with the bark (1); sugars carried down cannot pass, so build up above / water enters by osmosis (1).
7. π × 0.4² × 15 = 7.54 mm³ (1); ÷ 3 = 2.5 mm³ min⁻¹ (1).
8. Any two with explanation (2 each): rolled leaves trap moist air, reducing water potential gradient; sunken stomata / hairs trap moist air; thick waxy cuticle reduces evaporation; fewer stomata.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/12-dna-genes-and-protein-synthesis.md -->

## DNA, genes, chromosomes and protein synthesis

A gene is a length of DNA that codes for a polypeptide. This file covers how DNA is packaged in different cells, the genetic code, and how proteins are made by transcription and translation.

### Overview

AS content. Mutations are in files 13 (introduction) and 24 (A-level detail). Control of gene expression is in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.1, 3.4.2. Edexcel A 2.6 to 2.8, 2.12 (part). Edexcel B 1.4 iii to vii. OCR A 2.1.3(f), (g). OCR B 2.1.4(e) to (g). Eduqas CC5(i) to (n).
- Edexcel B and OCR B ask for the structure of mRNA and tRNA in detail (Edexcel B 1.4 iv, v; OCR B 2.1.4(f)).
- AQA asks how DNA differs in prokaryotes, mitochondria and chloroplasts, and for the terms genome and proteome; other boards cover some of this in cell structure.
- Eduqas names the "one gene – one polypeptide" hypothesis (CC5(n)).
- Splicing: AQA here; Edexcel A 6.10, Edexcel B 7.2 iii and OCR B 5.1.3(a) treat it as gene expression (file 24).

### 3.4.1 DNA, genes and chromosomes

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1 · Edexcel A 2.7, 2.8 · Edexcel B 1.4 iii, vii · OCR A 2.1.3(f) · OCR B 2.1.4(e) · Eduqas CC5(i), (j), (k), (n). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- In prokaryotic cells, DNA molecules are short, circular and not associated with proteins.
- In the nucleus of eukaryotic cells, DNA molecules are very long, linear and associated with proteins called histones. Together a DNA molecule and its associated proteins form a chromosome.
- The mitochondria and chloroplasts of eukaryotic cells also contain DNA which, like prokaryotic DNA, is short, circular and not associated with protein.
- A gene is a base sequence of DNA that codes for the amino acid sequence of a polypeptide, or a functional RNA (including ribosomal RNA and tRNAs).
- A gene occupies a fixed position, called a locus, on a particular DNA molecule.
- A sequence of three DNA bases, called a triplet, codes for a specific amino acid. The genetic code is universal, non-overlapping and degenerate.
- In eukaryotes, much of the nuclear DNA does not code for polypeptides. There are, for example, non-coding multiple repeats of base sequences between genes. Even within a gene only some sequences, called exons, code for amino acid sequences. Within the gene, these exons are separated by one or more non-coding sequences, called introns.

#### Explained

**Packaging.** Each human nucleus holds about 2 m of DNA. It is wound around histone proteins (like thread round a bobbin) and coiled again and again into a compact chromosome. In prokaryotes there are no histones; the circular DNA is just looped and folded.

**The genetic code.**

- *Triplet:* three bases code for one amino acid. Four bases taken three at a time give 4³ = 64 combinations, more than enough for 20 amino acids.
- *Degenerate:* most amino acids have more than one triplet. This means some base substitutions do not change the amino acid.
- *Non-overlapping:* each base is read once, as part of only one triplet.
- *Universal:* the same triplet codes for the same amino acid in (nearly) all organisms. This is evidence of common ancestry and is why a human gene can be expressed in a bacterium.
- *Start and stop:* a start codon (AUG on mRNA, coding for methionine) begins translation; stop codons (UAA, UAG, UGA) code for no amino acid and end translation.

**Non-coding DNA.** Only about 1.5% of human DNA codes for proteins. The rest includes introns, regulatory sequences, genes for functional RNAs, and repeated sequences between genes (useful in genetic fingerprinting, file 25).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a gene codes for "a protein" or "a characteristic". It codes for a polypeptide (or a functional RNA).
- Saying the genetic code is "universal" means all organisms have the same DNA. It means the same triplets code for the same amino acids.
- Saying introns are removed from DNA. They are removed from pre-mRNA.

### 3.4.2 DNA and protein synthesis

*Refs: AQA 3.4.2 · Edexcel A 2.6, 2.7 · Edexcel B 1.4 iv to vi · OCR A 2.1.3(g) · OCR B 2.1.4(f), (g) · Eduqas CC5(k) to (n). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The concept of the genome as the complete set of genes in a cell and of the proteome as the full range of proteins that a cell is able to produce.
- The structure of molecules of messenger RNA (mRNA) and of transfer RNA (tRNA).
- Transcription as the production of mRNA from DNA. The role of RNA polymerase in joining mRNA nucleotides. In prokaryotes, transcription results directly in the production of mRNA from DNA. In eukaryotes, transcription results in the production of pre-mRNA; this is then spliced to form mRNA.
- Translation as the production of polypeptides from the sequence of codons carried by mRNA. The role of ribosomes and tRNA, and the role of ATP in translation.
- Relate the base sequence of nucleic acids to the amino acid sequence of polypeptides, when provided with suitable data about the genetic code.
- Interpret data from experimental work investigating the role of nucleic acids.

#### Explained

**Genome and proteome.** Every body cell has the same genome, but each cell uses only some of its genes, so different cells have different proteomes. One gene can give several proteins by alternative splicing (file 24), so the proteome can be larger than the number of genes.

**mRNA and tRNA.**

| | mRNA | tRNA |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shape | Single, linear strand | Single strand folded into a clover-leaf shape |
| Hydrogen bonds | None | Yes, between paired bases within the molecule |
| Key part | Codons (triplets of bases complementary to the DNA template) | Anticodon (three bases complementary to an mRNA codon); amino acid binding site at one end |
| Length | Varies with gene | Short, about 80 nucleotides |

**Transcription** (in the nucleus).

1. DNA helicase unwinds the gene and breaks the hydrogen bonds between bases, exposing the template strand.
2. Free RNA nucleotides pair with complementary bases on the template strand (A with U, T with A, C with G, G with C).
3. RNA polymerase moves along the template, joining RNA nucleotides with phosphodiester bonds (condensation).
4. At a stop sequence, RNA polymerase detaches; pre-mRNA is released; the DNA rewinds.
5. In eukaryotes, the pre-mRNA is **spliced**: introns are removed and exons are joined to make mature mRNA.
6. mRNA leaves the nucleus through a nuclear pore.

In prokaryotes there are no introns, so transcription makes mRNA directly, and translation can start before transcription finishes.

**Translation** (in the cytoplasm, at ribosomes).

1. A ribosome binds to the mRNA at the start codon.
2. Each tRNA carries a specific amino acid; attaching the amino acid to its tRNA uses ATP.
3. A tRNA with an anticodon complementary to the first codon pairs with it (hydrogen bonds). A second tRNA pairs with the next codon. The ribosome holds two tRNAs at once.
4. A peptide bond forms between the two amino acids (condensation, catalysed by an enzyme in the ribosome, using ATP).
5. The ribosome moves along one codon; the first tRNA is released and goes off to collect another amino acid.
6. This continues until a stop codon is reached; the polypeptide is released.
7. Several ribosomes can translate one mRNA at the same time (a polysome), making many copies quickly.

The polypeptide then folds into its secondary and tertiary structure and may be modified in the Golgi apparatus (file 04).

**Evidence for the role of nucleic acids.** Typical data: when mRNA and ribosomes are added to a cell-free mixture of amino acids, tRNA and ATP, polypeptides are made; if RNase (which hydrolyses RNA) is added, no polypeptide is made. Synthetic mRNA made only of U nucleotides (UUU...) produced a polypeptide of only phenylalanine, showing UUU codes for phenylalanine. This was how the code was cracked.

#### Worked example

A section of DNA template strand reads: TAC GGA TTC CGT.

1. mRNA (complementary, U for A): AUG CCU AAG GCA.
2. tRNA anticodons (complementary to mRNA): UAC GGA UUC CGU.
3. Using a code table: AUG = Met, CCU = Pro, AAG = Lys, GCA = Ala.

The polypeptide begins Met–Pro–Lys–Ala.

Note that the anticodons have the same sequence as the DNA template, with U in place of T.

If a gene has 1200 bases in its exons and no stop codon is counted, how many amino acids does its polypeptide have? 1200 ÷ 3 = 400 (minus one if the last triplet is a stop codon).

#### Common mistakes

- Using T in mRNA or tRNA.
- Saying RNA polymerase forms hydrogen bonds between the mRNA and DNA.
- Saying "the tRNA reads the codon" without mentioning complementary anticodon.
- Saying the ribosome makes the amino acids.
- Saying introns are translated.
- Writing "translation happens in the nucleus".

### Check questions

1. Give two differences between DNA in a eukaryotic nucleus and DNA in a prokaryotic cell. (2 marks)
2. Explain what is meant by the genetic code being degenerate, and why this means some mutations have no effect. (3 marks)
3. Describe the role of RNA polymerase in transcription. (2 marks)
4. Explain why the mRNA of a eukaryotic gene is shorter than the gene itself. (2 marks)
5. Describe the role of tRNA in translation. (3 marks)
6. The mRNA codons for an amino acid sequence are GCU UGG AAA. Give the DNA template strand sequence. (1 mark)
7. Explain why two cells from the same person can have the same genome but different proteomes. (2 marks)
8. Give two differences between the structure of tRNA and mRNA. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any two: eukaryotic DNA long, linear, associated with histones / in chromosomes; prokaryotic DNA short, circular, not associated with proteins.
2. More than one triplet / codon codes for the same amino acid (1); a substitution may change the triplet into another for the same amino acid (1); so primary structure unchanged (1).
3. Joins RNA nucleotides together (1); by phosphodiester bonds / condensation, forming pre-mRNA (1).
4. Pre-mRNA contains introns (1); introns are removed by splicing (1).
5. Carries a specific amino acid (1); anticodon complementary to mRNA codon (1); brings amino acid to ribosome so that amino acids are joined in the right order (1).
6. CGA ACC TTT (1).
7. Different genes are expressed / switched on (1); so different proteins made (1).
8. Any two: tRNA clover-leaf shape vs mRNA linear; tRNA has hydrogen bonds between bases; tRNA has an anticodon / amino acid binding site; mRNA longer and has codons.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/13-mutation-meiosis-and-genetic-diversity.md -->

## Mutation, meiosis, genetic diversity and natural selection

Genetic diversity comes from mutation, meiosis and random fertilisation. Natural selection acts on that diversity. This file covers each, with AQA required practical 6 (aseptic technique and antimicrobial substances).

### Overview

AQA AS content. Gene mutations at A-level depth (frameshifts, mutagens, effects on proteins) are in file 24. Chromosome disorders in medicine (Down's syndrome, karyotypes) are in file 39. Evolution, Hardy–Weinberg and speciation are in file 22.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.3, 3.4.4, RP6. Edexcel A 3.9, 4.4. Edexcel B 2.3 iv, v, vii, 3.2 i, 8.1. OCR A 2.1.6(f), (g), 4.2.2(f) to (i), 6.1.2(a). OCR B 3.1.2(a) to (c), 3.1.3(e), (g). Eduqas C2.1(m), (n), C2.2(d), (e), C2.6(a), (b).
- OCR A asks for types of variation (continuous, discontinuous; genetic, environmental) with standard deviation and Student's t-test (4.2.2(f)); statistics are in file 26.
- OCR A and Eduqas ask for adaptations classed as anatomical, physiological and behavioural, and for convergent evolution (OCR A 4.2.2(g)).
- OCR A 4.2.2(i) asks for evolution of resistance in bacteria and pests; antibiotic resistance is also in file 27.
- AQA RP6 (aseptic technique) is grouped here because AQA places it under 3.4.4 (antibiotic resistance). Culturing for other boards is in file 28.

### 3.4.3 Genetic diversity can arise as a result of mutation or during meiosis

*Refs: AQA 3.4.3 · Edexcel A 3.9 · Edexcel B 2.3 iv, v, vii, 8.1 i, ii · OCR A 2.1.6(f), (g), 6.1.2(a) · OCR B 3.1.2(a) to (c) · Eduqas C2.2(d), (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Gene mutations involve a change in the base sequence of chromosomes. They can arise spontaneously during DNA replication and include base deletion and base substitution. Due to the degenerate nature of the genetic code, not all base substitutions cause a change in the sequence of encoded amino acids. Mutagenic agents can increase the rate of gene mutation.
- Mutations in the number of chromosomes can arise spontaneously by chromosome non-disjunction during meiosis.
- Meiosis produces daughter cells that are genetically different from each other. The process of meiosis only in sufficient detail to show how: two nuclear divisions result in the formation of haploid daughter cells from a single diploid parent cell; genetically different daughter cells result from the independent segregation of homologous chromosomes; crossing over between homologous chromosomes results in further genetic variation among daughter cells.
- Appreciate that random fertilisation of haploid gametes further increases genetic variation within a species.
- Complete diagrams showing the chromosome content of cells after the first and second meiotic division, when given the chromosome content of the parent cell. Explain the different outcomes of mitosis and meiosis. Recognise where meiosis occurs when given information about an unfamiliar life cycle. Explain how random fertilisation produces genetic variation. Calculate the number of possible combinations of chromosomes, using 2ⁿ.

#### Explained

**Gene mutations.**

- *Substitution:* one base replaced by another. Only one triplet changes. It may give the same amino acid (silent, because the code is degenerate), a different amino acid (missense), or a stop codon (nonsense, giving a short polypeptide).
- *Deletion:* one base lost. Every triplet after it changes: a frameshift. Usually the polypeptide is very different and does not work.

Mutagenic agents (mutagens) include ionising radiation (X-rays, gamma rays), ultraviolet light, and chemicals such as benzene and some in tobacco smoke.

**Homologous chromosomes** are a matching pair, one from each parent, with the same genes at the same loci but possibly different alleles. A diploid cell (2n) has two sets; a haploid cell (n) has one set. Human body cells: 2n = 46; gametes: n = 23.

**Meiosis.**

| Division | Main events |
| --- | --- |
| Meiosis I | Homologous chromosomes pair up (forming bivalents); crossing over happens; pairs line up on the equator; homologous chromosomes (each still two chromatids) are separated to opposite poles. Result: two haploid cells. |
| Meiosis II | Like mitosis: chromosomes line up singly; sister chromatids separate. Result: four haploid cells. |

Named stages for OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B and Eduqas: prophase I, metaphase I, anaphase I, telophase I, then prophase II, metaphase II, anaphase II, telophase II.

**How meiosis makes variety.**

1. *Independent segregation (independent assortment):* at metaphase I, each homologous pair lines up independently of the others. Which parent's chromosome goes to which pole is random. With n pairs, the number of possible combinations in the gametes is 2ⁿ.
2. *Crossing over:* in prophase I, chromatids of homologous chromosomes wrap round each other; they break and swap equivalent sections at points called chiasmata. New combinations of alleles appear on each chromatid.
3. *Random fertilisation:* any male gamete can fuse with any female gamete. The number of combinations of chromosomes in a zygote from two parents is (2ⁿ)², even ignoring crossing over.

**Non-disjunction.** If homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) fail to separate, one gamete gets an extra chromosome and another gets one fewer. Fertilisation then produces a zygote with 2n + 1 (trisomy) or 2n − 1 (monosomy). Down's syndrome is trisomy 21 (detail in file 39).

**Mitosis compared with meiosis.**

| | Mitosis | Meiosis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Divisions | One | Two |
| Cells made | Two | Four |
| Chromosome number | Same as parent (diploid stays diploid) | Halved (diploid to haploid) |
| Genetic make-up | Identical to each other and the parent | Different from each other and the parent |
| Homologous pairs | Do not pair | Pair up and cross over |
| Use | Growth, repair, asexual reproduction | Gamete formation for sexual reproduction |

**Unfamiliar life cycles.** Meiosis happens wherever the chromosome number halves (2n → n). In mammals it is in gamete formation; in fungi and many algae it happens straight after the zygote forms; in plants it forms spores.

#### Worked example

1. A species has 2n = 8. How many combinations of chromosomes are possible in its gametes, and in a zygote from two parents (ignoring crossing over)?

n = 4. Gametes: 2⁴ = 16. Zygotes: 16² = 256.

2. A cell with 2n = 4 contains 8 units of DNA at the start of meiosis (after replication). How many units are in each cell after meiosis I and after meiosis II?

After meiosis I: 4 units (two chromosomes, each two chromatids). After meiosis II: 2 units.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying crossing over happens between sister chromatids. It happens between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes.
- Saying independent segregation happens in anaphase II.
- Saying a deletion changes "one amino acid".
- Saying meiosis gives "two cells" or "diploid gametes".

### 3.4.4 Genetic diversity and adaptation

*Refs: AQA 3.4.4, RP6 · Edexcel A 4.4 · Edexcel B 3.2 i · OCR A 4.2.2(f) to (i) · OCR B 3.1.3(e), (g) · Eduqas C2.1(m), (n), C2.6(a), (b). Level: AS & A (A-level only for Eduqas C2.6).*

#### What you need to know

- Genetic diversity as the number of different alleles of genes in a population.
- Genetic diversity is a factor enabling natural selection to occur.
- The principles of natural selection in the evolution of populations: random mutation can result in new alleles of a gene; many mutations are harmful but, in certain environments, the new allele of a gene might benefit its possessor, leading to increased reproductive success; the advantageous allele is inherited by members of the next generation; as a result, over many generations, the new allele increases in frequency in the population.
- Directional selection, exemplified by antibiotic resistance in bacteria, and stabilising selection, exemplified by human birth weights.
- Natural selection results in species that are better adapted to their environment. These adaptations may be anatomical, physiological or behavioural.
- Appreciate the importance of evidence in the theory of natural selection. Use unfamiliar information to explain how selection produces changes within a population.

#### Explained

**Genetic diversity** is the variety of alleles in a population. A population with more alleles is more likely to contain individuals that survive a change, such as a new disease. Small populations (after a bottleneck or founder event) lose diversity (file 22).

**Natural selection, step by step.**

1. Individuals vary because of different alleles, which arise by random mutation.
2. A selection pressure acts (a predator, a disease, a change in climate, an antibiotic).
3. Individuals with an advantageous allele are more likely to survive and reproduce.
