4. They pass the allele to their offspring.
5. Over many generations, the frequency of the allele increases in the population.

Selection does not cause the mutation. The allele must already be there.

**Types of selection.**

- *Directional:* individuals at one extreme are favoured, so the mean shifts. Example: bacteria with an allele for resistance survive an antibiotic and reproduce; the population becomes resistant. Others: peppered moths; insecticide resistance; warfarin resistance in rats.
- *Stabilising:* individuals near the mean are favoured and extremes are selected against, so the mean stays the same but the range (standard deviation) narrows. Example: human birth weight; very small babies are more likely to die of cold or infection, and very large babies are more likely to cause problems at birth.
- (OCR A, Edexcel B) *Disruptive:* both extremes are favoured; can lead to two forms and eventually speciation (file 22).

**Types of adaptation.**

| Type | Meaning | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Anatomical | Body structure | Thick fur in arctic foxes; sunken stomata; long loop of Henle in desert mammals |
| Physiological | Processes inside the body | Hibernation (lower metabolic rate); producing venom; high-affinity haemoglobin at altitude |
| Behavioural | Actions | Courtship displays; migration; woodlice moving to damp places |

**Variation** (OCR A, Eduqas). Variation is differences between individuals. *Continuous variation* has a range of values with no categories (height, mass); it is usually controlled by many genes (polygenic) plus the environment, and gives a normal distribution. *Discontinuous variation* falls into distinct categories (blood group, sex); it is usually controlled by one or two genes with little environmental effect. Variation is genetic (inherited), environmental (diet, light, training), or both. Only genetic variation is heritable and can be acted on by natural selection.

**Convergent evolution** (OCR A). Unrelated species in similar environments evolve similar adaptations: the marsupial mole of Australia and the placental mole of Europe both have spade-like forelimbs and tiny eyes.

**Importance of evidence.** Darwin's theory was accepted because it explained many observations (fossils, selective breeding, distribution of species) and made predictions that later evidence confirmed, such as the genetic basis of inheritance and DNA similarities between related species.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bacteria "become resistant" or "build up immunity" after exposure to antibiotics. A mutation gives resistance at random; the antibiotic selects those that already have it.
- Saying organisms "adapt" in their lifetime in response to need.
- Saying individuals evolve. Populations evolve.
- Forgetting the last step: "over many generations the allele frequency increases".
- Confusing genetic diversity with biodiversity.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 6** (similar to Edexcel A CP9, OCR A PAG7 and OCR B 3.2.3(g); culturing detail for those boards is in file 28): use of aseptic techniques to investigate the effect of antimicrobial substances on microbial growth.

1. Spread a liquid culture of a safe bacterium (such as *Bacillus subtilis*) evenly over a nutrient agar plate with a sterile spreader.
2. Soak paper discs in different antibiotics, antiseptics or plant extracts (and one in sterile water as a control). Place them on the agar with sterile forceps.
3. Tape the lid on with two short pieces (not sealed all round, so aerobic conditions stay and anaerobic pathogens do not grow), label the base, and incubate upside down at no more than 25 °C (so human pathogens do not grow well) for 48 hours.
4. Measure the diameter of each clear zone (zone of inhibition); calculate area = πr².

Aseptic technique: wash hands and disinfect the bench; work near a lit Bunsen burner (rising air carries microbes away); flame the neck of the culture bottle; sterilise inoculating loops in the flame until red hot; lift the Petri dish lid only slightly; dispose of plates by autoclaving. These stop contamination of the culture and protect the user.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a base substitution may have no effect on a polypeptide. (2 marks)
2. Explain how meiosis produces genetic variation. (4 marks)
3. A species has a diploid number of 12. Calculate the number of chromosome combinations possible in gametes from one individual. (1 mark)
4. Give three differences between mitosis and meiosis. (3 marks)
5. Describe how non-disjunction can produce a zygote with 47 chromosomes. (3 marks)
6. Explain how a population of bacteria can become resistant to an antibiotic. (5 marks)
7. Describe stabilising selection, using human birth weight as an example. (3 marks)
8. In RP6, explain why plates are incubated at 25 °C and not 37 °C, and why the lid is not sealed all round. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Genetic code is degenerate (1); new triplet may code for the same amino acid (1).
2. Independent segregation of homologous chromosomes (1); random alignment at metaphase I gives different combinations of maternal and paternal chromosomes (1); crossing over between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes (1); exchanges sections, giving new combinations of alleles (1).
3. 2⁶ = 64 (1).
4. Any three: one vs two divisions; two vs four cells; diploid vs haploid; identical vs genetically different; no pairing / crossing over in mitosis.
5. Homologous chromosomes / chromatids fail to separate in meiosis (1); one gamete gets an extra chromosome / 24 chromosomes (1); fertilised by normal gamete (23), giving 47 (1).
6. Random mutation gives an allele for resistance (1); antibiotic kills non-resistant bacteria / selection pressure (1); resistant bacteria survive and reproduce (1); pass on the allele (1); frequency of resistance allele increases over generations (1).
7. Individuals with intermediate / mean values favoured (1); very low and very high birth weight babies less likely to survive (1); mean stays the same, range / standard deviation narrows (1).
8. 25 °C reduces the chance of growing human pathogens (1); taping not all round keeps aerobic conditions, so anaerobic pathogens do not grow (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/14-species-taxonomy-and-biodiversity.md -->

## Species, taxonomy, biodiversity and investigating diversity

Biologists name and group organisms and measure how varied life is in a place. This file covers the species concept, classification, measuring biodiversity, and comparing species using DNA and proteins.

### Overview

AS content. Conservation methods are in files 23 and 42; sampling methods in the field are also in file 23; standard deviation and other statistics in file 26.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.5 to 3.4.7. Edexcel A 4.1 to 4.3, 4.6. Edexcel B 3.1, 3.2 ii, 3.3 i. OCR A 4.2.1(a) to (e), 4.2.2(a) to (d). OCR B 3.1.3(a) to (d), (f), (h), (i). Eduqas C2.1(a) to (l).
- AQA and Edexcel A use the index of diversity d = N(N − 1) ÷ Σn(n − 1). OCR A and Eduqas use Simpson's index D = 1 − Σ(n/N)². OCR B asks for both kinds of biodiversity calculation and genetic diversity. Both are shown below.
- OCR A, Edexcel A, Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for the three-domain system against the five-kingdom system. AQA mentions domains in the hierarchy only.
- Edexcel A and Edexcel B ask for the niche and for the role of the scientific community (peer review, conferences) in accepting new classifications.
- OCR B asks for DNA barcoding and uses the evolution of language as a model for phylogenetic thinking (3.1.3(c), (f)).
- OCR A and OCR B ask for genetic biodiversity measured as the proportion of polymorphic gene loci (OCR A 4.2.1(e), OCR B 3.1.3(i)).

### 3.4.5 Species and taxonomy

*Refs: AQA 3.4.5 · Edexcel A 4.6 · Edexcel B 3.1 i to vii · OCR A 4.2.2(a) to (d) · OCR B 3.1.3(a) to (d), (f) · Eduqas C2.1(a) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Two organisms belong to the same species if they are able to produce fertile offspring. Courtship behaviour as a necessary precursor to successful mating; its role in recognising members of the same species, identifying a mate that is capable of breeding, forming a pair bond, synchronising mating, and becoming able to breed.
- A phylogenetic classification system attempts to arrange species into groups based on their evolutionary origins and relationships. It uses a hierarchy in which smaller groups are placed within larger groups, with no overlap between groups. Each group is called a taxon (plural taxa).
- One hierarchy comprises the taxa: domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus and species.
- Each species is universally identified by a binomial consisting of the name of its genus and species, eg *Homo sapiens*.
- Appreciate that advances in immunology and genome sequencing help to clarify evolutionary relationships between organisms.

#### Explained

**The species concept.** A species is a group of similar organisms that can interbreed to produce fertile offspring. A horse and a donkey can mate, but their offspring (a mule) is infertile, so horses and donkeys are separate species.

**Limits of the definition** (Edexcel B 3.1 ii, iii). It cannot be used for organisms that reproduce asexually (bacteria), for extinct organisms known only from fossils, or for populations that never meet. Some species hybridise and produce fertile young (some plants, some gulls). So biologists also use the morphological concept (similar appearance) and the phylogenetic concept (a group sharing a recent common ancestor, defined by DNA).

**Courtship.** Courtship behaviours (displays, songs, dances) are species-specific. They let animals:

- recognise a member of their own species, so they do not waste energy on matings that give no fertile offspring
- recognise a mate of the opposite sex who is sexually mature and fertile (or receptive)
- form a pair bond, so both parents raise the young
- synchronise mating so it happens when eggs are available.

**Classification.** Organisms are placed in a hierarchy: **D**omain, **K**ingdom, **P**hylum, **C**lass, **O**rder, **F**amily, **G**enus, **S**pecies. Each group contains organisms that share a more recent common ancestor the further down you go. Groups do not overlap.

Example, the grey wolf: Eukarya, Animalia, Chordata, Mammalia, Carnivora, Canidae, *Canis*, *lupus*.

**Binomial naming.** Genus (capital first letter) plus species (lower case), in italics or underlined. It is universal, so it avoids confusion from different common names.

**Five kingdoms and three domains** (OCR A, Edexcel A, Edexcel B, Eduqas).

| Kingdom | Main features |
| --- | --- |
| Prokaryotae | Unicellular; no nucleus; murein walls; 70S ribosomes |
| Protoctista | Mostly unicellular eukaryotes; some like plants, some like animals (algae, amoeba) |
| Fungi | Eukaryotic; chitin walls; saprotrophic nutrition; hyphae; spores |
| Plantae | Multicellular; cellulose walls; chlorophyll; autotrophic |
| Animalia | Multicellular; no cell walls; heterotrophic; nervous coordination |

In 1990 Carl Woese proposed three domains above the kingdoms: **Bacteria**, **Archaea** and **Eukarya**. Evidence: sequences of ribosomal RNA showed archaea are as different from bacteria as both are from eukaryotes. Archaea have different membrane lipids, different RNA polymerase (more like eukaryotes'), and histone-like proteins; bacteria have murein walls but archaea do not. This divides Prokaryotae into two domains.

**Validating new ideas** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B). A new classification is published in a scientific journal after peer review (other experts check the method and conclusions), discussed at conferences, and tested by other scientists. If repeated evidence supports it, it becomes accepted. The three-domain model went through this.

**Phylogeny.** A phylogenetic tree shows evolutionary relationships. Branch points show common ancestors; species that branch apart more recently are more closely related. Classification is now based on phylogeny.

**Evidence used to classify** (OCR B, Eduqas, Edexcel B):

- physical features (anatomy, embryology)
- DNA base sequences and the amino acid sequences of proteins (more different = more distant)
- immunological comparisons: antibodies against a protein from one species are mixed with the same protein from another; the more precipitate forms, the more similar the proteins, so the more closely related the species
- DNA barcoding (OCR B): sequencing a short, standard gene region (in animals, part of the mitochondrial gene for cytochrome c oxidase I; in plants, chloroplast genes) and matching it against a database to identify a species quickly
- gel electrophoresis of DNA or proteins (Edexcel B; method in file 25).

**Language as a model** (OCR B). Languages change over time by small changes passed on, and split when groups of speakers become separated, much as species do. Linguists group words by shared features and build family trees of languages, using the same kind of methods as phylogenetics. It is a model, not evidence for biological evolution.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing the binomial with a capital letter for the species name.
- Saying classification is based on "what organisms look like". Modern classification is based on evolutionary relationships.
- Saying "the more groups two species share, the less related they are".

### 3.4.6 Biodiversity within a community

*Refs: AQA 3.4.6 · Edexcel A 4.1, 4.2, 4.3 · Edexcel B 3.2 ii, 3.3 i · OCR A 4.2.1(a) to (d) · OCR B 3.1.3(h), 4.3.1(l) · Eduqas C2.1(h) to (j). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Biodiversity can relate to a range of habitats, from a small local habitat to the Earth.
- Species richness is a measure of the number of different species in a community.
- An index of diversity describes the relationship between the number of species in a community and the number of individuals in each species. Calculation of an index of diversity (d) from d = N(N − 1) ÷ Σn(n − 1), where N = total number of organisms of all species and n = total number of organisms of each species.
- Farming techniques reduce biodiversity. The balance between conservation and farming.

#### Explained

**Levels of biodiversity** (OCR A, Edexcel B): habitat diversity (the number of different habitats in an area), species diversity (species richness and evenness) and genetic diversity (the variety of alleles within a species).

**Species richness** counts species but ignores how many of each. **Species evenness** describes how equal the numbers of each species are. Two woods may each have 10 species, but if one is 95% oak it is less diverse than one with all ten in similar numbers. An index of diversity combines both.

**Endemism** (Edexcel A): an endemic species is found naturally in only one place (such as the Galápagos giant tortoise). Areas with many endemic species are priorities for conservation.

**Niche** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B). An organism's niche is its role in its habitat: what it eats, what eats it, where and when it feeds, the conditions it tolerates. No two species occupy exactly the same niche in a habitat for long: one outcompetes the other. Adaptations fit organisms to their niche.

**Farming and biodiversity.** Farming reduces biodiversity by: clearing woodland and removing hedgerows; growing monocultures; using pesticides and herbicides; draining wetlands; overgrazing; using fertilisers that favour a few fast-growing species. Ways to balance farming and conservation: keep and replant hedgerows, leave field margins uncultivated, rotate crops, use integrated pest management, maintain ponds and wetlands, pay farmers through agri-environment schemes.

#### Worked example

A pond survey found: species A 10, B 5, C 3, D 2.

**AQA / Edexcel A index:**

1. N = 20. N(N − 1) = 20 × 19 = 380.
2. Σn(n − 1) = (10 × 9) + (5 × 4) + (3 × 2) + (2 × 1) = 90 + 20 + 6 + 2 = 118.
3. d = 380 ÷ 118 = 3.22.

A higher d means more diversity. The lowest possible value is 1 (only one species).

**OCR A / Eduqas Simpson's index:** D = 1 − Σ(n/N)².

1. (10/20)² = 0.25; (5/20)² = 0.0625; (3/20)² = 0.0225; (2/20)² = 0.01.
2. Σ = 0.345. D = 1 − 0.345 = 0.655.

D lies between 0 and 1; values nearer 1 mean higher diversity.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying species richness takes numbers of individuals into account.
- Using N² instead of N(N − 1) in the AQA formula.
- Comparing an AQA index with a Simpson's index; they are on different scales.

### 3.4.7 Investigating diversity

*Refs: AQA 3.4.7 · Edexcel A 4.2 · Edexcel B 3.1 iv, v · OCR A 4.2.1(b), (e) · OCR B 3.1.3(i) · Eduqas C2.1(e), (k), (l). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Genetic diversity within, or between, species can be made by comparing: the frequency of measurable or observable characteristics; the base sequence of DNA; the base sequence of mRNA; the amino acid sequence of the proteins encoded by DNA and mRNA.
- Interpret data relating to similarities and differences in the base sequences of DNA and in the amino acid sequences of proteins to suggest relationships between different organisms within a species and between species.
- Appreciate that gene technology has caused a change in the methods of investigating genetic diversity; inferring DNA differences from measurable or observable characteristics has been replaced by direct investigation of DNA sequences.
- Quantitative investigations of variation within a species involve: collecting data from random samples; calculating a mean value and the standard deviation of that mean; interpreting mean values and their standard deviations.

#### Explained

**Comparing species.** Closely related species share a recent common ancestor, so they have had less time for mutations to build up. Their DNA base sequences, and so their mRNA and amino acid sequences, are more similar. Count the differences: fewer differences mean a closer relationship.

Comparing observable characteristics was the old method. Its problems: many characteristics are controlled by many genes and affected by the environment, and similar features can evolve separately (convergent evolution). DNA sequencing is now fast and cheap, and compares genes directly.

**Random sampling** (all boards). To avoid bias, choose sample sites at random: lay out two tape measures at right angles and use random numbers as coordinates. Take a large sample to reduce the effect of chance. Non-random sampling (OCR A) includes systematic sampling along a transect (when there is a gradient), opportunistic sampling (quick but biased) and stratified sampling (sampling each area in proportion to its size). Field methods are in file 23.

**Mean and standard deviation.** The mean is the average. The standard deviation (SD) measures the spread of data around the mean: about 68% of values lie within 1 SD of the mean, about 95% within 2 SD. If two samples' means ± SD overlap a lot, the difference between them is probably due to chance. Formula and test in file 26.

**Genetic biodiversity within a species** (OCR A, OCR B). Proportion of polymorphic gene loci = number of polymorphic loci ÷ total number of loci tested. A locus is polymorphic if it has more than one allele in the population (commonly with the most common allele below 99% frequency). A higher proportion means more genetic diversity. Eduqas also lists DNA fingerprinting for comparing genetic diversity (file 25).

#### Worked example

The amino acid sequence of one protein was compared in four species. Differences from species W: X 2, Y 14, Z 9. Which species is most closely related to W?

X: fewest amino acid differences, so it shares the most recent common ancestor with W. Order of relatedness to W: X, then Z, then Y.

Genetic diversity: 40 gene loci were tested in a population; 13 were polymorphic. Proportion = 13 ÷ 40 = 0.325.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying that the same amino acid sequence means identical DNA. The code is degenerate, so DNA can differ.
- Saying a large standard deviation means "the data are wrong". It means more spread.
- Choosing sample sites "randomly" by walking about.

### Check questions

1. Explain why two species that produce hybrid offspring are still classed as separate species. (2 marks)
2. Give three roles of courtship behaviour. (3 marks)
3. Give the correct order of the taxa from domain to species. (1 mark)
4. Calculate the AQA index of diversity for a habitat with species counts of 6, 6, 4 and 4. (2 marks)
5. Calculate Simpson's index (1 − Σ(n/N)²) for the same habitat. (2 marks)
6. Explain why comparing DNA base sequences gives a more reliable picture of relationships than comparing physical features. (3 marks)
7. Describe the evidence that led to the three-domain system. (2 marks)
8. Explain two ways farming reduces biodiversity and one way farmers can increase it. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Hybrid offspring are infertile (1); species definition requires fertile offspring (1).
2. Any three: recognise same species; identify a fertile / receptive mate; form a pair bond; synchronise mating; stimulate release of gametes.
3. Domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus, species (1).
4. N = 20; N(N − 1) = 380; Σn(n − 1) = 30 + 30 + 12 + 12 = 84 (1); d = 380 ÷ 84 = 4.52 (1).
5. Σ(n/N)² = 0.09 + 0.09 + 0.04 + 0.04 = 0.26 (1); D = 0.74 (1).
6. Physical features may be affected by environment / polygenic (1); similar features may evolve in unrelated species / convergent evolution (1); DNA differences reflect mutations accumulated since common ancestor / direct comparison of genes (1).
7. Ribosomal RNA sequences / membrane lipids / RNA polymerase differ (1); archaea as different from bacteria as from eukaryotes, so prokaryotes split into two domains (1).
8. Any two (1 each): hedgerow removal; monoculture; pesticides / herbicides; draining wetlands; fertilisers. One (1): keep hedgerows; field margins; crop rotation; integrated pest management; ponds.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/15-photosynthesis.md -->

## Photosynthesis

Photosynthesis traps light energy and stores it in organic molecules. This file covers the light-dependent and light-independent reactions, limiting factors, and AQA required practicals 7 and 8.

### Overview

A-level only. ATP and ATP synthase are introduced in file 03; chemiosmosis is explained alongside respiration in file 16. Energy flow through ecosystems is in file 17. Uses of mineral ions such as magnesium and nitrate (Eduqas C1.2(l)) are in file 41. Crop yields and pest control (OCR B) are in file 42.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.1, RP7, RP8. Edexcel A 5.5 to 5.9, CP11. Edexcel B 5.6, 5.7, CP10, CP11. OCR A 5.2.1(a) to (g). OCR B 4.3.1(a) to (f). Eduqas C1.1(b), C1.2(a) to (k).
- AQA calls the products glycerate 3-phosphate (GP) and triose phosphate (TP). Edexcel A and Edexcel B call TP glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate (GALP). Same molecule.
- Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for absorption and action spectra and for photosystems I and II by name. OCR A asks for accessory pigments.
- Edexcel A CP11 and AQA RP8 both use the reduction of a blue dye by chloroplasts (the Hill reaction, DCPIP).
- Edexcel B CP10 asks for the effect of light wavelength on photosynthesis.

### 3.5.1 Photosynthesis

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1, RP7, RP8 · Edexcel A 5.5 to 5.9, CP11 · Edexcel B 5.6 i, ii, 5.7 i to viii, CP10, CP11 · OCR A 5.2.1(a) to (g) · OCR B 4.3.1(a) to (g) · Eduqas C1.1(b), C1.2(a) to (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The light-dependent reaction in such detail as to show that: chlorophyll absorbs light, leading to photoionisation of chlorophyll; some of the energy from electrons released during photoionisation is conserved in the production of ATP and reduced NADP; the production of ATP involves electron transfer associated with the electron transfer chain and passage of protons across chloroplast membranes and is catalysed by ATP synthase embedded in these membranes (chemiosmotic theory); photolysis of water produces protons, electrons and oxygen.
- The light-independent reaction uses reduced NADP from the light-dependent reaction to form a simple sugar. The hydrolysis of ATP, also from the light-dependent reaction, provides the additional energy for this reaction.
- The light-independent reaction in such detail as to show that: carbon dioxide reacts with ribulose bisphosphate (RuBP) to form two molecules of glycerate 3-phosphate (GP), catalysed by the enzyme rubisco; ATP and reduced NADP from the light-dependent reaction are used to reduce GP to triose phosphate; some of the triose phosphate is used to regenerate RuBP in the Calvin cycle; some of the triose phosphate is converted to useful organic substances.
- Identify environmental factors that limit the rate of photosynthesis; evaluate data relating to common agricultural practices used to overcome the effect of these limiting factors.

#### Explained

**Overall equation:** 6CO₂ + 6H₂O → C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ (using light energy, in chlorophyll).

**The chloroplast.**

- Double membrane (envelope).
- Thylakoids: flattened membrane sacs stacked into grana, joined by intergranal lamellae. Thylakoid membranes hold chlorophyll and other pigments in photosystems, electron carriers and ATP synthase. Site of the light-dependent reaction. Many grana give a large surface area.
- Stroma: fluid around the thylakoids with the enzymes of the Calvin cycle (including rubisco), starch grains, lipid droplets, circular DNA and 70S ribosomes. Site of the light-independent reaction.
- (Eduqas) Chloroplasts are most numerous in palisade cells near the upper surface and can move within the cell towards light. They act as transducers, turning light energy into chemical energy.

**Pigments** (OCR A, Edexcel B, Eduqas). Chlorophyll a and b absorb red and blue-violet light and reflect green. Accessory pigments (carotenoids, xanthophylls) absorb other wavelengths and pass energy to chlorophyll a, widening the range of light used. Pigments are grouped in light-harvesting systems (antenna complexes) around a reaction centre of chlorophyll a. There are two photosystems: PSI (reaction centre absorbs best at 700 nm) and PSII (680 nm).

- *Absorption spectrum:* how much light each pigment absorbs at each wavelength.
- *Action spectrum:* the rate of photosynthesis at each wavelength.

The two match closely, which is evidence that these pigments drive photosynthesis.

**The light-dependent reaction** (thylakoid membranes).

1. Light hits PSII. Energy passes to the chlorophyll in the reaction centre, exciting electrons to a higher energy level so they leave the chlorophyll: **photoionisation**.
2. The electrons pass along an electron transfer chain of carriers in the thylakoid membrane, losing energy at each step.
3. This energy is used to pump protons (H⁺) from the stroma into the thylakoid space, building up a high concentration.
4. Protons diffuse back into the stroma through ATP synthase, which uses the energy to join ADP and Pi: **photophosphorylation**. This is the chemiosmotic theory.
5. The electrons reach PSI, are re-excited by light, and then reduce NADP: NADP + 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → reduced NADP.
6. PSII must replace its lost electrons. These come from **photolysis** of water: 2H₂O → 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ + O₂. Oxygen is a by-product and diffuses out of the leaf; the protons add to the gradient and are used to reduce NADP.

*Non-cyclic photophosphorylation* uses both photosystems and makes ATP, reduced NADP and oxygen (as above). *Cyclic photophosphorylation* (OCR A, Edexcel B, Eduqas) uses PSI only: excited electrons return to PSI via the electron chain, so ATP is made but no reduced NADP and no oxygen. It tops up the ATP needed for the Calvin cycle.

**The light-independent reaction (Calvin cycle)** (stroma).

1. CO₂ combines with ribulose bisphosphate (RuBP, 5C), catalysed by rubisco. The unstable 6C product immediately splits into two molecules of glycerate 3-phosphate (GP, 3C). This is carbon fixation.
2. GP is reduced to triose phosphate (TP, 3C) using reduced NADP (supplies hydrogen) and ATP (supplies energy).
3. Of every 6 TP made, 5 are used to regenerate 3 RuBP, using more ATP. 1 TP leaves the cycle.

Six turns of the cycle fix 6 CO₂ and make one hexose (from 2 TP).

**Uses of TP** (all boards). Two TP join to make glucose, which can make sucrose, starch and cellulose. TP makes glycerol; GP makes fatty acids; with these, lipids. GP plus nitrogen (from nitrate) makes amino acids. TP also makes nucleotides.

**Limiting factors.** At any moment, the rate is limited by the factor in shortest supply: light intensity, CO₂ concentration or temperature. Increasing a limiting factor raises the rate until another factor becomes limiting.

| Factor | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Light intensity | Low light: less ATP and reduced NADP made, so GP is not reduced to TP; GP rises, TP and RuBP fall |
| CO₂ concentration | Low CO₂: less RuBP is carboxylated; RuBP rises, GP and TP fall |
| Temperature | The light-independent reaction is controlled by enzymes, so rate rises with temperature to an optimum; above it rubisco and other enzymes denature, and stomata may close to save water |
| Water (OCR A) | Water stress closes stomata, reducing CO₂ uptake |

**Agricultural practice.** Glasshouse growers raise CO₂ (by burning fuel, which also warms the air), use artificial lighting, and heat the glasshouse. The extra cost must be balanced against the extra yield: adding more of a factor that is not limiting just wastes money.

**Respiration and photosynthesis** (OCR A, OCR B). Plants respire all the time. At the light compensation point, the rate of photosynthesis equals the rate of respiration, so there is no net gas exchange and no net gain in biomass. The products of each are the raw materials for the other. (OCR B 4.3.1(g)) For crop production, plants must spend as long as possible above the compensation point: growers extend the hours of light and raise light intensity and CO₂ in glasshouses, but heating at night raises respiration and lowers the net gain, so night temperatures are kept low.

#### Worked example

1. In a Calvin cycle experiment, the light was switched off. Predict and explain the changes in GP and RuBP.

GP rises: it is still being made from RuBP and CO₂, but without ATP and reduced NADP it cannot be reduced to TP. RuBP falls: it is still being used, but it cannot be regenerated from TP without ATP.

2. In chromatography of leaf pigments, a spot moved 4.2 cm; the solvent moved 9.6 cm. Rf = 4.2 ÷ 9.6 = 0.44. Compare with known values to identify the pigment.

3. A plant fixes 12 molecules of CO₂. How many molecules of glucose can it make, and how many turns of the cycle are needed? 12 turns; 12 ÷ 6 = 2 glucose.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the light-independent reaction "happens in the dark". It happens in light too, and stops soon after dark because ATP and reduced NADP run out.
- Saying oxygen comes from CO₂. It comes from water.
- Saying chlorophyll "absorbs green light".
- Saying ATP is used to reduce GP. Reduced NADP reduces; ATP provides energy.
- Saying rubisco makes GP from TP.
- Saying temperature affects the light-dependent reaction most.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 7** (also Edexcel B CP11, OCR A 5.2.1(c) and PAG6): use of chromatography to investigate the pigments isolated from leaves of different plants.

1. Grind leaves with a little sand and propanone (or another solvent) to extract pigments.
2. Draw a pencil line (origin) about 1.5 cm from the bottom of the chromatography paper or TLC plate.
3. Spot the extract onto the origin with a capillary tube, letting each spot dry, many times, to get a small, concentrated spot.
4. Stand the paper in a little solvent, with the solvent below the origin. Cover.
5. Remove when the solvent nearly reaches the top; mark the solvent front.
6. Calculate Rf values; compare plants (for example shade and sun leaves, or green and red leaves).

Pencil, not ink (ink would dissolve and run). Solvent below the origin (so spots do not dissolve into the solvent). Risks: propanone is flammable and an irritant; work in a ventilated room, no flames.

**AQA required practical 8** (also Edexcel A CP11): investigation into the effect of a named factor on the rate of dehydrogenase activity in extracts of chloroplasts.

1. Grind leaves in ice-cold, isotonic buffer; filter; centrifuge to get a suspension of chloroplasts. Keep on ice.
2. Add DCPIP (blue) to the chloroplast suspension. DCPIP accepts electrons from the light-dependent reaction in place of NADP and turns colourless.
3. Shine light on the tubes; measure the time for the blue colour to go, or record absorbance with a colorimeter at intervals.
4. Vary one factor: light intensity (distance from lamp), light colour (filters), temperature, or an inhibitor.
5. Controls: a tube kept in the dark (no colour change shows light is needed); a tube with no chloroplasts (no change shows chloroplasts are needed).

Why cold, isotonic and buffered: as in cell fractionation (file 04) — cold slows enzymes that damage chloroplasts; isotonic stops osmosis damaging them; buffer keeps the pH stable.

**Rate of photosynthesis with pondweed** (Edexcel B CP10, OCR A PAG11, OCR B 4.3.1(d)). Count oxygen bubbles or collect gas in a photosynthometer from *Elodea* or *Cabomba* in sodium hydrogencarbonate solution (supplies CO₂). Vary light intensity (light intensity ∝ 1/d²) or use colour filters for wavelength. Keep temperature constant with a water bath or a heat shield.

### Check questions

1. Describe the role of photolysis in the light-dependent reaction. (3 marks)
2. Explain how ATP is made in the light-dependent reaction. (4 marks)
3. Describe the Calvin cycle. (5 marks)
4. Explain what happens to the concentrations of GP and RuBP when the CO₂ concentration falls. (3 marks)
5. Explain how a glasshouse grower could use knowledge of limiting factors to increase profit. (3 marks)
6. Give two differences between cyclic and non-cyclic photophosphorylation. (2 marks)
7. In RP8, explain why a tube with chloroplasts kept in the dark is included. (2 marks)
8. A lamp 20 cm from pondweed is moved to 10 cm. By what factor does light intensity change? (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. Water split using light energy into protons, electrons and oxygen (1); electrons replace those lost from chlorophyll / PSII (1); protons used to reduce NADP / add to proton gradient; oxygen released as by-product (1).
2. Light causes photoionisation; electrons pass along electron transfer chain (1); energy used to pump protons into thylakoid space (1); protons diffuse back through ATP synthase (1); ADP + Pi → ATP (1).
3. CO₂ combines with RuBP (1); catalysed by rubisco (1); forms two GP (1); GP reduced to TP using reduced NADP and ATP (1); most TP regenerates RuBP using ATP; some used to make glucose / organic substances (1).
4. Less CO₂ to combine with RuBP (1); RuBP rises, as still regenerated but used less (1); GP falls as less made but still converted to TP (1).
5. Identify limiting factor (e.g. CO₂ in winter) and increase it (1); only increase factor up to point where another becomes limiting (1); compare cost of input with value of extra yield (1).
6. Any two: cyclic uses PSI only; cyclic produces no reduced NADP; cyclic produces no oxygen / no photolysis.
7. Shows DCPIP reduction / colour change needs light (1); so any change in other tubes is due to light-dependent reaction (1).
8. Intensity ∝ 1/d²; halving distance gives 4 times the intensity (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/16-respiration.md -->

## Respiration

Respiration releases energy from organic molecules to make ATP. This file covers glycolysis, anaerobic respiration, the link reaction, the Krebs cycle, oxidative phosphorylation, other respiratory substrates, the respiratory quotient, and AQA required practical 9.

### Overview

A-level only. ATP's structure is in file 03. Lactate, oxygen debt and exercise (Edexcel A, OCR B) are in file 40.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.2, RP9. Edexcel A 7.3 to 7.7, CP16. Edexcel B 5.1 to 5.5, CP9. OCR A 5.2.2(a) to (l). OCR B 4.1.1(a) to (g). Eduqas C1.1(a) to (d), C1.3(a) to (f).
- OCR A and OCR B ask for the respiratory quotient (RQ) and the relative energy values of carbohydrate, lipid and protein. Eduqas asks how lipids and amino acids enter respiration. AQA asks only that lipids and proteins can be respiratory substrates.
- OCR A asks for the structure of the mitochondrion and the roles of coenzymes NAD, FAD and coenzyme A by name.
- Eduqas C1.1 compares the membranes of mitochondria and chloroplasts in ATP synthesis.
- Edexcel A and Edexcel B name triose phosphate as GALP, and Edexcel A uses the name "acetyl coenzyme A".

### 3.5.2 Respiration

*Refs: AQA 3.5.2, RP9 · Edexcel A 7.3 to 7.7, CP16 · Edexcel B 5.1 to 5.5, CP9 · OCR A 5.2.2(a) to (l) · OCR B 4.1.1(a) to (g) · Eduqas C1.1(a) to (d), C1.3(a) to (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Respiration produces ATP.
- Glycolysis is the first stage of anaerobic and aerobic respiration. It occurs in the cytoplasm and is an anaerobic process: phosphorylation of glucose to glucose phosphate, using ATP; production of triose phosphate; oxidation of triose phosphate to pyruvate with a net gain of ATP and reduced NAD.
- If respiration is only anaerobic, pyruvate can be converted to ethanol or lactate using reduced NAD. The oxidised NAD produced in this way can be used in further glycolysis.
- If respiration is aerobic, pyruvate from glycolysis enters the mitochondrial matrix by active transport. Pyruvate is oxidised to acetate, with the production of reduced NAD; acetate combines with coenzyme A in the link reaction to produce acetylcoenzyme A; acetylcoenzyme A reacts with a four-carbon molecule, releasing coenzyme A and producing a six-carbon molecule that enters the Krebs cycle; in a series of oxidation-reduction reactions, the Krebs cycle generates reduced coenzymes and ATP by substrate-level phosphorylation, and carbon dioxide is lost.
- Synthesis of ATP by oxidative phosphorylation is associated with the transfer of electrons down the electron transfer chain and passage of protons across inner mitochondrial membranes, and is catalysed by ATP synthase embedded in these membranes (chemiosmotic theory).
- Other respiratory substrates include the breakdown products of lipids and amino acids, which enter the Krebs cycle.

#### Explained

**The mitochondrion** (OCR A, Edexcel B). Outer membrane; inner membrane folded into cristae (large surface area for the electron transfer chain and ATP synthase); intermembrane space (where protons build up); matrix containing Krebs cycle and link reaction enzymes, mitochondrial DNA and ribosomes.

**Coenzymes.** NAD and FAD carry hydrogen (protons and electrons) from the earlier stages to the electron transfer chain. Coenzyme A carries the acetyl group into the Krebs cycle.

**Glycolysis** (cytoplasm; no oxygen needed).

1. Glucose is phosphorylated twice using 2 ATP, forming hexose bisphosphate. This makes it more reactive and traps it in the cell.
2. It splits into two triose phosphate (TP) molecules.
3. Each TP is oxidised (hydrogen removed) to pyruvate. The hydrogen reduces NAD; 2 ATP are made per TP by substrate-level phosphorylation.

Per glucose: 4 ATP made − 2 ATP used = **net 2 ATP**, plus **2 reduced NAD** and **2 pyruvate**.

**Anaerobic respiration.** Without oxygen, the electron transfer chain stops, so reduced NAD cannot be reoxidised there. Glycolysis would stop when NAD ran out. Instead pyruvate accepts the hydrogen from reduced NAD, regenerating NAD so glycolysis continues and makes 2 ATP per glucose.

- Animals (and some bacteria): pyruvate + reduced NAD → lactate + NAD (lactate dehydrogenase). Lactate is carried to the liver, where it is converted back to pyruvate or glucose when oxygen is available.
- Plants and yeast: pyruvate → ethanal + CO₂; ethanal + reduced NAD → ethanol + NAD. This cannot be reversed.

Only 2 ATP per glucose are made anaerobically, against about 32 aerobically, because most of the energy is still in lactate or ethanol.

**The link reaction** (matrix). Pyruvate is actively transported into the matrix.

1. Pyruvate (3C) is decarboxylated (CO₂ removed) and oxidised (hydrogen removed, reducing NAD), forming acetate (2C).
2. Acetate combines with coenzyme A to form acetylcoenzyme A.

Per pyruvate: 1 CO₂, 1 reduced NAD. Per glucose: double.

**The Krebs cycle** (matrix).

1. Acetylcoenzyme A (2C) combines with a 4C molecule (oxaloacetate) to form a 6C molecule (citrate); coenzyme A is released to collect more acetate.
2. The 6C molecule is decarboxylated and oxidised to a 5C molecule, then to the 4C molecule, which is regenerated.
3. Per turn: 2 CO₂, 3 reduced NAD, 1 reduced FAD and 1 ATP (by substrate-level phosphorylation).

The cycle turns twice per glucose.

**Oxidative phosphorylation** (inner mitochondrial membrane, the cristae).

1. Reduced NAD and reduced FAD release hydrogen atoms, which split into protons and electrons.
2. Electrons pass along the electron transfer chain, a series of carrier proteins, in a series of oxidation-reduction reactions, losing energy.
3. The energy is used to pump protons from the matrix into the intermembrane space, creating an electrochemical gradient.
4. Protons diffuse back into the matrix through ATP synthase, which makes ATP from ADP and Pi (chemiosmosis).
5. At the end of the chain, oxygen is the final electron acceptor: it combines with electrons and protons to form water.

Without oxygen, electrons cannot leave the chain, so the whole process stops, and NAD and FAD stay reduced, stopping the Krebs cycle and link reaction too.

Reduced NAD gives about 2.5 ATP; reduced FAD about 1.5 (FAD's electrons enter the chain further along, so fewer protons are pumped).

**ATP tally per glucose (approximate).**

| Stage | ATP directly | Reduced NAD | Reduced FAD |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Glycolysis | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| Link reaction | 0 | 2 | 0 |
| Krebs cycle | 2 | 6 | 2 |
| Total | 4 | 10 | 2 |

From oxidative phosphorylation: 10 × 2.5 + 2 × 1.5 = 28. Total about 32. (Older texts give 38; the real yield is lower because some energy is used to move pyruvate and ADP into the mitochondrion and protons leak through the membrane.)

**Comparing mitochondria and chloroplasts** (Eduqas C1.1). Both make ATP by chemiosmosis: an electron transfer chain in a membrane pumps protons across it, and protons flow back through ATP synthase. In mitochondria protons are pumped into the intermembrane space; in chloroplasts into the thylakoid space. The energy source differs: excited electrons from light in chloroplasts; electrons from reduced NAD and FAD in mitochondria.

**Other respiratory substrates.**

- *Lipids:* triglycerides are hydrolysed to glycerol and fatty acids. Glycerol is phosphorylated and converted to triose phosphate, which enters glycolysis. Fatty acids are split into many 2C fragments, which become acetylcoenzyme A and enter the Krebs cycle, producing a lot of reduced NAD and FAD.
- *Proteins:* hydrolysed to amino acids; the amino group is removed in the liver (deamination, file 30) and the rest enters the Krebs cycle or link reaction as pyruvate, acetylcoenzyme A or a Krebs intermediate.

**Energy values** (OCR A, OCR B). Lipids about 39 kJ g⁻¹; proteins about 17 kJ g⁻¹; carbohydrates about 16 kJ g⁻¹. Lipids yield the most because they contain the most hydrogen atoms per gram, so they supply the most protons and electrons for oxidative phosphorylation.

**Respiratory quotient** (OCR A, OCR B). RQ = CO₂ produced ÷ O₂ used. Carbohydrate 1.0; protein about 0.9; lipid about 0.7. An RQ above 1 suggests some anaerobic respiration (CO₂ made without oxygen use).

#### Worked example

1. Calculate the RQ for oleic acid: C₁₈H₃₄O₂ + 25.5O₂ → 18CO₂ + 17H₂O.

RQ = 18 ÷ 25.5 = 0.71.

2. In a respirometer, the coloured liquid in the capillary moved 30 mm in 10 minutes towards the organism. The capillary has a radius of 0.5 mm. Calculate the rate of oxygen uptake in mm³ min⁻¹.

Volume = π × 0.5² × 30 = 23.6 mm³. Rate = 23.6 ÷ 10 = 2.4 mm³ min⁻¹.

3. How many molecules of CO₂ are produced from one glucose in aerobic respiration, and where? 2 in the link reaction, 4 in the Krebs cycle: 6 in total.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying glycolysis happens in the mitochondria.
- Saying anaerobic respiration "produces lactate to make ATP". Lactate is made to regenerate NAD so glycolysis can continue.
- Saying oxygen "makes ATP" or "combines with ADP". Oxygen is the final electron acceptor.
- Saying the Krebs cycle produces most of the ATP. Oxidative phosphorylation does.
- Saying "energy is made". Energy is released or transferred.
- Saying protons are pumped into the matrix.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 9** (also Edexcel A CP16, Edexcel B CP9, OCR A 5.2.2(i), (l) and PAG10/11, OCR B 4.1.1(g)): investigation into the effect of a named variable on the rate of respiration of cultures of single-celled organisms.

*Method 1: respirometer.* Put small organisms (yeast culture, germinating seeds or woodlice) in one tube with soda lime (or potassium hydroxide) to absorb CO₂. A second tube has glass beads of the same volume (control, to account for temperature and pressure changes). As oxygen is used, the pressure in the tube falls and the coloured liquid moves towards the organisms. Measure the distance moved in a set time and convert to volume. Change temperature with a water bath.

*Method 2: redox indicator.* Methylene blue (or TTC) accepts hydrogen from dehydrogenase enzymes in respiring yeast and turns colourless (TTC turns red). Time how long it takes at each temperature or glucose concentration.

*Method 3: CO₂ production.* Count bubbles or measure gas collected from yeast in glucose solution; or measure pH change with a datalogger.

Control variables: volume and concentration of yeast; glucose concentration; temperature (water bath); time allowed to equilibrate. Ethics: invertebrates must be returned unharmed; keep them away from soda lime (corrosive).

### Check questions

1. Describe glycolysis. (4 marks)
2. Explain why anaerobic respiration in mammals produces lactate. (3 marks)
3. Describe the link reaction. (3 marks)
4. Explain how the electron transfer chain leads to ATP production. (5 marks)
5. Explain why the Krebs cycle stops if oxygen is unavailable. (3 marks)
6. Explain why the energy yield per gram of lipid is higher than that of carbohydrate. (2 marks)
7. Calculate the RQ for glucose: C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ → 6CO₂ + 6H₂O, and state what an RQ of 0.7 suggests. (2 marks)
8. In a respirometer, explain the purpose of the soda lime and the control tube. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Glucose phosphorylated using 2 ATP (1); splits into two triose phosphate (1); triose phosphate oxidised to pyruvate, reducing NAD (1); 4 ATP made, net gain of 2 ATP (1).
2. No oxygen to accept electrons, so electron transfer chain stops / NAD not regenerated (1); pyruvate reduced to lactate using reduced NAD (1); NAD regenerated so glycolysis continues / some ATP made (1).
3. Pyruvate decarboxylated / CO₂ removed (1); oxidised, reducing NAD (1); acetate combines with coenzyme A to form acetylcoenzyme A (1).
4. Reduced NAD / FAD release hydrogen / electrons (1); electrons pass along carriers losing energy (1); energy used to pump protons into intermembrane space (1); protons diffuse back through ATP synthase (1); oxygen is final electron acceptor forming water (1).
5. Oxygen is final electron acceptor; without it electrons cannot leave the chain (1); reduced NAD / FAD not oxidised (1); no NAD / FAD for Krebs cycle (1).
6. More hydrogen atoms per gram (1); more protons / electrons for oxidative phosphorylation, so more ATP (1).
7. 6 ÷ 6 = 1.0 (1); 0.7 suggests lipid being respired (1).
8. Soda lime absorbs CO₂ so change in volume / pressure is due to oxygen uptake only (1); control tube with beads / no organisms (1); accounts for changes in temperature / pressure (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/17-energy-in-ecosystems-and-nutrient-cycles.md -->

## Energy in ecosystems and nutrient cycles

Energy flows through ecosystems and is lost at each step; nutrients are recycled. This file covers productivity, efficiency of energy transfer, ecological pyramids, and the nitrogen, phosphorus and carbon cycles, with fertilisers and eutrophication.

### Overview

A-level only. Populations, succession and sampling are in file 23. Climate change and carbon management (Edexcel A, Eduqas) are in file 42. Decomposition after death (Edexcel A forensics) is in file 43.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.3, 3.5.4. Edexcel A 5.10, 5.11, 5.21, 6.2. Edexcel B 10.1 ii, iii, 10.2 i to iii. OCR A 6.3.1(b), (c). OCR B 4.3.1(h) to (k). Eduqas C1.5(f), (h), (k), (l), (n) to (p).
- OCR A, Edexcel A and Eduqas ask for the carbon cycle; AQA does not. Included below.
- AQA alone asks for the phosphorus cycle and mycorrhizae.
- Edexcel B asks for pyramids of numbers, biomass and energy (10.1 iii).
- Eduqas asks about ploughing and drainage (C1.5(o)).

### 3.5.3 Energy and ecosystems

*Refs: AQA 3.5.3 · Edexcel A 5.10, 5.11 · Edexcel B 10.1 ii, iii, 10.2 i, ii · OCR A 6.3.1(b) · OCR B 4.3.1(i) to (k) · Eduqas C1.5(f), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- In any ecosystem, plants synthesise organic compounds from atmospheric, or aquatic, carbon dioxide.
- Most of the sugars synthesised by plants are used by the plant as respiratory substrates. The rest are used to make other groups of biological molecules. These biological molecules form the biomass of the plants.
- Biomass can be measured in terms of mass of carbon or dry mass of tissue per given area.
- The chemical energy store in dry biomass can be estimated using calorimetry.
- Gross primary production (GPP) is the chemical energy store in plant biomass, in a given area or volume. Net primary production (NPP) is the chemical energy store in plant biomass after respiratory losses to the environment have been taken into account: NPP = GPP − R.
- This net primary production is available for plant growth and reproduction. It is also available to other trophic levels in the ecosystem, such as herbivores and decomposers.
- The net production of consumers (N), such as animals, can be calculated as N = I − (F + R), where I represents the chemical energy store in ingested food, F the chemical energy lost to the environment in faeces and urine and R the respiratory losses.
- Primary and secondary productivity is the rate of primary or secondary production, measured as biomass in a given area in a given time, eg kJ ha⁻¹ year⁻¹.
- The ways in which production is affected by farming practices designed to increase the efficiency of energy transfer by simplifying food webs to reduce energy losses to non-human food chains, and by reducing respiratory losses within a human food chain.

#### Explained

**Trophic levels.** Producers (plants, algae) → primary consumers (herbivores) → secondary consumers → tertiary consumers. Decomposers and detritivores feed on dead material from all levels.

**Biomass.** Fresh mass varies with water content, so dry mass is used. Measuring dry mass means killing the organism and drying it in an oven at about 80 °C until the mass stops changing. Usually a small sample is dried and the result scaled up.

**Calorimetry.** A known dry mass is burned in a bomb calorimeter surrounded by a known volume of water. The temperature rise of the water gives the energy released: energy = mass of water × 4.18 J g⁻¹ °C⁻¹ × temperature rise.

**Why only a small fraction of light becomes plant biomass.**

- Most sunlight is reflected, passes through leaves, or is the wrong wavelength.
- Light may not hit a chlorophyll molecule.
- Other factors (CO₂, temperature) limit photosynthesis.
- Energy is lost as heat in the reactions.

Typically 1 to 3% of light energy falling on a plant ends up as GPP.

**Why transfer between consumers is inefficient** (often about 10%, though it varies):

- Not all of the organism is eaten (roots, bones).
- Some is indigestible and lost in faeces.
- Energy is lost in urine (as urea).
- Most energy absorbed is used in respiration and lost as heat, especially by mammals and birds keeping a constant body temperature.

Because so much is lost at each step, food chains rarely have more than four or five levels.

**Ecological pyramids** (Edexcel B).

| Pyramid | Shows | Problems |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Numbers | Number of organisms at each level | Size ignored: one oak supports thousands of insects, so it can be inverted |
| Biomass | Dry mass per area at each level, at one time | Seasonal change; organisms killed to measure; can be inverted in oceans, where phytoplankton reproduce fast but have little standing biomass |
| Energy | Energy passing through each level per area per year | Hard to measure, but always the right way up and allows comparison of ecosystems |

**Farming practices.**

- *Simplifying food webs:* removing other species that would take energy from the crop. Herbicides remove weeds (competitors for light, water, ions); pesticides and fungicides remove pests that eat the crop. More of the NPP goes to the crop.
- *Reducing respiratory losses in livestock:* keep animals indoors in warm conditions (less energy for heat), restrict movement (less energy for muscle contraction), feed high-energy, easily digested food, slaughter young (young animals put more of their energy into growth). More of the food becomes meat. Ethical concerns: animal welfare, antibiotic overuse.

**Ruminants in the human food chain** (OCR B 4.3.1(k)). Cattle and sheep (file 45) can turn grass and other cellulose-rich plants, which humans cannot digest, into meat and milk, so they make food from land unsuitable for crops. But energy transfer from plants to cattle is inefficient (often less than 10%), so more people can be fed per hectare by eating crops directly; ruminants also release methane, a greenhouse gas (file 42).

#### Worked example

1. Grassland GPP = 21 000 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹; plant respiration = 12 500 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹. NPP = 21 000 − 12 500 = 8500 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹.

2. A cow ingests 3000 kJ per day; 1800 kJ is lost in faeces and urine and 1050 kJ in respiration. N = 3000 − (1800 + 1050) = 150 kJ per day. Efficiency of conversion of ingested food to biomass = 150 ÷ 3000 × 100 = 5%.

3. Efficiency of transfer between two trophic levels = (energy in the higher level ÷ energy in the lower level) × 100. If producers have NPP 8500 and primary consumers 900 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹: 900 ÷ 8500 × 100 = 10.6%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying energy is "used up" or "lost" as if destroyed. It is transferred to the environment as heat.
- Saying energy is recycled.
- Using fresh mass for biomass.
- Forgetting units for productivity (energy per area per time).

### 3.5.4 Nutrient cycles

*Refs: AQA 3.5.4 · Edexcel A 5.21, 6.2 · Edexcel B 10.2 iii · OCR A 6.3.1(c) · OCR B 4.3.1(h) · Eduqas C1.5(k), (l), (n) to (p). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Nutrients are recycled within natural ecosystems, exemplified by the nitrogen cycle and the phosphorus cycle. Microorganisms play a vital role in recycling chemical elements such as phosphorus and nitrogen.
- The role of saprobionts in decomposition. The role of mycorrhizae in facilitating the uptake of water and inorganic ions by plants. The role of bacteria in the nitrogen cycle in sufficient detail to illustrate the processes of saprobiotic nutrition, ammonification, nitrification, nitrogen fixation and denitrification. (Names of individual species are not required.)
- The main stages of the phosphorus cycle, limited to the roles of: saprobionts in decomposition; mycorrhizae in facilitating uptake of phosphate ions by plants; the movement of phosphate ions into ecosystems from rock, and back into rock in sediments.
- The use of natural and artificial fertilisers to replace the nitrates and phosphates lost by harvesting plants and removing livestock.
- The environmental issues arising from the use of fertilisers including leaching and eutrophication.

#### Explained

**Saprobionts** (saprotrophs: decomposing bacteria and fungi) secrete enzymes onto dead organisms and waste, digest them externally, and absorb the soluble products. This releases inorganic ions such as ammonium and phosphate back into the soil.

**The nitrogen cycle.** Plants need nitrogen for amino acids, proteins, nucleotides and chlorophyll, but cannot use nitrogen gas (N₂).

| Process | What happens | Organisms |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nitrogen fixation | N₂ → ammonia (NH₃), using the enzyme nitrogenase; needs ATP and anaerobic conditions | Free-living bacteria (*Azotobacter*) and bacteria in root nodules of legumes (*Rhizobium*, which gets sugars from the plant: mutualism). Lightning also fixes some. |
| Ammonification | Nitrogen in proteins, urea and nucleic acids of dead organisms and waste → ammonium ions (NH₄⁺) | Saprobionts |
| Nitrification | NH₄⁺ → nitrite (NO₂⁻) → nitrate (NO₃⁻); oxidation reactions that release energy for the bacteria; needs oxygen | Nitrifying bacteria (*Nitrosomonas*, then *Nitrobacter*) |
| Assimilation | Plants absorb nitrate by active transport and use it to make amino acids | Plants |
| Denitrification | NO₃⁻ → N₂; happens in waterlogged, anaerobic soils | Denitrifying bacteria |

AQA does not require the names of species; the genus names above are given as common examples.

Farmers improve soil nitrogen by ploughing (aerates soil, so nitrification is favoured and denitrification reduced) and draining waterlogged fields (Eduqas C1.5(o)). Ploughing can also increase erosion and the loss of organic matter.

**Studying *Rhizobium*** (OCR B 4.3.1(h)). Cut open the root nodules of a legume (clover, pea): a pink colour shows leghaemoglobin, which binds oxygen so that nitrogenase (which oxygen inactivates) can work. *Rhizobium* can be cultured from a crushed, surface-sterilised nodule on a yeast-mannitol agar plate using aseptic technique (file 28). Legumes are used in crop rotation because their nodules add nitrogen compounds to the soil.

**Mycorrhizae.** Associations between fungi and plant roots. The fungal hyphae spread through the soil, greatly increasing the surface area for absorbing water and ions, especially phosphate. They also store water. The fungus receives sugars from the plant. This is a mutualistic relationship.

**The phosphorus cycle.** Phosphorus is needed for ATP, nucleic acids and phospholipids. It has no gas phase.

1. Phosphate ions are released from rocks by weathering and dissolve in water.
2. Plants absorb phosphate (helped by mycorrhizae); animals get it by eating plants.
3. Saprobionts release phosphate from dead organisms and waste (also guano from seabirds).
4. Phosphate washed into lakes and seas settles into sediments, which over geological time form new rock, later uplifted.

The cycle is slow, so phosphate is often a limiting nutrient.

**The carbon cycle** (OCR A, Edexcel A, Eduqas).

- CO₂ is removed from the air and water by photosynthesis, and dissolves in the oceans (some forming carbonate shells and rocks).
- Carbon passes through food chains as organic compounds.
- CO₂ returns by respiration of plants, animals and decomposers.
- Dead material that does not decompose (waterlogged, acidic or cold conditions) forms peat and, over millions of years, fossil fuels.
- Combustion of fossil fuels and wood releases CO₂.
- Deforestation reduces photosynthesis and, if trees are burned, releases CO₂.

Carbon sinks store carbon for long periods (oceans, forests, peat bogs). Edexcel A 5.21 asks how knowing the carbon cycle helps reduce atmospheric CO₂ (file 42).

**Fertilisers.** Harvesting crops and removing livestock takes nitrates and phosphates away, so they are not returned to the soil by decomposition. Fertilisers replace them.

- *Natural (organic):* manure, compost, sewage sludge. Release ions slowly as they decompose; add organic matter that improves soil structure; cheaper on farms with animals, but the exact ion content is unknown.
- *Artificial (inorganic):* mined or manufactured salts of nitrate, phosphate and potassium. Exact amounts are known; ions immediately available; but soluble, so easily leached.

**Leaching and eutrophication.**

1. Rain washes soluble nitrates (and some phosphates) out of the soil into rivers and lakes (leaching).
2. Nitrate is no longer limiting, so algae grow rapidly, forming an algal bloom on the surface.
3. The bloom blocks light from plants below, which die.
4. Saprobiotic bacteria decompose the dead plants and algae, and their numbers increase.
5. The bacteria respire aerobically and use up the dissolved oxygen.
6. Fish and other aerobic organisms die.

Reduce it by: applying fertiliser in the right amounts at the right time (not before heavy rain); leaving buffer strips by water; using manure; treating sewage.

#### Worked example

A farmer's field loses 120 kg of nitrogen per hectare per year in harvested crops. Ammonium nitrate (NH₄NO₃) is 35% nitrogen by mass. How much fertiliser is needed per hectare to replace the loss?

120 ÷ 0.35 = 343 kg ha⁻¹ (3 s.f.).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying nitrifying bacteria fix nitrogen.
- Saying decomposers release nitrate directly. They release ammonium (ammonification); nitrifying bacteria make nitrate.
- Saying plants absorb nitrogen gas.
- In eutrophication, saying "the algae use up the oxygen" or "fish die because of the algae". The bacteria decomposing dead material use the oxygen.
- Saying phosphorus cycles through the atmosphere.

### Check questions

1. Define net primary production and give the equation that links it to GPP. (2 marks)
2. Give three reasons why less than 10% of the energy in a trophic level is passed on to the next. (3 marks)
3. A sheep ingests 4200 kJ day⁻¹, loses 2500 kJ in faeces and urine and 1500 kJ in respiration. Calculate its net production and the percentage of ingested energy this represents. (2 marks)
4. Explain why keeping pigs indoors in small pens increases the efficiency of meat production. (3 marks)
5. Describe the roles of bacteria in converting protein in a dead leaf into nitrate in the soil. (4 marks)
6. Explain how mycorrhizae benefit plants. (2 marks)
7. Explain how leaching of fertiliser can lead to the death of fish. (5 marks)
8. Explain why ploughing a waterlogged field can increase its nitrate content. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Chemical energy in plant biomass after respiratory losses (1); NPP = GPP − R (1).
2. Any three: not all eaten; some indigestible / lost in faeces; lost in urine / excretion; lost as heat in respiration.
3. N = 4200 − (2500 + 1500) = 200 kJ day⁻¹ (1); 200 ÷ 4200 × 100 = 4.8% (1).
4. Less movement, so less energy used in respiration for muscle contraction (1); warm, so less energy used for heat (1); more energy available for growth / biomass (1).
5. Saprobionts / decomposers digest protein, releasing ammonium (ammonification) (1); nitrifying bacteria (1); oxidise ammonium to nitrite (1); then nitrite to nitrate (1).
6. Increase surface area for absorption (1); of water and ions, especially phosphate (1).
7. Nitrates washed into water (1); rapid algal growth / bloom blocks light (1); plants die (1); saprobionts / bacteria decompose them and increase in number (1); aerobic respiration of bacteria uses oxygen, so fish die (1).
8. Aerates soil / adds oxygen (1); favours nitrifying bacteria / reduces denitrification (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/18-stimuli-receptors-and-control-of-heart-rate.md -->

## Survival and response, receptors and control of heart rate

Organisms detect changes and respond to them. This file covers plant tropisms and IAA in outline, taxes, kineses and reflexes, receptors (the Pacinian corpuscle, rods and cones), and how the heartbeat is started and its rate controlled, with AQA required practical 10.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Plant hormones in depth are in file 31; the eye as an organ and visual problems in file 35; the organisation of the nervous system and reflex arcs in file 32; nerve impulses in file 19; behaviour (Eduqas, Edexcel A habituation) in file 48.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.6.1.1 to 3.6.1.3, RP10. Edexcel A 7.8, 7.9 (part), 8.5. Edexcel B 4.4 iv, v, 9.7 ii, iii, 9.8 i. OCR A 3.1.2(g), (h), 5.1.3(a), 5.1.5(k). OCR B 2.2.1(d), (e), (h), 5.3.1(b). Eduqas C3.2(c) (part).
- OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel A and Edexcel B ask for electrocardiograms (ECGs); AQA does not. Included below.
- OCR A uses the Pacinian corpuscle as its example of a transducer; Edexcel B and Edexcel A use rod cells and rhodopsin (included here; retina structure in file 35).
- Taxes, kineses and tropisms appear in AQA here, in Eduqas OC.3 (file 48) and in OCR A 5.1.5 (file 31).

### 3.6.1.1 Survival and response

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.1, RP10. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Organisms increase their chance of survival by responding to changes in their environment.
- In flowering plants, specific growth factors move from growing regions to other tissues, where they regulate growth in response to directional stimuli.
- The effect of different concentrations of indoleacetic acid (IAA) on cell elongation in the roots and shoots of flowering plants as an explanation of gravitropism and phototropism in flowering plants.
- Taxes and kineses as simple responses that can maintain a mobile organism in a favourable environment.
- The protective effect of a simple reflex, exemplified by a three-neurone simple reflex in a mammal. The importance of simple reflexes in avoiding damage to the body.

#### Explained

**Tropisms** are growth responses of plants towards or away from a directional stimulus. IAA (an auxin) is made in shoot and root tips and moves to the cells behind them.

- *Phototropism in shoots:* light from one side causes IAA to move to the shaded side. IAA makes shoot cells elongate (by loosening cell walls, in an acid-growth process). The shaded side grows faster, so the shoot bends towards light: positive phototropism.
- *Phototropism in roots:* IAA also moves to the shaded side, but in roots high IAA concentrations inhibit elongation. The shaded side grows less, so the root bends away from light: negative phototropism.
- *Gravitropism:* IAA gathers on the lower side. In shoots the lower side elongates more, so the shoot grows upwards (negative gravitropism). In roots the lower side elongates less, so the root grows downwards (positive gravitropism).

**Taxes and kineses.**

- A *taxis* is a directional movement towards (positive) or away from (negative) a stimulus. Examples: woodlice move away from light (negative phototaxis), keeping them hidden from predators and in damp places; single-celled algae move towards light.
- A *kinesis* is a non-directional response where the rate of movement or turning changes with the intensity of a stimulus. Example: woodlice in dry air move faster and turn less, so they are more likely to leave; in humid air they slow down and turn more, so they stay.

**Simple reflexes.** A reflex is a rapid, automatic response to a stimulus. The three-neurone reflex arc: receptor → sensory neurone → relay neurone (in the spinal cord) → motor neurone → effector (muscle). Example: withdrawing a hand from a hot object. Because the pathway does not go through the brain's conscious areas and has few synapses, it is fast and protects the body from damage before the brain even becomes aware.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying light "destroys" IAA on the lit side. The accepted explanation for AQA is that IAA moves to the shaded side.
- Saying IAA "inhibits growth" in all tissues. It promotes elongation in shoots and, at high concentration, inhibits it in roots.
- Calling a kinesis a "directional response".

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 10**: investigation into the effect of an environmental variable on the movement of an animal using either a choice chamber or a maze.

*Choice chamber.* A dish divided into sections with a gauze platform. Put a drying agent (silica gel or anhydrous calcium chloride) under one side and water under the other to compare humidity; or cover half with black card to compare light. Place ten woodlice in the centre; after 10 minutes count how many are on each side. Repeat with fresh woodlice.

*Maze.* Record which way an animal (maggot, woodlouse) turns at a T-junction when one arm is dark or damp.

Controls: the same number and species of animal; the same time; only one factor changed (cover the whole chamber in the humidity experiment so light is the same); return animals after each trial. Analysis: a chi-squared test compares the observed numbers with those expected if there were no preference (file 21, file 26). Ethics: handle animals gently with a soft brush, keep them for a short time, return them to their habitat.

### 3.6.1.2 Receptors

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.2 · Edexcel A 8.5 · Edexcel B 9.7 ii, iii · OCR A 5.1.3(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Receptors respond only to specific stimuli. Stimulation of a receptor leads to the establishment of a generator potential.
- The basic structure of a Pacinian corpuscle as an example of a receptor. How a Pacinian corpuscle produces a generator potential as a result of a pressure stimulus, by the opening of stretch-mediated sodium ion channels.
- Differences in sensitivity to light, sensitivity to colour and visual acuity as explained by differences in the optic pigments of rods and cones and the connections rods and cones make in the optic nerve.

#### Explained

Receptors are **transducers**: they convert energy from a stimulus (pressure, light, chemicals) into electrical energy, as a generator potential.

**Pacinian corpuscle.** Found deep in the skin (fingertips, soles) and joints; detects pressure. It is a sensory neurone ending wrapped in many layers of connective tissue (lamellae) separated by gel.

1. At rest, the stretch-mediated sodium ion channels in the neurone membrane are too narrow for Na⁺ to pass, so there is a resting potential.
2. Pressure deforms the lamellae, which press on the neurone ending and stretch its membrane.
3. The stretch-mediated sodium channels widen and Na⁺ diffuses in.
4. This depolarises the membrane: a generator potential.
5. If the generator potential reaches the threshold, an action potential is sent along the sensory neurone.

Stronger pressure opens more channels, giving a larger generator potential and a higher frequency of action potentials.

**Rods and cones.** Light-sensitive cells in the retina.

| | Rods | Cones |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pigment | Rhodopsin | Iodopsin (three types, each most sensitive to red, green or blue light) |
| Sensitivity to light | High: work in dim light | Low: need bright light |
| Colour | Black and white only | Colour vision |
| Connections | Many rods share one bipolar neurone (retinal convergence) | Each cone has its own bipolar neurone |
| Visual acuity | Low | High |
| Distribution | Mostly at the edges (periphery) of the retina | Concentrated in the fovea |

*Why rods are sensitive:* rhodopsin is broken down by low light intensity; several rods connect to one bipolar cell, so their small generator potentials add together (summation) to reach the threshold.

*Why rods give low acuity:* because several rods feed one neurone, the brain cannot tell which rod was stimulated, so two close points of light are seen as one.

*Why cones give high acuity:* each cone has its own route to the brain, so separate points are distinguished. But iodopsin needs high light intensity to be broken down, and each cone alone must reach threshold.

*Colour:* the brain compares how much each of the three cone types is stimulated.

**Rhodopsin** (Edexcel B, Edexcel A). Rhodopsin is retinal (a light-absorbing molecule) bound to opsin (a protein). Light changes retinal from *cis* to *trans*, so it no longer fits opsin and rhodopsin splits ("bleaching"). In the dark, sodium ions continually enter the rod and it releases an inhibitory neurotransmitter (glutamate) that stops the bipolar cell firing. When rhodopsin bleaches, a cascade closes sodium channels; the rod hyperpolarises and stops releasing glutamate, so the bipolar cell depolarises and an impulse is sent along the optic nerve. In bright light rhodopsin is bleached faster than it is remade, which is why it takes time to see after going into a dark room.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the Pacinian corpuscle detects "touch" without saying pressure deforms the membrane.
- Saying cones are found at the edge of the retina.
- Saying rods are more sensitive "because they have more pigment" rather than because of convergence.
- Saying generator potentials are "all or nothing". Action potentials are; generator potentials vary in size.

### 3.6.1.3 Control of heart rate

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.3 · Edexcel A 7.8, 7.9 · Edexcel B 4.4 iv, v, 9.8 i · OCR A 3.1.2(g), (h), 5.1.5(k) · OCR B 2.2.1(d), (e), (h), 5.3.1(b) · Eduqas C3.2(c). Level: A-level only (AS for OCR A, OCR B and Edexcel B on the initiation of the heartbeat and ECGs).*

#### What you need to know

- Myogenic stimulation of the heart and transmission of a subsequent wave of electrical activity. Roles of the sinoatrial node (SAN), atrioventricular node (AVN) and bundle of His.
- The roles and locations of chemoreceptors and pressure receptors and the roles of the autonomic nervous system and effectors in controlling heart rate.

#### Explained

**Myogenic.** Cardiac muscle contracts on its own, without nerve impulses. The nervous system only changes the rate.

**The sequence.**

1. The SAN, in the wall of the right atrium (the pacemaker), sends out a wave of electrical activity (excitation) at regular intervals.
2. The wave spreads across both atria, which contract (atrial systole).
3. A layer of non-conducting tissue between the atria and ventricles stops the wave passing directly to the ventricles.
4. The AVN picks up the wave and, after a short delay (about 0.1 s), passes it on. The delay lets the atria empty fully before the ventricles contract.
5. The wave passes down the bundle of His (in the septum) to the base (apex) of the ventricles.
6. It spreads up through Purkyne (Purkinje) fibres in the ventricle walls, so the ventricles contract from the bottom up, pushing blood up into the arteries.

**Changing the rate.** The cardiac control centre is in the medulla oblongata of the brain. It sends impulses along two parts of the autonomic nervous system:

- Sympathetic nerves (accelerator) to the SAN increase the rate (and force).
- Parasympathetic nerves (the vagus nerve, inhibitory) to the SAN decrease the rate.

Receptors:

| Receptor | Location | Detects | Response |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Chemoreceptors | Aorta, carotid arteries, medulla | Rise in CO₂ concentration (fall in blood pH), as during exercise | More impulses to the medulla, more sympathetic impulses to the SAN, heart rate rises; extra CO₂ is removed faster in the lungs |
| Pressure receptors (baroreceptors) | Aorta and carotid arteries | Rise in blood pressure | More parasympathetic impulses to the SAN, heart rate falls |
| | | Fall in blood pressure | More sympathetic impulses, heart rate rises |

Adrenaline from the adrenal glands also raises heart rate (OCR A 5.1.5(k), Edexcel B 9.8 ii; file 32).

**Electrocardiograms** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel A, Edexcel B). Electrodes on the skin record the electrical activity of the heart.

- P wave: depolarisation (excitation) of the atria, leading to atrial systole.
- QRS complex: depolarisation of the ventricles, leading to ventricular systole. Large, because the ventricles have more muscle.
- T wave: repolarisation of the ventricles (diastole).
- The P–R interval includes the AVN delay.

Abnormal traces:

- *Tachycardia:* heart rate over 100 beats per minute at rest.
- *Bradycardia:* under 60 beats per minute (normal in trained athletes).
- *Ectopic heartbeat:* an early, extra beat, followed by a pause.
- *Atrial fibrillation:* atria twitch irregularly instead of contracting; no clear P waves and an irregular rhythm of QRS complexes.
- A raised or depressed S–T segment can show a heart attack (damage to ventricle muscle).

#### Worked example

On an ECG the paper moves at 25 mm s⁻¹ and the distance between two R peaks is 20 mm. Calculate the heart rate.

1. Time per beat = 20 ÷ 25 = 0.8 s.
2. Heart rate = 60 ÷ 0.8 = 75 beats min⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the brain starts each heartbeat.
- Saying the AVN "slows the heart rate". It delays the wave so atria empty first.
- Saying the SAN is in the left atrium, or that the bundle of His carries blood.
- Saying chemoreceptors detect oxygen in the AQA answer: the main stimulus at A-level is CO₂ / pH.
- Mixing up which branch of the autonomic system speeds the heart.

### Check questions

1. Explain how IAA causes a shoot to grow towards light. (3 marks)
2. Woodlice in a choice chamber gathered in the damp half. Explain whether this is a taxis or a kinesis and why it aids survival. (3 marks)
3. Describe how a Pacinian corpuscle produces a generator potential. (4 marks)
4. Explain why rods give low visual acuity but high sensitivity to light. (4 marks)
5. Describe how a wave of electrical activity spreads through the heart. (5 marks)
6. During exercise, CO₂ concentration in the blood rises. Explain how this increases heart rate. (4 marks)
7. On an ECG with paper speed 25 mm s⁻¹, R peaks are 15 mm apart. Calculate heart rate. (2 marks)
8. Explain the importance of the delay at the AVN. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. IAA moves to shaded side (1); stimulates cell elongation on shaded side (1); shaded side grows faster, so shoot bends towards light (1).
2. Could be taxis (directional movement to damp) or kinesis (slower movement / more turning in damp); credit either with correct justification (1); damp prevents water loss / desiccation (1); also dark / hidden from predators (1).
3. Pressure deforms lamellae / stretches membrane (1); stretch-mediated sodium channels open (1); Na⁺ diffuses in (1); depolarisation / generator potential (1).
4. Many rods connect to one bipolar neurone (1); brain cannot distinguish separate points / low acuity (1); generator potentials of several rods summate to reach threshold (1); rhodopsin broken down by low light intensity (1).
5. SAN sends wave over atria, causing atrial contraction (1); non-conducting tissue prevents direct spread to ventricles (1); AVN delays then passes wave (1); down bundle of His to apex (1); up Purkyne fibres so ventricles contract from base upwards (1).
6. Chemoreceptors in aorta / carotid arteries / medulla detect fall in pH (1); more impulses to cardiac centre in medulla (1); more impulses along sympathetic nerve (1); SAN increases rate of impulses (1).
7. 15 ÷ 25 = 0.6 s per beat (1); 60 ÷ 0.6 = 100 beats min⁻¹ (1).
8. Atria contract and empty before ventricles contract (1); so ventricles fill fully / maximum blood ejected (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/19-nerve-impulses-synapses-and-muscle.md -->

## Nerve impulses, synapses and skeletal muscle

Neurones carry electrical impulses; synapses pass the signal chemically to the next cell; muscles turn the signal into movement. This file covers the resting and action potential, conduction speed, synaptic transmission and the sliding filament model of muscle contraction.

### Overview

A-level only. Neurone types and the organisation of the nervous system are in file 32. Drugs that act at synapses (beyond the examples here) are in file 34. Bones, joints and muscle energy for Eduqas Option B are in file 47; muscle fibre types and exercise in file 40.

#### Board differences
