- AQA 3.6.2.1, 3.6.2.2, 3.6.3. Edexcel A 7.1, 7.2, 7.10, 8.1, 8.3, 8.4. Edexcel B 9.5 i to v. OCR A 5.1.3(b) to (d), 5.1.5(l). OCR B 4.1.2(k), (l), 5.2.1(c) to (e). Eduqas C3.5(f) to (l), OB.1(h) to (k).
- Edexcel B asks for excitatory and inhibitory postsynaptic potentials (EPSPs and IPSPs) by name.
- OCR A asks you to compare skeletal, cardiac and involuntary (smooth) muscle (5.1.5(l)).
- Eduqas asks for oscilloscope traces and conduction speed in other organisms, including giant axons (C3.5(h), (i)), and organophosphate insecticides (C3.5(l)).
- Edexcel A 7.1 asks how muscles, tendons, ligaments and the skeleton interact; joints are expanded in file 47.

### 3.6.2.1 Nerve impulses

*Refs: AQA 3.6.2.1 · Edexcel A 8.1, 8.3 · Edexcel B 9.5 i to iii · OCR A 5.1.3(b), (c) · OCR B 5.2.1(c), (d) · Eduqas C3.5(f) to (i). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of a myelinated motor neurone.
- The establishment of a resting potential in terms of differential membrane permeability, electrochemical gradients and the movement of sodium ions and potassium ions.
- Changes in membrane permeability lead to depolarisation and the generation of an action potential. The all-or-nothing principle.
- The passage of an action potential along non-myelinated and myelinated axons, resulting in nerve impulses.
- The nature and importance of the refractory period in producing discrete impulses and in limiting the frequency of impulse transmission.
- Factors affecting the speed of conductance: myelination and saltatory conduction; axon diameter; temperature.

#### Explained

**A motor neurone** has a cell body (nucleus, many ribosomes and rough ER to make neurotransmitter and proteins), dendrites that receive impulses from other neurones, a long axon that carries impulses away, and axon terminals ending at the effector. Many axons are wrapped in a **myelin sheath** formed by **Schwann cells** wrapping round the axon. Between Schwann cells are small gaps called **nodes of Ranvier**. (Sensory neurones have the cell body part-way along; relay neurones are short and in the CNS: file 32.)

**Resting potential** (about −70 mV: the inside is negative compared to the outside).

1. The sodium–potassium pump actively moves 3 Na⁺ out for every 2 K⁺ in, using ATP.
2. The membrane is more permeable to K⁺ than Na⁺: many K⁺ channels are open; most Na⁺ channels are closed.
3. K⁺ diffuses back out down its gradient. Na⁺ cannot easily diffuse back in.
4. More positive ions are outside than inside, so the axon is polarised.

**Action potential.**

1. *Depolarisation:* a stimulus opens some voltage-gated Na⁺ channels; Na⁺ diffuses in. If the potential reaches the **threshold** (about −55 mV), many more voltage-gated Na⁺ channels open (positive feedback); Na⁺ floods in; the inside reaches about +40 mV.
2. *Repolarisation:* Na⁺ channels close; voltage-gated K⁺ channels open; K⁺ diffuses out; the potential falls.
3. *Hyperpolarisation:* K⁺ channels are slow to close, so too many K⁺ leave and the potential briefly drops below −70 mV.
4. The resting potential is restored by the sodium–potassium pump and K⁺ channels closing.

**All-or-nothing.** If the threshold is reached, an action potential of the same size (+40 mV) always follows; below threshold, none. A stronger stimulus does not give a bigger action potential; it gives more action potentials per second (higher frequency).

**Passage along the axon.** In a non-myelinated axon, Na⁺ entering one region diffuses sideways (local currents), depolarising the next region to threshold; a wave of depolarisation travels along. In a myelinated axon, the myelin insulates the axon, so depolarisation happens only at the nodes of Ranvier; the impulse jumps from node to node (**saltatory conduction**), much faster.

**Refractory period.** After an action potential, voltage-gated Na⁺ channels are closed and cannot open again for a short time. This:

- means the impulse travels in one direction only (the region behind cannot fire)
- produces discrete (separate) impulses rather than merging
- limits the number of impulses per second.

**Speed of conduction.**

- Myelination: much faster (saltatory conduction).
- Axon diameter: wider axons conduct faster (less resistance to the flow of ions; less leakage).
- Temperature: faster up to about 40 °C, because ions diffuse faster and the pump works faster; above this proteins denature.

(Eduqas) Invertebrates such as squid and earthworms have **giant axons** (up to 1 mm wide, non-myelinated) for fast escape responses. A myelinated mammalian axon conducts up to about 100 m s⁻¹; a non-myelinated one about 1 m s⁻¹.

**Oscilloscope traces** (Eduqas) plot membrane potential against time. Read off the resting potential, threshold, peak, and the time of one action potential (about 1 to 2 ms) to calculate maximum frequency.

#### Worked example

An action potential takes 3 ms from the start of depolarisation to the end of the refractory period. What is the maximum frequency of impulses?

1 ÷ 0.003 = 333 impulses per second.

A myelinated neurone 0.9 m long conducts at 90 m s⁻¹. How long does an impulse take to travel its length? 0.9 ÷ 90 = 0.01 s = 10 ms.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the inside is positive at rest.
- Saying the sodium–potassium pump causes depolarisation. Voltage-gated channels do.
- Saying a bigger stimulus gives a bigger action potential.
- Saying the impulse "jumps from one Schwann cell to another"; it is from node to node.
- Saying myelin conducts the impulse; it insulates.

### 3.6.2.2 Synaptic transmission

*Refs: AQA 3.6.2.2 · Edexcel A 8.4 · Edexcel B 9.5 iv, v · OCR A 5.1.3(d) · OCR B 5.2.1(e) · Eduqas C3.5(j) to (l). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The detailed structure of a synapse and of a neuromuscular junction.
- The sequence of events involved in transmission across a cholinergic synapse in sufficient detail to explain: unidirectionality; temporal and spatial summation; inhibition by inhibitory synapses.
- A comparison of transmission across a cholinergic synapse and across a neuromuscular junction.
- Use information provided to predict the effects of specific drugs on a synapse.

#### Explained

**Structure.** The presynaptic neurone ends in a swollen knob with many mitochondria and vesicles of neurotransmitter. A gap of about 20 nm (the synaptic cleft) separates it from the postsynaptic membrane, which has receptor proteins for the neurotransmitter. In a cholinergic synapse the neurotransmitter is acetylcholine (ACh).

**Transmission.**

1. An action potential reaches the presynaptic knob.
2. Voltage-gated calcium ion channels open; Ca²⁺ diffuses into the knob.
3. Ca²⁺ causes vesicles to move to and fuse with the presynaptic membrane, releasing ACh into the cleft by exocytosis.
4. ACh diffuses across the cleft and binds to complementary receptors on the postsynaptic membrane.
5. Sodium ion channels in the receptors open; Na⁺ diffuses in, depolarising the postsynaptic membrane. If threshold is reached, an action potential starts.
6. Acetylcholinesterase in the cleft hydrolyses ACh into choline and ethanoic acid (acetyl). These diffuse back into the presynaptic knob and are recombined into ACh using ATP. Removal stops the signal and lets the receptor be used again.

**Unidirectional.** Only the presynaptic knob has vesicles; only the postsynaptic membrane has receptors.

**Summation.** One release of neurotransmitter may not reach threshold.

- *Spatial summation:* several presynaptic neurones release neurotransmitter onto one postsynaptic neurone at the same time; together they reach threshold.
- *Temporal summation:* one presynaptic neurone releases neurotransmitter repeatedly in quick succession; the effect builds up.

**Inhibitory synapses.** Some neurotransmitters (such as GABA) open chloride channels (Cl⁻ diffuses in) or potassium channels (K⁺ diffuses out) on the postsynaptic membrane. The inside becomes more negative (hyperpolarised), so more excitatory input is needed to reach threshold. Edexcel B calls these **inhibitory postsynaptic potentials (IPSPs)**; depolarisations that move towards threshold are **excitatory postsynaptic potentials (EPSPs)**. The postsynaptic neurone sums EPSPs and IPSPs.

**Neuromuscular junction** compared with a cholinergic synapse:

| | Cholinergic synapse | Neuromuscular junction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Connects | Neurone to neurone | Motor neurone to muscle fibre |
| Effect | Excitatory or inhibitory | Always excitatory |
| Postsynaptic membrane | Smooth | Folded into clefts with many receptors and acetylcholinesterase |
| Result | Action potential in next neurone (if threshold reached) | Every impulse normally causes depolarisation of the muscle fibre membrane |

**Drugs** (predict effects from information).

- A drug that mimics the neurotransmitter (same shape, binds receptors) increases stimulation (nicotine at some ACh receptors).
- A drug that blocks receptors reduces it (curare at the neuromuscular junction causes paralysis).
- A drug that inhibits acetylcholinesterase (organophosphate insecticides, nerve agents) leaves ACh in the cleft, so muscles stay contracted (Eduqas C3.5(l)).
- A drug that stops neurotransmitter release (some anaesthetics) reduces stimulation.
- A drug that stops reuptake leaves more neurotransmitter in the cleft (file 34 for MDMA and others).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the impulse "jumps" across the synapse.
- Forgetting calcium ions.
- Saying acetylcholinesterase "breaks down the receptor".
- Saying inhibitory synapses "stop the action potential". They make reaching threshold less likely.

### 3.6.3 Skeletal muscles are stimulated to contract by nerves and act as effectors

*Refs: AQA 3.6.3 · Edexcel A 7.1, 7.2, 7.10 · OCR A 5.1.5(l) · OCR B 4.1.2(k), (l) · Eduqas OB.1(h) to (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Muscles act in antagonistic pairs against an incompressible skeleton.
- Gross and microscopic structure of skeletal muscle. The ultrastructure of a myofibril.
- The roles of actin, myosin, calcium ions and ATP in myofibril contraction. The roles of calcium ions and tropomyosin in the cycle of actinomyosin bridge formation. (The role of troponin is not required by AQA.)
- The roles of ATP and phosphocreatine in muscle contraction.
- The structure, location and general properties of slow and fast skeletal muscle fibres.

#### Explained

**Antagonistic pairs.** Muscles can only pull. One muscle contracts while its partner relaxes: biceps contracts to flex (bend) the elbow; triceps contracts to extend it. The skeleton is incompressible, so the force moves the bones. Tendons join muscle to bone (strong, inelastic); ligaments join bone to bone at joints (Edexcel A 7.1).

**Structure.** A muscle is made of bundles of muscle fibres. Each fibre is a long multinucleate cell with a cell membrane (sarcolemma) folded inwards as T-tubules, sarcoplasm with many mitochondria, sarcoplasmic reticulum (stores Ca²⁺), and many myofibrils.

**Myofibril.** Made of repeating units called **sarcomeres**, joined end to end at Z-lines.

- Thin filaments: actin (with tropomyosin wrapped round them, and troponin attached).
- Thick filaments: myosin, each with many heads.
- A-band (dark): the length of the myosin filaments, including where they overlap actin.
- I-band (light): actin only.
- H-zone: myosin only, in the middle of the A-band.
- M-line: middle of the sarcomere.

**Sliding filament model.** In contraction the actin filaments slide over the myosin, pulled towards the middle. The sarcomere shortens: the I-band and H-zone get shorter; the A-band stays the same length.

**The cycle.**

1. An action potential arrives at the neuromuscular junction; depolarisation spreads along the sarcolemma and down T-tubules.
2. Ca²⁺ is released from the sarcoplasmic reticulum into the sarcoplasm.
3. Ca²⁺ binds to troponin, which moves tropomyosin away from the binding sites on actin.
4. Myosin heads (with ADP attached) bind to the exposed binding sites, forming actinomyosin cross-bridges.
5. The myosin heads change angle, pulling the actin filament towards the M-line (the power stroke), releasing ADP.
6. ATP binds to the myosin head, causing it to detach from actin.
7. ATP hydrolase (ATPase) in the myosin head hydrolyses the ATP; the energy returns the head to its original position.
8. The head binds further along the actin, and the cycle repeats while Ca²⁺ and ATP are present.

When stimulation stops, Ca²⁺ is actively transported back into the sarcoplasmic reticulum (using ATP); tropomyosin covers the binding sites again; the muscle relaxes.

**ATP sources.** A muscle's ATP lasts only a few seconds.

- Aerobic respiration (main source over time).
- Anaerobic respiration (glycolysis to lactate; fast but short).
- **Phosphocreatine** (PCr), stored in muscle, gives its phosphate to ADP: PCr + ADP → creatine + ATP. Very fast, no oxygen needed, lasts a few seconds (sprints). It is regenerated from ATP when the muscle is resting.

**Slow and fast fibres.**

| | Slow-twitch | Fast-twitch |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Contraction | Slower, less powerful, for a long time | Rapid, powerful, short bursts |
| Where common | Calves, back (posture), endurance | Arms, legs used for sprinting |
| Respiration | Aerobic | Anaerobic; also phosphocreatine stores |
| Mitochondria | Many | Few |
| Blood supply | Many capillaries | Fewer |
| Myoglobin | Lots (red colour; stores oxygen) | Little (white) |
| Fatigue | Resistant | Tire quickly (lactate) |
| Other | | Thicker, more myosin filaments; more enzymes for glycolysis; more glycogen |

**Three muscle types** (OCR A).

| | Skeletal | Cardiac | Involuntary (smooth) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Appearance | Striated, long multinucleate fibres | Striated, branched cells joined by intercalated discs | Not striated; spindle-shaped cells, one nucleus |
| Control | Voluntary (somatic nervous system) | Myogenic; rate changed by autonomic nerves | Involuntary (autonomic) |
| Contraction | Rapid; fatigues | Rhythmic; does not fatigue | Slow, sustained |
| Where | Attached to bones | Heart | Walls of gut, blood vessels, bronchioles |

#### Worked example

A sarcomere is 2.4 µm long when relaxed; the A-band is 1.6 µm. When contracted the sarcomere is 2.0 µm. Find the A-band and total I-band lengths when contracted.

1. The A-band stays the same: 1.6 µm.
2. Sarcomere − A-band = I-band total (the two half I-bands): 2.0 − 1.6 = 0.4 µm (relaxed it was 0.8 µm).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the filaments themselves shorten.
- Saying the A-band shortens.
- Saying ATP is needed for the power stroke. ATP is needed to detach the head and recock it; the power stroke releases ADP.
- Saying calcium binds to tropomyosin (it binds to troponin; AQA accepts "calcium ions cause tropomyosin to move").
- Saying slow-twitch fibres "contract slowly so they use less energy" without linking to aerobic respiration.

### Check questions

1. Explain how a resting potential is maintained in an axon. (3 marks)
2. Describe the events that produce an action potential, from the stimulus to repolarisation. (5 marks)
3. Explain why myelinated axons conduct impulses faster than non-myelinated axons of the same diameter. (2 marks)
4. Explain why impulses travel in only one direction along an axon and across a synapse. (3 marks)
5. Describe the role of calcium ions at a cholinergic synapse. (2 marks)
6. An organophosphate inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Explain why it causes muscle spasms. (3 marks)
7. Describe how the binding of calcium ions leads to the sliding of filaments. (5 marks)
8. Explain why sprinters' leg muscles have many fast-twitch fibres. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Sodium–potassium pump moves 3 Na⁺ out and 2 K⁺ in (1); membrane more permeable to K⁺ / K⁺ diffuses out (1); fewer Na⁺ channels open, so Na⁺ cannot diffuse back in (1).
2. Stimulus opens some Na⁺ channels; Na⁺ diffuses in (1); threshold reached, more voltage-gated Na⁺ channels open (1); inside becomes positive / +40 mV / depolarisation (1); Na⁺ channels close and K⁺ channels open (1); K⁺ diffuses out, repolarisation (1).
3. Myelin insulates; depolarisation only at nodes of Ranvier (1); impulse jumps between nodes / saltatory conduction (1).
4. Axon: refractory period / Na⁺ channels behind cannot open (1). Synapse: vesicles only in presynaptic knob (1); receptors only on postsynaptic membrane (1).
5. Ca²⁺ enter presynaptic knob through voltage-gated channels (1); cause vesicles to fuse with membrane / release ACh (1).
6. ACh not hydrolysed (1); remains bound to receptors / Na⁺ channels stay open (1); continuous depolarisation / muscle stimulated (1).
7. Ca²⁺ bind to troponin (1); tropomyosin moves, exposing binding sites on actin (1); myosin heads bind forming cross-bridges (1); heads change angle, pulling actin / power stroke (1); ATP detaches heads; hydrolysis re-cocks them (1).
8. Contract rapidly / powerfully (1); anaerobic respiration / phosphocreatine supplies ATP quickly (1); sprinting is short so fatigue is not a problem (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/20-homeostasis-blood-glucose-and-water.md -->

## Homeostasis: blood glucose and blood water potential

Homeostasis keeps the internal environment within narrow limits. This file covers negative and positive feedback, the control of blood glucose by insulin, glucagon and adrenaline, diabetes, and the control of blood water potential by the kidney and ADH, with AQA required practical 11.

### Overview

A-level only. Temperature control is in file 33, with diabetes treatment and management for OCR A and OCR B. The liver, urea formation, kidney failure and dialysis are in file 30. Hormones in general and the adrenal glands are in file 32.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.6.4.1 to 3.6.4.3, RP11. Edexcel A 7.11, 7.12 (part). Edexcel B 9.1, 9.9 i, iii, iv. OCR A 5.1.1(c), 5.1.2(c), (d), 5.1.4(c) to (e). OCR B 5.3.1(a), 5.3.2(a) to (c), 5.3.3(c), (d). Eduqas C3.4(a) to (g).
- OCR A asks for the histology of the pancreas (islets of Langerhans) and the kidney (5.1.4(c), 5.1.2(c)), with a kidney dissection.
- Edexcel B asks for aquaporins and osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus by name (9.9 iv); AQA asks for the same mechanism.
- Edexcel A 7.11 asks for positive feedback as well as negative; AQA asks for both.
- Eduqas C3.4(e) asks for adaptations of proximal tubule cells (microvilli, mitochondria).
- OCR A and OCR B ask how insulin secretion is controlled in β cells (potassium channels, calcium ions); included below.

### 3.6.4.1 Principles of homeostasis and negative feedback

*Refs: AQA 3.6.4.1 · Edexcel A 7.11, 7.12 · Edexcel B 9.1 i to iii · OCR A 5.1.1(c) · OCR B 5.3.1(a) · Eduqas C3.4(a), (b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Homeostasis in mammals involves physiological control systems that maintain the internal environment within restricted limits.
- The importance of maintaining a stable core temperature and stable blood pH in relation to enzyme activity.
- The importance of maintaining a stable blood glucose concentration in terms of availability of respiratory substrate and of the water potential of the blood.
- Negative feedback restores systems to their original level. The possession of separate mechanisms involving negative feedback controls departures in different directions from the original state, giving a greater degree of control.
- Positive feedback results in greater departures from the original levels. Positive feedback is often associated with a breakdown of control systems, eg in temperature control.

#### Explained

**Why keep things stable.**

- *Temperature:* too low and enzymes work slowly (too little kinetic energy); too high and they denature.
- *pH:* changes break ionic and hydrogen bonds in enzymes, changing the active site.
- *Blood glucose:* too low and cells (especially brain cells) lack respiratory substrate; too high and blood water potential falls, so water leaves cells by osmosis.

Homeostasis is a dynamic equilibrium (Edexcel B): values fluctuate around a set point but are kept within limits.

**Components.** A receptor detects a change → a coordinator (often the brain or a gland) → an effector (muscle or gland) responds.

**Negative feedback.** The response reverses the change, bringing the value back to the set point. Having separate mechanisms for a rise and a fall (insulin and glucagon; sweating and shivering) gives finer control than one mechanism that is simply switched on or off.

**Positive feedback.** The response increases the change. Examples: the opening of voltage-gated sodium channels during depolarisation; oxytocin in childbirth (contractions cause more oxytocin, causing stronger contractions); blood clotting. In hypothermia, a low body temperature slows metabolism, so less heat is made and temperature falls further: control breaks down.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying negative feedback "keeps things exactly constant".
- Saying positive feedback is "good feedback".

### 3.6.4.2 Control of blood glucose concentration

*Refs: AQA 3.6.4.2, RP11 · OCR A 5.1.4(c) to (e) · OCR B 5.3.2(a) to (c) · Eduqas C3.4(f) (part). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The factors that influence blood glucose concentration.
- The role of the liver in glycogenesis, glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.
- The action of insulin by: attaching to receptors on the surfaces of target cells; controlling the uptake of glucose by regulating the inclusion of channel proteins in the surface membranes of target cells; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glucose to glycogen.
- The action of glucagon by: attaching to receptors on the surfaces of target cells; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glycogen to glucose; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glycerol and amino acids into glucose.
- The role of adrenaline by: attaching to receptors on the surfaces of target cells; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glycogen to glucose.
- The second messenger model of adrenaline and glucagon action, involving adenylate cyclase, cyclic AMP (cAMP) and protein kinase.
- The causes of types I and II diabetes and their control by insulin and/or manipulation of the diet.
- Evaluate the positions of health advisers and the food industry in relation to the increased incidence of type II diabetes.

#### Explained

**What changes blood glucose.** Rises after eating carbohydrate. Falls with exercise or fasting (glucose used in respiration).

**Three liver processes.**

- **Glycogenesis:** glucose → glycogen (when glucose is high).
- **Glycogenolysis:** glycogen → glucose (when glucose is low).
- **Gluconeogenesis:** making glucose from non-carbohydrates, such as glycerol and amino acids (when glycogen runs low).

**The pancreas.** The islets of Langerhans contain α cells (make glucagon) and β cells (make insulin). The rest of the pancreas (acinar tissue, in clusters around ducts) makes digestive enzymes. In a stained section, islets are lighter-staining patches (OCR A).

**When blood glucose rises:**

1. β cells detect the rise. (OCR A, OCR B: glucose enters β cells and is respired, raising ATP; ATP closes K⁺ channels, depolarising the membrane; voltage-gated Ca²⁺ channels open; Ca²⁺ entry causes vesicles of insulin to be released by exocytosis.)
2. Insulin binds to specific receptors on liver and muscle cells (and others).
3. Vesicles carrying glucose transporter proteins (GLUT4) fuse with the cell-surface membrane, so more glucose enters by facilitated diffusion.
4. Insulin activates enzymes that convert glucose to glycogen (glycogenesis) and to fat; respiration rate increases.
5. Blood glucose falls; less insulin is released (negative feedback).

**When blood glucose falls:**

1. α cells detect the fall and secrete glucagon.
2. Glucagon binds to receptors on liver cells only (muscle has no glucagon receptors).
3. It activates enzymes for glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.
4. Glucose is released into the blood; blood glucose rises; less glucagon is secreted.

**Adrenaline**, released by the adrenal glands under stress or exercise, also binds to liver cells and activates glycogenolysis, raising blood glucose to supply muscles.

**Second messenger model** (for adrenaline and glucagon, which cannot enter the cell).

1. The hormone (first messenger) binds to its receptor on the cell-surface membrane.
2. This changes the shape of the receptor, which activates the enzyme adenylate cyclase on the inside.
3. Adenylate cyclase converts ATP into cyclic AMP (cAMP), the second messenger.
4. cAMP activates protein kinase A.
5. Protein kinase A activates, through a cascade, the enzymes that break down glycogen.

The cascade amplifies the signal: one hormone molecule leads to many glucose molecules being released.

**Diabetes.**

| | Type I | Type II |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cause | Autoimmune attack destroys β cells; no insulin made | Receptors on target cells become less responsive to insulin; β cells may also make less |
| Onset | Usually in childhood | Usually in adults; linked to obesity, poor diet, lack of exercise, family history, ethnicity |
| Control | Insulin injections (or pump), matched to diet and exercise; regular blood glucose tests | Diet (less sugar and refined carbohydrate), exercise, weight loss; drugs such as metformin; sometimes insulin |

Insulin cannot be taken by mouth: it is a protein and would be digested.

**Type II and society.** Health advisers recommend less sugar and more exercise, and support measures such as a tax on sugary drinks and clearer labels. The food industry has reduced sugar in some products, but points out that it provides choice and that sweeteners have their own questions; some argue that pressure has been resisted for commercial reasons. When evaluating, look at who funded the evidence and whether claims are supported by data.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing glycogen, glucagon and glycogenesis.
- Saying insulin "turns glucose into glycogen". Insulin activates the enzymes that do this.
- Saying glucagon acts on muscle cells.
- Saying type I diabetes is caused by eating too much sugar.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 11**: production of a dilution series of a glucose solution and use of colorimetric techniques to produce a calibration curve with which to identify the concentration of glucose in an unknown "urine" sample.

1. Make a dilution series of glucose (for example 0, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 mmol dm⁻³) from a stock solution (file 01 shows the calculation).
2. Add the same volume of Benedict's reagent (in excess) to the same volume of each solution. Heat in a water bath at the same temperature for the same time.
3. Allow to cool; filter or centrifuge out the precipitate if using the remaining blue colour, or use quantitative Benedict's reagent.
4. Zero the colorimeter with distilled water; use a red filter; measure absorbance (or transmission) of each.
5. Plot absorbance against glucose concentration: the calibration curve.
6. Test the unknown samples the same way and read their concentrations from the curve.

Risks: Benedict's reagent is an irritant; hot water bath. Limitations: colorimeter readings are affected by cloudiness; the method only measures reducing sugars; the unknown must lie within the range of standards.

### 3.6.4.3 Control of blood water potential

*Refs: AQA 3.6.4.3 · Edexcel B 9.9 i, iii, iv · OCR A 5.1.2(c), (d) · OCR B 5.3.3(c), (d) · Eduqas C3.4(c) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of the nephron and its role in: the formation of glomerular filtrate; reabsorption of glucose and water by the proximal convoluted tubule; maintaining a gradient of sodium ions in the medulla by the loop of Henle; reabsorption of water by the distal convoluted tubule and collecting ducts.
- The roles of the hypothalamus, posterior pituitary and antidiuretic hormone (ADH) in osmoregulation.

#### Explained

**The kidney.** The renal artery brings blood; the renal vein takes it away; the ureter carries urine to the bladder. Outer cortex; inner medulla; the pelvis where urine collects. Each kidney has about a million nephrons.

**The nephron.**

| Part | Where | Role |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Glomerulus and Bowman's (renal) capsule | Cortex | Ultrafiltration |
| Proximal convoluted tubule (PCT) | Cortex | Selective reabsorption of glucose, amino acids, most water and ions |
| Loop of Henle | Dips into medulla | Creates a high sodium ion concentration (low water potential) in the medulla |
| Distal convoluted tubule (DCT) | Cortex | Fine-tunes water and ion balance |
| Collecting duct | Cortex to medulla | Water reabsorbed under the control of ADH |

**Ultrafiltration.**

- The afferent arteriole entering the glomerulus is wider than the efferent arteriole leaving it, so hydrostatic pressure in the glomerulus is high.
- Water, glucose, amino acids, urea and ions are forced out through the gaps in the capillary endothelium, through the basement membrane (the actual filter, which holds back proteins) and between the podocytes (cells of the capsule with foot-like processes) into the capsule.
- Blood cells and large proteins stay in the blood. The fluid in the capsule is the glomerular filtrate.

**Proximal convoluted tubule.** All glucose and amino acids, and about 85% of water and much of the sodium, are reabsorbed here.

- Na⁺ is actively pumped out of PCT cells into the blood, keeping Na⁺ low inside the cells.
- Na⁺ diffuses from the filtrate into the cells by co-transport, carrying glucose (or amino acids).
- Glucose diffuses out into the blood by facilitated diffusion.
- Water follows by osmosis.
- (Eduqas) Adaptations of PCT cells: microvilli give a large surface area; many mitochondria supply ATP for active transport; many co-transporter proteins; close to capillaries.

If blood glucose is very high (untreated diabetes), there are not enough co-transporters to reabsorb it all, and glucose appears in urine.

**Loop of Henle (countercurrent multiplier).**

1. In the ascending limb, Na⁺ (and Cl⁻) are actively transported out into the medulla. The ascending limb is impermeable to water, so water cannot follow.
2. This makes the tissue fluid of the medulla have a low water potential.
3. The descending limb is permeable to water. Water leaves it by osmosis into the medulla and is carried away by the blood capillaries (vasa recta).
4. The filtrate becomes more concentrated going down, and less concentrated going up.
5. The deepest part of the medulla has the lowest water potential.
6. The collecting duct passes through this region, so water can be drawn out of it by osmosis.

Animals living in deserts (kangaroo rat, file 30) have longer loops of Henle, giving a steeper gradient and more concentrated urine.

**Distal tubule and collecting duct.** Reabsorption of water depends on how permeable their walls are, which ADH controls.

**Osmoregulation with ADH.**

1. Osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus detect a fall in blood water potential (for example after sweating or eating salty food). Water leaves the osmoreceptor cells by osmosis and they shrink, sending impulses.
2. The hypothalamus makes ADH; it passes down neurones to the posterior pituitary gland, which releases it into the blood.
3. ADH binds to receptors on cells of the DCT and collecting duct.
4. This activates a second messenger pathway: vesicles containing **aquaporins** (water channel proteins) fuse with the cell-surface membrane facing the filtrate.
5. More water is reabsorbed by osmosis into the medulla and blood. A small volume of concentrated urine is produced.
6. Blood water potential rises; less ADH is released (negative feedback).

When blood water potential is too high (after drinking a lot), less ADH is released; fewer aquaporins are in the membranes; less water is reabsorbed; a large volume of dilute urine is produced.

#### Worked example

The glomerular filtration rate is 125 cm³ min⁻¹. Urine is produced at 1 cm³ min⁻¹. What percentage of filtrate is reabsorbed?

(125 − 1) ÷ 125 × 100 = 99.2%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying ADH "makes the kidney reabsorb salt".
- Saying the loop of Henle reabsorbs water by active transport. Water always moves by osmosis.
- Saying the descending limb pumps out sodium ions.
- Saying ADH is made in the pituitary. It is made in the hypothalamus and released from the posterior pituitary.
- Saying proteins are filtered then reabsorbed. They are too large to be filtered.

### Check questions

1. Explain why it is important to keep blood glucose concentration within narrow limits. (2 marks)
2. Describe how insulin lowers blood glucose concentration. (4 marks)
3. Describe the second messenger model of glucagon action. (4 marks)
4. Explain why people with type I diabetes cannot take insulin as a tablet. (2 marks)
5. Describe how ultrafiltration happens in the glomerulus. (3 marks)
6. Explain how the loop of Henle creates a low water potential in the medulla. (4 marks)
7. Describe and explain the response of the body to dehydration. (5 marks)
8. In RP11, explain why the same volume of Benedict's reagent and the same heating time must be used for every tube. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Too low: not enough respiratory substrate (for brain) (1); too high: lowers blood water potential, water leaves cells by osmosis (1).
2. Insulin binds to receptors on liver / muscle cells (1); more glucose transporter / channel proteins inserted in membrane (1); more glucose enters cells (1); activates enzymes for glycogenesis / increases respiration (1).
3. Glucagon binds to receptor on liver cell membrane (1); activates adenylate cyclase (1); converts ATP to cAMP (1); cAMP activates protein kinase, which activates enzymes for glycogenolysis (1).
4. Insulin is a protein (1); digested / hydrolysed by proteases in the gut (1).
5. High hydrostatic pressure in glomerulus (afferent wider than efferent) (1); small molecules / water forced out through capillary endothelium, basement membrane and podocytes (1); large proteins and cells remain (1).
6. Na⁺ actively transported out of ascending limb (1); ascending limb impermeable to water (1); medulla has high Na⁺ / low water potential (1); water leaves descending limb by osmosis, concentration greatest deep in medulla (1).
7. Osmoreceptors in hypothalamus detect fall in water potential (1); more ADH released from posterior pituitary (1); ADH binds to receptors on DCT / collecting duct (1); more aquaporins inserted, walls more permeable to water (1); more water reabsorbed by osmosis; small volume of concentrated urine (1).
8. Colour / absorbance depends on these as well as glucose concentration (1); so differences are only due to glucose concentration and the curve applies to the unknown (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/21-inheritance.md -->

## Inheritance

Genetic crosses predict the offspring of parents with known genotypes. This file covers monohybrid and dihybrid crosses, codominance, multiple alleles, sex linkage, autosomal linkage, epistasis, polygenic inheritance and the chi-squared test.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Meiosis is in file 13; Hardy–Weinberg in file 22; genetic disorders, screening and counselling in file 39.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.1. Edexcel A 2.13, 3.8, 3.15. Edexcel B 8.2 i to vi. OCR A 6.1.2(a) to (d). OCR B 5.1.1(a), (c) to (e). Eduqas C2.5(a) to (e).
- Edexcel A asks for pedigree diagrams and cystic fibrosis as its monohybrid example (CF itself in file 39), and polygenic inheritance (3.15).
- OCR A asks for continuous and discontinuous variation linked to polygenes (6.1.2(d)), and for environmental effects such as chlorosis and etiolation in plants (6.1.2(a)).
- OCR B asks for model organisms used in genetics (5.1.1(e)).
- Eduqas names haemophilia and Duchenne muscular dystrophy as sex-linked examples (C2.5(e)); AQA uses no fixed examples.

### 3.7.1 Inheritance

*Refs: AQA 3.7.1 · Edexcel A 2.13, 3.8, 3.15 · Edexcel B 8.2 i to vi · OCR A 6.1.2(a) to (d) · OCR B 5.1.1(a), (c) to (e) · Eduqas C2.5(a) to (e). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A).*

#### What you need to know

- The genotype is the genetic constitution of an organism. The phenotype is the expression of this genetic constitution and its interaction with the environment.
- There may be many alleles of a single gene. Alleles may be dominant, recessive or codominant.
- In a diploid organism, the alleles at a specific locus may be either homozygous or heterozygous.
- The use of fully labelled genetic diagrams to interpret, or predict, the results of: monohybrid and dihybrid crosses involving dominant, recessive and codominant alleles; crosses involving sex-linkage, autosomal linkage, multiple alleles and epistasis.
- The use of the chi-squared (χ²) test to compare the goodness of fit of observed phenotypic ratios with expected ratios.

#### Explained

**Terms.**

- *Gene:* a length of DNA at a locus that codes for a polypeptide.
- *Allele:* a different form of the same gene.
- *Dominant:* always expressed in the phenotype when present (capital letter).
- *Recessive:* expressed only when two copies are present (lower case).
- *Codominant:* both alleles are expressed in the heterozygote.
- *Homozygous:* two identical alleles (AA or aa). *Heterozygous:* two different alleles (Aa).

**Monohybrid cross.** One gene. Two heterozygotes (Aa × Aa) give offspring genotypes 1 AA : 2 Aa : 1 aa, and phenotypes 3 dominant : 1 recessive.

A full genetic diagram has: parental phenotypes; parental genotypes; gametes (circled); a Punnett square; offspring genotypes; offspring phenotypes; ratio.

*Test cross:* cross an organism with the dominant phenotype with a homozygous recessive. If any offspring show the recessive phenotype, the parent was heterozygous.

**Codominance.** In snapdragons, C^R C^R gives red, C^W C^W white, C^R C^W pink. A cross of two pink gives 1 red : 2 pink : 1 white. Human example: the sickle cell alleles (Hb^A Hb^S gives sickle cell trait).

**Multiple alleles.** Human ABO blood groups: one gene with three alleles, I^A, I^B and I^O. I^A and I^B are codominant; both are dominant to I^O.

| Genotype | Blood group |
| --- | --- |
| I^A I^A, I^A I^O | A |
| I^B I^B, I^B I^O | B |
| I^A I^B | AB |
| I^O I^O | O |

A parent with group A (I^A I^O) and a parent with group B (I^B I^O) can have children of all four groups.

**Dihybrid cross.** Two genes on different chromosomes. AaBb × AaBb: each parent makes four kinds of gamete (AB, Ab, aB, ab) in equal numbers because of independent segregation. Offspring phenotypes: 9 : 3 : 3 : 1.

**Sex linkage.** Genes on the X chromosome. Females are XX; males XY. The Y chromosome is small and carries few of the genes on the X, so males have only one allele of X-linked genes and express it, even if recessive. Example: haemophilia (a recessive allele, X^h, for a missing clotting factor).

- Carrier mother (X^H X^h) × normal father (X^H Y): daughters X^H X^H or X^H X^h (none affected, half carriers); sons X^H Y or X^h Y (half affected).
- Males inherit their X from their mother, so a father cannot pass an X-linked allele to his sons.
- Duchenne muscular dystrophy (Eduqas) and red-green colour blindness are other X-linked recessive conditions.

**Autosomal linkage.** Genes on the same autosome are linked: they tend to be inherited together, because they move together into gametes. A dihybrid cross then does not give 9:3:3:1. Parental combinations appear more often than expected. New (recombinant) combinations appear only when crossing over separates the alleles; the further apart the genes are on the chromosome, the more often crossing over separates them.

*Recombination frequency* = number of recombinant offspring ÷ total offspring.

**Epistasis.** The allele of one gene affects the expression of another gene. Often one gene controls an enzyme early in a pathway:

- *Recessive epistasis:* homozygous recessive at gene A (aa) masks gene B. In some mammals, aa means no pigment at all (white), whatever the colour alleles at B. AaBb × AaBb gives 9 : 3 : 4.
- *Dominant epistasis:* a dominant allele at gene A masks gene B. AaBb × AaBb gives 12 : 3 : 1 (or 13 : 3).

**Polygenic inheritance** (Edexcel A, OCR A). Many genes, each with a small additive effect, affect one characteristic (height, skin colour). With the environment, this gives continuous variation and a normal distribution. Discontinuous variation is usually one gene with distinct phenotypes.

**Environment** (OCR A). Phenotype = genotype + environment. Plants grown in the dark are tall, thin and yellow (etiolation); plants short of magnesium are yellow (chlorosis), even if their genes for chlorophyll are normal.

**Pedigree diagrams** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B). Squares are males, circles females; shaded symbols are affected. If two unaffected parents have an affected child, the condition is recessive and both parents are carriers. If an affected child always has an affected parent, it is likely dominant.

**Model organisms** (OCR B). Fruit flies (*Drosophila*), mice, peas and *Arabidopsis* are used to study inheritance because they breed quickly, produce many offspring, are cheap and easy to keep, have visible variation, and their genomes are known. Results often apply to humans because genes and processes are shared.

**The chi-squared test.** Tests whether the difference between observed (O) and expected (E) results is due to chance.

χ² = Σ (O − E)² ÷ E

1. Null hypothesis: there is no significant difference between observed and expected results.
2. Calculate expected numbers from the ratio and the total.
3. Calculate χ².
4. Degrees of freedom = number of categories − 1.
5. Compare with the critical value at p = 0.05.
6. If χ² is less than the critical value, accept the null hypothesis: any difference is due to chance. If greater, reject it: the difference is significant (perhaps the genes are linked, or there is epistasis).

#### Worked example

A dihybrid cross in peas gave: round yellow 296, round green 104, wrinkled yellow 92, wrinkled green 28. Total = 520. Expected ratio 9:3:3:1.

1. Expected: 520 × 9/16 = 292.5; 520 × 3/16 = 97.5; 97.5; 520 × 1/16 = 32.5.
2. (O − E)²/E:
   - (296 − 292.5)² ÷ 292.5 = 12.25 ÷ 292.5 = 0.042
   - (104 − 97.5)² ÷ 97.5 = 42.25 ÷ 97.5 = 0.433
   - (92 − 97.5)² ÷ 97.5 = 30.25 ÷ 97.5 = 0.310
   - (28 − 32.5)² ÷ 32.5 = 20.25 ÷ 32.5 = 0.623
3. χ² = 1.41.
4. Degrees of freedom = 3. Critical value at p = 0.05 is 7.82.
5. 1.41 < 7.82, so accept the null hypothesis. The results fit a 9:3:3:1 ratio; the difference is due to chance.

Sex linkage example: a colour-blind man (X^b Y) and a woman with normal vision whose father was colour-blind (X^B X^b). Probability that their next child is a colour-blind son: gametes X^B, X^b from mother; X^b, Y from father. Children: X^B X^b, X^B Y, X^b X^b, X^b Y. Colour-blind son = X^b Y = 1/4.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing gametes with two alleles of the same gene (Aa) or omitting the circle convention.
- Writing sex-linked alleles without the X (just "h") or putting alleles on the Y.
- Saying a 3:1 ratio means "exactly 3 out of every 4". It is a probability.
- Concluding the null hypothesis is "proved". It is accepted or rejected.
- Using the wrong degrees of freedom (number of categories − 1, not number of individuals).
- Saying linked genes "are on different chromosomes".

### Check questions

1. Define the terms genotype and codominant. (2 marks)
2. In cats, the allele for orange fur (X^O) and the allele for black fur (X^o) are codominant and X-linked; heterozygous females are tortoiseshell. Explain why male tortoiseshell cats are very rare. (2 marks)
3. Two parents with blood groups A and B have a child with group O. Give the parents' genotypes and the probability that their next child is group AB. (3 marks)
4. A dihybrid cross between two heterozygotes gave far more offspring with the parental combinations than expected. Suggest and explain why. (3 marks)
5. In a species, gene C controls pigment production (cc = no pigment, white) and gene B gives black (B) or brown (bb). Predict the phenotype ratio from CcBb × CcBb. (3 marks)
6. A monohybrid cross gave 72 dominant and 32 recessive. Use chi-squared to test whether this fits 3:1 (critical value 3.84 at 1 degree of freedom, p = 0.05). (4 marks)
7. Explain why a father cannot pass haemophilia to his son. (2 marks)
8. Give three features that make *Drosophila* a useful model organism in genetics. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Genotype: alleles an organism has / genetic constitution (1); codominant: both alleles expressed in the heterozygote (1).
2. Tortoiseshell needs both X^O and X^o / two X chromosomes (1); males have only one X / XY (1).
3. I^A I^O and I^B I^O (1); genetic diagram / four outcomes (1); probability of AB = 1/4 (1).
4. Genes linked / on the same chromosome (1); alleles inherited together (1); recombinants only from crossing over, which is infrequent (1).
5. Diagram or reasoning (1); 9 black (C_B_) : 3 brown (C_bb) (1) : 4 white (cc__) (1).
6. Total 104; expected 78 and 26 (1); χ² = (72 − 78)²/78 + (32 − 26)²/26 = 0.46 + 1.38 = 1.85 (1); less than 3.84 (1); accept null hypothesis: difference due to chance, fits 3:1 (1).
7. Haemophilia allele on X chromosome (1); father gives son a Y chromosome (1).
8. Any three: short life cycle / breed quickly; many offspring; cheap / easy to keep; visible mutations / variation; small genome / sequenced; few chromosomes.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/22-populations-evolution-and-speciation.md -->

## Populations, evolution and speciation

Evolution is a change in allele frequencies in a population over time. This file covers gene pools, the Hardy–Weinberg principle, the forces that change allele frequencies, and how new species form.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Natural selection and types of selection are introduced in file 13. Classification is in file 14.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.2, 3.7.3. Edexcel A 4.4, 4.5, 5.17, 5.19. Edexcel B 3.2 i, iii, 8.3. OCR A 4.2.2(e), 6.1.2(e) to (h). OCR B 3.1.3(e), 5.1.2(a) to (e). Eduqas C2.6(c) to (k).
- OCR A asks for evidence for evolution (fossils, DNA, molecular evidence) and for artificial selection (4.2.2(e), 6.1.2(h)).
- OCR B asks how changes in amino acid sequences give evidence of evolution (5.1.2(b)).
- Eduqas asks for intra- and inter-specific competition and selective agencies (C2.6(c), (d)) and for Darwin's theory by name.
- Edexcel A asks how the scientific community validates evidence about evolution (5.18, file 42).

### 3.7.2 Populations

*Refs: AQA 3.7.2 · Edexcel A 4.5 · Edexcel B 8.3 iv · OCR A 6.1.2(f) · OCR B 5.1.2(c) · Eduqas C2.6(e), (g), (h). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A).*

#### What you need to know

- Species exist as one or more populations.
- A population as a group of organisms of the same species occupying a particular space at a particular time that can potentially interbreed.
- The concepts of gene pool and allele frequency.
- The Hardy–Weinberg principle provides a mathematical model, which predicts that allele frequencies will not change from generation to generation. The conditions under which the principle applies.
- Calculate allele, genotype and phenotype frequencies from appropriate data and from the Hardy–Weinberg equation, p² + 2pq + q² = 1, where p is the frequency of one (dominant) allele of the gene and q is the frequency of the other (recessive) allele of the gene.
- Understand and use the expression p + q = 1.

#### Explained

**Gene pool:** all the alleles of all the genes in a population at a given time. **Allele frequency:** the proportion of all copies of a gene that are one particular allele.

**Hardy–Weinberg.** For a gene with two alleles:

- p + q = 1 (allele frequencies)
- p² + 2pq + q² = 1 (genotype frequencies): p² = homozygous dominant, 2pq = heterozygous, q² = homozygous recessive.

The principle predicts allele frequencies stay the same from one generation to the next, if:

- the population is large
- mating is random
- there is no selection (all genotypes equally likely to survive and reproduce)
- there is no mutation
- there is no migration into or out of the population (no gene flow).

In reality these are rarely all true. If observed frequencies change over generations, or differ from predictions, one of the conditions has been broken, such as selection acting. That is how the equation is used (Edexcel B: to monitor change).

The usual starting point is q², because the homozygous recessive phenotype is the only one whose genotype you can identify by looking.

#### Worked example

1 in 2500 babies in a population is born with a recessive condition. Calculate the frequency of carriers.

1. q² = 1/2500 = 0.0004.
2. q = √0.0004 = 0.02.
3. p = 1 − 0.02 = 0.98.
4. Carriers = 2pq = 2 × 0.98 × 0.02 = 0.0392, about 1 in 26 people (3.9%).

From genotype counts: in 200 snails, 98 are AA, 84 Aa, 18 aa. Allele A count = (2 × 98) + 84 = 280 out of 400 alleles; p = 0.7, q = 0.3. Expected under Hardy–Weinberg: AA = 0.49 × 200 = 98; Aa = 0.42 × 200 = 84; aa = 0.09 × 200 = 18. The population fits.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking the square root of the dominant phenotype frequency to get p. The dominant phenotype is p² + 2pq.
- Forgetting the 2 in 2pq.
- Saying Hardy–Weinberg "proves" that a population is not evolving.

### 3.7.3 Evolution may lead to speciation

*Refs: AQA 3.7.3 · Edexcel A 4.4, 5.17, 5.19 · Edexcel B 3.2 i, iii, 8.3 i to iii · OCR A 4.2.2(e), 6.1.2(e), (g), (h) · OCR B 3.1.3(e), 5.1.2(a), (b), (d), (e) · Eduqas C2.6(c), (d), (f), (i) to (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Individuals within a population of a species may show a wide range of variation in phenotype. This is due to genetic factors and, to some extent, the environment.
- The primary source of genetic variation is mutation. Meiosis and the random fertilisation of gametes during sexual reproduction produce further genetic variation.
- Predation, disease and competition for the means of survival result in differential survival and reproduction, ie natural selection.
- Those organisms with phenotypes providing selective advantages are likely to produce more offspring and pass on their favourable alleles to the next generation. The effect of this differential reproductive success on the allele frequencies within a gene pool.
- The effects of stabilising, directional and disruptive selection.
- Evolution as a change in the allele frequencies in a population.
- Reproductive separation of two populations can result in accumulation of differences in their gene pools. New species arise when these genetic differences lead to an inability of members of the populations to interbreed and produce fertile offspring. In this way, new species arise from existing species.
- Allopatric and sympatric speciation.
- The importance of genetic drift in causing changes in allele frequency in small populations.
- Explain why individuals within a population of a species may show a wide range of variation in phenotype.

#### Explained

**Sources of variation.** Mutation creates new alleles. Meiosis (independent segregation, crossing over) and random fertilisation reshuffle them. The environment adds non-heritable variation.

**Selection pressures.** Predation, disease and competition for food, mates and territory mean more offspring are produced than can survive. Individuals with advantageous phenotypes are more likely to survive and reproduce (differential reproductive success), so their alleles become more frequent in the gene pool.

- *Intraspecific competition* (Eduqas): between members of the same species, for food, territory and mates; it drives selection within a species.
- *Interspecific competition:* between different species for the same resources.

**Three types of selection.**

| Type | Favoured | Effect on distribution | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Directional | One extreme | Mean shifts | Antibiotic resistance; longer necks; darker moths in polluted areas |
| Stabilising | The mean | Range narrows; mean the same | Human birth weight; clutch size in birds |
| Disruptive | Both extremes | Two peaks; can lead to speciation | Beak sizes in a finch population where only large and small seeds are available |

**Genetic drift.** Random changes in allele frequency from one generation to the next, because which individuals happen to reproduce is partly chance. In small populations chance has a much bigger effect: an allele can be lost or fixed even if it gives no advantage. Drift reduces genetic diversity.

- *Founder effect* (Edexcel B, OCR A, OCR B): a few individuals start a new population; their allele frequencies are by chance unlike those of the original population (for example a high frequency of a rare disease allele in an isolated community).
- *Genetic bottleneck:* a population is greatly reduced (by disaster or hunting); the survivors carry only a fraction of the original alleles. Northern elephant seals and cheetahs have very low genetic diversity after bottlenecks.
- *Gene flow* (migration) brings alleles in or takes them out.

**Speciation.**

1. Two populations become reproductively isolated, so no gene flow between them.
2. Different mutations arise in each.
3. Different selection pressures act (different environments), and drift acts differently.
4. Allele frequencies in the two gene pools change differently.
5. Eventually the populations can no longer interbreed to produce fertile offspring: they are separate species.

*Allopatric speciation:* populations are separated geographically (by a mountain range, a river, the sea). Darwin's finches on the Galápagos Islands are an example.

*Sympatric speciation:* populations live in the same area but become reproductively isolated because of:

- mutations that change courtship behaviour, flowering time or the shape of genitalia
- a mutation that causes polyploidy in plants (offspring cannot breed with the parent population)
- different feeding preferences, so groups mate in different places (apple maggot flies).

**Isolating mechanisms** (OCR A, Eduqas).

- *Prezygotic* (stop fertilisation): geographical; ecological (different habitats); temporal (breeding at different times); behavioural (different courtship); mechanical (genitalia or flowers incompatible); gametic (gametes cannot fuse).
- *Postzygotic* (after fertilisation): hybrid inviability (embryo dies); hybrid sterility (mule); hybrid breakdown (second generation infertile).

**Evidence for evolution** (OCR A, OCR B, Eduqas).

- *Fossils:* show change over time; simpler organisms in older rock layers; transitional forms. Limits: few organisms fossilise; the record has gaps.
- *Comparative anatomy:* homologous structures, such as the pentadactyl limb, show common ancestry.
- *Molecular evidence:* DNA base sequences and protein amino acid sequences (cytochrome c, haemoglobin) are more similar in closely related species. The number of differences increases with time since the species diverged, so differences act as a "molecular clock" (OCR B 5.1.2(b)).
- *Darwin and Wallace* (Eduqas C2.6(k)): observed variation, over-production of offspring, struggle for existence, survival of the fittest, inheritance of advantageous characteristics; the theory explains the origin of species by natural selection.

**Artificial selection** (OCR A 6.1.2(h)). Humans choose individuals with desired characteristics to breed, over many generations: high-yield wheat, dairy cattle with high milk yield, dogs. It reduces genetic diversity, can cause health problems from inbreeding (breathing problems in flat-faced dog breeds), and makes populations vulnerable to new diseases. Gene banks of wild types and old varieties keep a resource of alleles for future breeding.

#### Worked example

Before an antibiotic was used, 2% of a bacterial population carried a resistance allele. After a course of treatment, 60% did. Explain the change.

The antibiotic was a selection pressure. Bacteria without the allele were killed; those with it survived and reproduced. Their offspring inherited the allele, so its frequency rose. This is directional selection; it took place over many generations because bacteria reproduce quickly.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying mutations happen "in order to" help an organism survive.
- Saying "the population adapts by mutating".
- Saying geographical isolation alone makes a new species. The populations must also change genetically until they can no longer interbreed.
- Saying genetic drift is the same as natural selection. Drift is random; selection is not.
- Saying sympatric speciation needs a physical barrier.

### Check questions

1. Define the terms gene pool and allele frequency. (2 marks)
2. Give three conditions needed for the Hardy–Weinberg principle to apply. (3 marks)
3. In a population, 16% of individuals show a recessive phenotype. Calculate the percentage that are heterozygous. (3 marks)
4. Explain how allopatric speciation happens. (5 marks)
5. Describe how sympatric speciation could occur in a population of insects. (3 marks)
6. Explain why genetic drift has a greater effect in small populations. (2 marks)
7. Describe disruptive selection and explain how it could lead to speciation. (3 marks)
8. Explain why a crop variety produced by artificial selection may be more vulnerable to a new disease than its wild relative. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Gene pool: all the alleles of all genes in a population (1); allele frequency: proportion of an allele of all alleles of that gene in a population (1).
2. Any three: large population; random mating; no selection; no mutation; no migration / gene flow.
3. q² = 0.16, q = 0.4 (1); p = 0.6 (1); 2pq = 0.48, so 48% (1).
4. Populations geographically separated (1); no gene flow / reproductive isolation (1); different mutations (1); different selection pressures change allele frequencies differently (1); eventually cannot interbreed to produce fertile offspring (1).
5. Mutation changes courtship / mating time / food preference (1); some individuals become reproductively isolated from others in the same area (1); gene pools diverge until new species form (1).
6. Chance events affect allele frequencies more when there are few individuals (1); an allele can be lost or fixed regardless of advantage (1).
7. Both extremes favoured, intermediates selected against (1); two distinct groups form (1); if they stop interbreeding, gene pools diverge, leading to new species (1).
8. Artificial selection reduces genetic diversity (1); fewer alleles, so less chance that some individuals have resistance (1); whole crop may be susceptible (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/23-populations-in-ecosystems.md -->

## Populations in ecosystems, succession and conservation

Populations of different species live together in ecosystems, and their sizes are set by biotic and abiotic factors. This file covers carrying capacity, competition and predation, estimating population size, succession, and conservation, with AQA required practical 12.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Energy flow and nutrient cycles are in file 17. Biodiversity indices are in file 14. Human impact, conservation agreements and sustainable management (OCR A 6.3.2(d), (e), Edexcel B 10.4, Eduqas C1.6) are in file 42. Statistical tests on field data are in file 26.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.4, RP12. Edexcel A 4.3, 4.16, 5.1 to 5.4, CP10. Edexcel B 3.3 ii, iii, 10.1 i, iv, v, 10.3, CP15, CP16. OCR A 4.2.1(b), (h), 6.3.1(a), (d), (e), 6.3.2(a) to (c). Eduqas C1.5(a) to (e), (g), (i), (j), C1.6(b).
- Eduqas asks for population growth curves and density-dependent and density-independent factors (C1.5(a) to (c)).
- OCR A asks for the difference between conservation and preservation (6.3.2(c)) and predator–prey cycles (6.3.2(b)).
- Edexcel B CP15 compares sampling methods for estimating population size; Edexcel B CP16, Edexcel A CP10 and OCR A PAG3 are field studies like AQA RP12.
- Edexcel A, Edexcel B and OCR A ask for in situ and ex situ conservation (zoos, seed banks).

### 3.7.4 Populations in ecosystems

*Refs: AQA 3.7.4, RP12 · Edexcel A 4.3, 4.16, 5.1 to 5.4, CP10 · Edexcel B 3.3 ii, iii, 10.1 i, iv, v, 10.3 i, ii, CP15, CP16 · OCR A 4.2.1(b), (h), 6.3.1(a), (d), (e), 6.3.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 4.3.1(m) · Eduqas C1.5(a) to (e), (g), (i), (j), C1.6(b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Populations of different species form a community. A community and the non-living components of its environment together form an ecosystem. Ecosystems can range in size from the very small to the very large.
- Within a habitat, a species occupies a niche governed by adaptation to both biotic and abiotic conditions.
- An ecosystem supports a certain size of population of a species, called the carrying capacity. This population size can vary as a result of: the effect of abiotic factors; interactions between organisms: interspecific and intraspecific competition and predation.
- The size of a population can be estimated using: randomly placed quadrats, or quadrats along a belt transect, for slow-moving or non-motile organisms; the mark-release-recapture method for motile organisms. The assumptions made when using the mark-release-recapture method.
- Ecosystems are dynamic and are subject to change over time. The concept of succession from pioneer species to climax community. At each stage in succession, certain species may be recognised that change the environment so that it becomes more suitable for other species with different adaptations. The new species may change the environment in such a way that it becomes less suitable for the previous species. Changes that organisms produce in their abiotic environment can result in a less hostile environment and change biodiversity.
- Conservation of habitats frequently involves management of succession.
- Evaluate evidence and data concerning issues relating to the conservation of species and habitats and consider conflicting evidence. Use given data to calculate the size of a population estimated using the mark-release-recapture method.

#### Explained

**Terms.**

- *Population:* all the organisms of one species in an area at one time.
- *Community:* all the populations of different species in an area.
- *Ecosystem:* a community plus the abiotic (non-living) parts of its environment.
- *Habitat:* the place where an organism lives.
- *Niche:* the role of a species in its habitat, including its interactions with other species and the abiotic conditions it needs. Two species cannot occupy the same niche in the same habitat indefinitely.

**Carrying capacity.** The largest population an environment can support long term. A population introduced to a new area grows slowly at first (lag phase: few individuals; adjusting), then rapidly (log or exponential phase: plenty of resources), then levels off (stationary phase) as resources, predators or disease limit it around the carrying capacity. (Eduqas, OCR A.)

**Abiotic factors:** temperature, light, pH, water availability, mineral ions, oxygen, wind, soil type. Each species has an optimum range.

**Biotic factors.**

- *Intraspecific competition:* within a species. When resources are short, the population falls; with less competition it rises again; numbers fluctuate around the carrying capacity.
- *Interspecific competition:* between species for the same resource. If niches overlap, one species may outcompete the other (red squirrels replaced by grey squirrels in most of the UK).
- *Predation:* predator and prey populations cycle. Prey increase → more food for predators, so predators increase → prey eaten, so prey decrease → predators starve, so predators decrease → prey recover. Predator peaks lag behind prey peaks. Real cycles are also affected by food supply and disease.

**Density-dependent and density-independent factors** (Eduqas). Density-dependent factors have a bigger effect as the population gets denser: competition, predation, disease, parasitism. Density-independent factors affect a population whatever its density: floods, fire, extreme cold.

**Estimating population size.**

*Quadrats* (for plants and slow-moving animals):

1. Use random coordinates (two tape measures at right angles; random number generator) to place quadrats.
2. Record abundance in each: count individuals (density), estimate percentage cover, or record presence in a point frame or gridded quadrat (frequency).
3. Use enough quadrats that the running mean levels off.
4. Population estimate = mean number per quadrat × (area of habitat ÷ area of one quadrat).

*Transects* (when conditions change across an area, such as from a hedge into a field, or down a seashore):

- Line transect: record species touching the line at regular intervals.
- Belt transect: place quadrats at regular intervals along the line (continuous or interrupted).

*Mark-release-recapture* (for mobile animals):

1. Capture a sample, count them (M), mark them harmlessly (paint dot under the shell), release.
2. After time to mix back in, capture a second sample; count the total (C) and how many are marked (R).
3. Population estimate N = (M × C) ÷ R.

Assumptions: marked individuals mix fully back into the population; the mark does not affect survival (not more visible to predators) and does not rub off; no births, deaths, immigration or emigration between samples; the population is in a defined area.

**Sampling methods and techniques** (Edexcel B 10.1 iv, v, CP15; OCR A 4.2.1(b)). Choose the method for the organism and habitat: kick sampling and nets for freshwater invertebrates; pitfall traps for ground insects; pooters; sweep nets; Tullgren funnels for soil invertebrates; light traps for moths. Measure abiotic factors with light meters, thermometers, pH probes, oxygen probes. Random sampling avoids bias; systematic sampling along a transect shows patterns; stratified sampling covers each part of a habitat in proportion. More samples give a more representative estimate.

**Succession.** The change in a community over time.

*Primary succession* starts on bare ground with no soil (bare rock, sand dunes, cooled lava):

1. **Pioneer species** colonise: lichens, mosses, algae. Adaptations: spores or seeds spread by wind, tolerance of extreme conditions, ability to fix nitrogen, rapid germination, photosynthesis.
2. Pioneers break down rock and, when they die and decompose, form a thin soil with some organic matter (humus).
3. Grasses and small plants can now grow. The soil deepens, holds more water and more nutrients.
4. Shrubs, then trees, colonise. Each stage changes the abiotic environment (more shade, deeper soil, more shelter) in ways that suit new species and may make it less suitable for the previous ones, which are outcompeted.
5. A stable **climax community** forms (in the UK, usually deciduous woodland, such as oak).

As succession proceeds: biomass increases; the number of species (biodiversity) usually increases; conditions become less hostile; food webs become more complex.

*Secondary succession* starts where soil is already present but vegetation has been removed (after fire, ploughing or felling). It is faster.

*Deflected succession / plagioclimax:* human activity (grazing, mowing, burning) stops succession at an earlier stage. Heather moorland is kept by controlled burning; chalk grassland by sheep grazing.

**Conservation.**

- *Conservation* means active management to maintain or increase biodiversity, and allows sustainable human use. *Preservation* (OCR A) means protecting an area from any human interference.
- *Managing succession:* conserving a habitat at an early stage (heathland, meadow) means preventing the climax: grazing, mowing, cutting back scrub, controlled burning.
- *In situ conservation:* protecting species in their natural habitat — national parks, nature reserves, marine conservation zones, controlling invasive species, legal protection.
- *Ex situ conservation:* outside the natural habitat — zoos with captive breeding programmes (keeping studbooks to avoid inbreeding, reintroducing animals to the wild), botanic gardens and seed banks (seeds dried and frozen; tested regularly for viability; such as the Millennium Seed Bank). Advantages: protects species from immediate threats; seeds store many alleles in little space. Disadvantages: small captive populations lose genetic diversity; animals may lose natural behaviours; reintroduction fails if the habitat is still damaged; expensive.

**Conflicting evidence.** Conservation decisions balance biological evidence with economic and social needs. When evaluating data, consider sample size, how the data were collected, whether a correlation shows cause, and who funded or published the study.

#### Worked example

1. In a first sample, 40 woodlice were caught and marked. In the second sample, 50 were caught, 8 of them marked. Estimate the population.

N = (40 × 50) ÷ 8 = 250.

2. Ten 0.25 m² quadrats in a 200 m² meadow contained a total of 36 daisies. Estimate the number of daisies in the meadow.

Mean per quadrat = 3.6; per m² = 3.6 ÷ 0.25 = 14.4; total = 14.4 × 200 = 2880.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the climax community "is reached when no more species can live there".
- Saying pioneers "make soil" without saying they break down rock and decompose to add organic matter.
- Mixing up population, community and ecosystem.
- In mark-release-recapture, assuming marking "has no effect" without saying what effects it must not have.
- Placing quadrats where there is "the most" of a plant.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 12** (also Edexcel A CP10, Edexcel B CP16, OCR A PAG3 and 6.3.1(e)): investigation into the effect of a named environmental factor on the distribution of a given species.

Example: the effect of light intensity on the distribution of ivy from the edge of a wood into its interior.

1. Lay a tape measure from the open field into the wood (a belt transect).
2. Place a quadrat at regular intervals (every 2 m).
3. In each, estimate percentage cover of ivy and measure light intensity with a light meter at the same height and time of day.
4. Repeat with several transects.
5. Plot percentage cover against light intensity and use Spearman's rank correlation coefficient to test for a correlation (file 26).

Control variables: same time of day; same weather; same person estimating cover. Risks: uneven ground, stinging plants, ticks; work in pairs; wash hands. Limitations: other factors (soil moisture, pH) also vary along the transect, so a correlation does not show light is the cause.

### Check questions

1. Define the terms niche and carrying capacity. (2 marks)
2. Explain why the populations of a predator and its prey rise and fall in cycles. (4 marks)
3. Describe how a student could estimate the number of dandelions on a school field. (4 marks)
4. In a mark-release-recapture study, 60 beetles were marked; a second sample of 45 contained 9 marked beetles. Estimate the population and give two assumptions. (4 marks)
5. Describe and explain the changes during primary succession on bare rock. (5 marks)
6. Explain why conserving heathland requires management. (3 marks)
7. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of using seed banks for conservation. (2 marks)
8. A student found a positive correlation between soil moisture and the cover of a moss. Explain why this does not show that soil moisture causes greater moss cover. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Niche: role / position of a species in its habitat, including biotic and abiotic interactions (1); carrying capacity: maximum population size an ecosystem can support (long term) (1).
2. More prey means more food so predator numbers rise (1); more predators eat more prey, prey decline (1); less food, predators decline (1); fewer predators, prey recover; predator peak lags behind prey (1).
3. Random coordinates / random number generator (1); place many quadrats and count dandelions in each (1); calculate mean per quadrat (1); multiply by field area ÷ quadrat area (1).
4. (60 × 45) ÷ 9 = 300 (2); any two assumptions (1 each): marks do not affect survival / do not rub off; marked mix fully; no births / deaths / migration between samples.
5. Pioneer species (lichens) colonise (1); break down rock / die and decompose, forming thin soil (1); allows grasses / mosses to grow, soil deepens, more nutrients and water (1); each stage changes environment, making it suitable for new species that outcompete earlier ones (1); shrubs then trees; climax community; biodiversity and biomass increase (1).
6. Heathland is an early stage of succession (1); without management it would become scrub / woodland (1); grazing / burning / cutting prevents succession (1).
7. Advantage (1): stores many alleles / many species in little space / cheap / protects against extinction in the wild. Disadvantage (1): seeds may lose viability; plants not adapting to changing conditions in the wild; samples may not represent full genetic diversity.
8. Correlation does not show cause (1); another factor, such as light or pH, may vary with moisture and affect moss (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/24-mutation-stem-cells-and-gene-expression.md -->

## Gene mutations, stem cells and the control of gene expression

Every cell has the same genes, but each uses only some of them. This file covers the types and effects of gene mutation, stem cells, how transcription and translation are controlled (transcription factors, epigenetics, RNA interference, the lac operon, homeobox genes), and how faulty control leads to cancer.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Basic mutations are introduced in file 13; chromosome disorders in file 39; cancer screening and treatment in file 44.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.8.1, 3.8.2.1 to 3.8.2.3. Edexcel A 2.12, 3.11, 3.12, 3.14, 6.10, 7.16. Edexcel B 1.4 viii, ix, 7.2, 7.3, 8.1 i. OCR A 2.1.6(j) to (m), 6.1.1(a) to (d). OCR B 3.1.1(c) to (e), 3.3.1(b) to (d), 5.1.1(b), 5.1.2(f), 5.1.3(a), (h). Eduqas C2.5(f) to (h), C2.7(i).
- OCR A alone asks for the lac operon, homeobox genes, and the formation of erythrocytes, neutrophils, xylem and phloem from stem cells (6.1.1(b), (c), 2.1.6(k), (l)). Included below.
- OCR A and OCR B ask for apoptosis.
- OCR B asks for RNA interference (5.1.3(h)); AQA includes RNAi under translation.
- Edexcel A, Edexcel B and Eduqas use sickle cell anaemia as the example of a point mutation.
- Edexcel B and Edexcel A ask for post-transcriptional modification (alternative splicing) giving more than one protein from one gene.

### 3.8.1 Alteration of the sequence of bases in DNA can alter the structure of proteins

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1 · Edexcel A 2.12 · Edexcel B 1.4 viii, ix, 8.1 i · OCR A 6.1.1(a) · OCR B 5.1.1(b) · Eduqas C2.5(f), (g). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A and B).*

#### What you need to know

- Gene mutations might arise during DNA replication. They include addition, deletion, substitution, inversion, duplication and translocation of bases.
- Gene mutations occur spontaneously. The mutation rate is increased by mutagenic agents. Some mutations result in a different amino acid sequence in the encoded polypeptide. Due to the degenerate nature of the genetic code, not all base substitutions cause a change in the sequence of encoded amino acids. Some gene mutations change only one triplet code; others result in a frame shift.
- Relate the nature of a gene mutation to its effect on the encoded polypeptide.

#### Explained

| Mutation | What happens | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Substitution | One base replaced by another | One triplet changes; may be silent (same amino acid), missense (one different amino acid) or nonsense (stop codon, short polypeptide) |
| Addition (insertion) | Extra base added | Frame shift: every triplet after it changes |
| Deletion | Base lost | Frame shift |
| Duplication | One or more bases repeated | Frame shift (unless a whole number of triplets) |
| Inversion | A group of bases reversed | Changes the triplets within that section |
| Translocation of bases | A group of bases moves from one location to another (same or different chromosome) | Can disrupt genes at both locations |

Adding or deleting three bases removes or adds one amino acid without a frame shift.

**Effect on the protein.** A different primary structure may change the bonds that form between R groups, so the tertiary structure changes. An enzyme's active site may no longer be complementary to its substrate. Some mutations are harmful, most are neutral, and a few are beneficial.

**Sickle cell anaemia** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B, Eduqas). A substitution in the gene for the β chain of haemoglobin changes one base on the coding strand from A to T, so the triplet GAG (glutamic acid) becomes GTG (valine); on the mRNA, GAG becomes GUG. Valine is non-polar, so haemoglobin molecules stick together at low oxygen, forming long fibres. Red blood cells become sickle-shaped, carry less oxygen and block capillaries. Heterozygotes are protected against malaria, so the allele is common where malaria is.

**Mutagens.** Ionising radiation (breaks DNA strands); UV light (makes adjacent thymines bond together); chemicals such as benzene, mustard gas, nitrogen oxides and tar in tobacco smoke (alter bases or insert between them); some viruses insert DNA into the genome. A carcinogen is a mutagen that causes cancer (Eduqas C2.5(g)).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying all mutations are harmful.
- Saying a substitution "always changes the protein".
- Saying a frame shift changes "one amino acid".

### 3.8.2.1 Most of a cell's DNA is not translated (stem cells)

*Refs: AQA 3.8.2.1 · Edexcel A 3.11, 3.12 · Edexcel B 7.3 i to v · OCR A 2.1.6(j) to (m) · OCR B 3.1.1(e) · Eduqas C2.7(i). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A, OCR A, OCR B).*

#### What you need to know

- Totipotent cells are cells that can mature into any body cell. During development, totipotent cells translate only part of their DNA, resulting in cell specialisation.
- In mature mammals, only a few totipotent cells, called stem cells, remain. Stem cells are undifferentiated cells that can divide indefinitely and can become specialised.
- Totipotent cells occur only for a limited time in early mammalian embryos. Pluripotent cells are found in embryos; multipotent and unipotent cells are found in mature mammals.
- Unipotent cells, exemplified by the formation of cardiomyocytes.
- Induced pluripotent stem cells (iPS cells) can be produced from adult somatic cells using appropriate protein transcription factors.
- Stem cells can be used in the treatment of human disorders.
- Evaluate the use of stem cells in treating human disorders.

#### Explained

| Type | Can become | Found in |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Totipotent | Any cell, including placenta | Zygote and the first few divisions (morula) |
| Pluripotent | Almost any cell type, but not placenta | Inner cell mass of the blastocyst (embryonic stem cells) |
| Multipotent | A few related types | Adult tissues: bone marrow (all blood cells) |
| Unipotent | One type | Adult tissues: cells in the heart that form cardiomyocytes (heart muscle cells) |

**Specialisation.** All cells have the same genes. As cells differentiate, some genes are switched on and others off, so only some proteins are made (differential gene expression, Edexcel A 3.12). Once specialised, most mammalian cells cannot go back.

**Examples of differentiation** (OCR A). Multipotent stem cells in bone marrow produce erythrocytes (lose the nucleus and most organelles, fill with haemoglobin; live about 120 days) and neutrophils (lobed nucleus, many lysosomes; live about 6 hours).
